=== Strategy: An Introduction ===


--- The role of Strategy and what makes a Strategy successful ---

A simple definition of the term strategy indicates these are ways companies achieve their goals.
The word had been originally used in the military and more recently adapted for business.
It descends from the Greek expression, strategic meaning, generalship or commanding an army.
Both armies and companies need strategies to use their resources in the most effective way and to establish
a favourable position.
Over the past five decades, the business environment has changed dramatically and continues to evolve
at a really fast pace.
It is also less predictable than ever before, which makes analytical reasoning and strategic positioning
even more important today.
Most strategies applied by companies that are successful have four elements in common.
One.
They are based on goals that are simple, consistent and long term to the goals the company wants to
achieve have been formulated after a deep analysis of the competitive environment.
Three.
They objectively consider a company's resources and exploit them in an effective manner.
Four.
And finally, the people responsible for achieving these goals are strong willed and have solid decision
making capabilities.
Michael Porter, the American professor at Harvard Business School, who has influenced the business
world more than any other academic and revolutionized the concept of strategy, says strategy is about
making choices.
It's about deliberately choosing to be different.
That's quite straightforward, isn't it?
And yet so difficult to achieve.
Thousands of companies compete for a place under the sun, but only few come up with original ideas
and are different.
Zara, the world's largest clothing retailer, is a typical example of how different strategies can
lead to success.
Founded in 1975, in Spain, the successful retailer now runs over 6500 stores in 88 countries.
Its founder, Amancio Ortega, is the third richest man in the world, according to Forbes.
So what makes the company's strategy so successful?
Zara changed the fashion industry by breaking up the biannual cycle of fashion.
Its designers need only three weeks to have an item in stores, starting from concept to reaching the
store shelves, while other fashion retailers prepare their collections six months in advance.
Zara locks approximately 50% of its stock by the start of the season.
In this way, if a new fashion trend appears, the company can immediately react and quickly produce
what customers really want and look for.
If there is no demand for a certain item.
Zara can simply discontinue production low levels of stock and a frequently updated offering reduce
the company's risk.
This makes customers want to visit Zara shops frequently, given that the collections are changed relatively
quickly.
Another key element in the company's strategy is that Zara produces its clothing mainly in Spain and
in Europe, where we can find the larger portion of its retail stores.
This enables shorter lead times and faster replenishing of stock, with turnaround as short as two weeks.
Higher production costs are offset by the reduced amount of money the company spends on advertising.
This is a strategy model that is original, hadn't been implemented before and a true success story.
Things aren't always as rosy, though.
Sometimes companies make wrong strategic choices and struggle to adapt to a changing environment.
Kodak is a well-known example of a company that could not adapt to the changing business circumstances.
In the late 19th century, the American company invented the roll film, which easily replaced the old
photographic plates.
The company was perceived as an emblem of industrial innovation in the US.
Kodak was a leader in a market it had pioneered.
Sounds great, right?
For many years, Kodak dominated the photography market and was the leading company in the industry.
However, about 20 years ago, film photography started to decline and Kodak made a losing bet.
The company did not capture the potential of digital photography on time and instead focused on investing
capital in new technologies for taking pictures with mobile phones.
This did not work for them because it meant running away from their niche and missing a fantastic opportunity.
In retrospect, we can definitely say Kodak did not invest in developing digital cameras as much as
it should have, but other companies did.
Japanese companies, such as Canon, renowned for their market innovations, quickly embraced digital
photography.
Kodak failed to recognise the competitive environment was changing and underestimated the importance
of digital photography.
Such strategic mistakes are more than costly.
The company filed for bankruptcy in 2012.
The two stories we saw here are good examples of the importance of strategy, and it's no coincidence
the strategy module is the first one in this course.
These are some of the most critical decisions a company must make.
They are as important as having the right direction is for a ship in the ocean.
If you don't sail in the right direction, you probably will not get where you want to be.
It's the same with companies.
Excellent strategic decisions.
Get you where you want to be.


=== Strategy: The industry life cycle model ===


--- The difference between Corporate and Business Strategy ---

In the previous lesson, we got acquainted with the term strategy and the main characteristics of a
successful strategy.
Here we will make a distinction between corporate and business strategy as there is a subtle difference.
Corporate strategy deals with questions related to markets and industries in which the company competes.
It answers the question where do we want to compete?
Business strategy is about how do we want to compete?
Usually, a company's top management team, CEO and board of directors would be the one that decides
where the firm will compete.
They handle its corporate strategy.
Top managers decide on major issues like vertical integration, diversification, mergers and acquisitions,
capital intensive investments and allocation of resources between the different units of a company.
They approve new product and service lines, which could be added to the company's existing product
offering.
These are the big time decisions shaping the future and a part of a firm's corporate strategy.
Business strategy is about how a company competes within the chosen markets and industries.
Gaining a competitive advantage over other firms makes a business successful.
That is why this strategy is also called a competitive strategy.
Depending on a company's organizational structure, business strategy will be implemented by business
unit managers or division heads.
Let's use a practical example to illustrate these concepts.
A quick glance at Amazon would allow us to do that.
In terms of corporate strategy, Amazon is the world's largest retailer operating online.
Amazon started as an online bookstore, but the company diversified its business substantially, and
today it sells almost anything that can be sold online.
And in terms of geographical presence, Amazon is continuously expanding the scale of its operations,
and it's a high class worldwide competitor.
What about its business strategy?
How does Amazon compete?
The world's largest online retailer follows a cost leadership strategy with a relentless focus on cost
reduction and limited product differentiation.
Besides this, Amazon has a strong focus on technology, the benefits from economies of scale, and
leveraging efficiencies.
The company uses big data analytics to map consumer behavior.
This facilitates predictive modeling and indicates what kind of products would interest the company's
customers.
Once the list of products of interest for a specific customer has been identified, Amazon makes a list
of recommendations tailored to your individual preferences.
Clever, right?
If some of these terms sound unfamiliar, don't worry, you can use the course glossary to learn about
them.
Moreover, we will use them several times over the next lessons.
You can download the course, glossary and other useful files from the Course Resources section.
It is very easy to do that.
Okay, having said all that, let's summarize a few key outtakes.
Corporate strategy addresses the entire scope of a company and answers the following questions.
One which industry or industries should the company compete in?
Two what would be the geographical scope of operations?
National.
International.
Global?
Three should we diversify our business and if yes, at what level?
Four how should our company be organized in terms of product development, marketing, production,
sales, customer service, and distribution?
Which of these have to be in-house and which are going to be outsourced?
Five should the company enter alliances or make acquisitions?
When business strategy is concerned with a single area of the business and answers the following questions
one.
What type of resources and capabilities should our company develop to gain a competitive advantage over
competitors?
Two what is an efficient way to manage different business units to achieve strategic synergies?
Three how should the company monitor the industry environment so its strategies conform to the market
needs?
These are some very interesting topics aren't they?
And we are just getting started.


=== Strategy: Porter's Five Forces model - The competitive dynamics in an industry ===


--- The importance of the Mission, Vision, Goals, and Values statements ---

In our previous lesson, we learned about the two dimensions of a company's strategy, corporate and
business strategy.
They define how a firm competes in the present.
However, strategy is not just about competing for today.
It is also about competing for tomorrow.
When we talk about the future, strategy becomes a dynamic concept which allows top managers to define
goals and objectives for the future and outline how these goals and objectives will be achieved.
A great way to formalize a company's purposes, goals and values is to have formal mission and vision
statements.
Both are related to the main reason a company exists and help stakeholders understand and keep that
reason in mind.
Okay.
So the mission statement addresses the question why do we exist?
Or What is the fundamental reason for our organization's existence?
And the vision statement describes the desired future position of the company and answers the questions
What do we want to achieve in the future and who do we want to become?
When trying to define the vision statement, we should include specific goals and objectives.
Goals help companies define what they want to achieve.
While objectives are the specific actions and measures for attaining these goals by setting goals,
managers commit themselves to a concrete course of action that would allow them to accomplish the company's
vision.
Goals can target different areas like growth, profitability, retention, efficiency or customer service.
Usually goals are expressed as a percentage.
When considering financials, a company's goal could be to increase revenue and decrease overheads,
which is stated in the following way increase revenue by 10% and decrease overheads by 5% in 2017.
An example of an objective that will help you achieve a 10% increase of sales is acquiring five new
customers every month.
Reducing overheads can be achieved by finding new and less expensive office premises, or maybe outsourcing
activities.
Both are specific objectives that might allow a firm to reduce its overhead by 5%.
Another important question company should answer is How are we going to behave and what do we believe
in?
Maximizing profit is important, but it is also important to uphold certain values and principles.
This is why most organizations have a set of values they stay behind.
The role of this statement is to define the firm's values and place constraints on how the organization
pursues its goals.
This can be important for the talented individuals being recruited by the company.
Usually they are not only interested in large compensation packages, but also want to work for an employer
that is socially responsible and believes in integrity and business ethics.
Right.
Obviously, it is not hard to find a firm's mission, vision and values.
All stakeholders can access these statements easily.
Just open a company's website and click around.
You can locate them in no time.
All right.
It's time for a few practical examples.
Let's look at the way some large corporations have stated their mission, vision and value statements.
Some firms are very specific when defining their mission statements and clearly express why the firm
exists and what kind of services it offers.
To build the Web's most convenient, secure, cost effective payment solution PayPal to accelerate the
advent of sustainable transport by bringing compelling mass market electric cars to market as soon as
possible.
Tesla.
These are the missions of PayPal and Tesla.
Both companies have a mission statement that is laser focused.
A statement that isn't ambiguous is to the point and indicates the company's main purpose.
But that's not always the case.
The mission statements of other companies show that business strategy their organization follows.
The two examples you see here show that Wal-Mart and IKEA follow a cost leadership business strategy.
We save people money so they can live better.
Wal-Mart and IKEA.
Our vision is to create a better, everyday life for the many people.
Our business idea supports this vision by offering a wide range of well-designed, functional home furnishing
products at prices so low that as many people as possible will be able to afford them.
There is a third type of mission statement as well that is polar to what we've seen so far.
Some companies are too vague in their mission statements and do not present the market scope of the
business to be a company that inspires and fulfills your curiosity.
Sony It is our mission to continue to authoritatively provide access to diverse services to stay relevant
in tomorrow's world.
Guess not very clear, right?
The same reasoning applies to selecting the vision statement.
It needs to express what the company wants to achieve.
The best vision statements are concise, clear and inspirational.
Here are examples of clear and memorable vision statements to provide access to the world's information
in one click.
Google to create economic opportunity for every member of the global workforce.
LinkedIn.
And here is an example of a vision statement that is too general and hard to remember.
Our vision serves as the framework for our roadmap and guides every aspect of our business by describing
what we need to accomplish in order to continue achieving sustainable quality growth.
Coca-Cola that covers mission and vision statements.
Now values.
Let's look at the values of Facebook.
These are the guiding principles for the company's employees.
The company values are impactful, dynamic, bold, open and socially responsible behavior.
These are the five values that Facebook and its employees should stand for.
Defining these principles helps the company communicate to its employees what the organization stands
behind and how it intends to do business.
It is interesting to see how different companies understand the world, how they want to behave in it,
and what they want to achieve.
This is what we can learn by reading a firm's mission, vision and values statements.


=== Strategy: Game Theory - Studying the interaction between multiple parties ===


--- The four stages of the industry life cycle model - An introduction ---

In this lesson, I would like to show you the industry life cycle model and its four stages introduction,
growth, maturity and decline.
It should be an interesting one.
So let's get started.
Just like everything else in life, industries have a life cycle too.
Well, most of them do.
Some industries will never face a decline.
Can you imagine the demand for clothing and food products drying up?
I can't.
However, we can say most industries go through four stages in their development.
The introduction phase is characterized by the initial offering of the products or services sold in
this industry.
People are unaware of the existence of these products, and firms try to grow consumer awareness as
much as possible.
In the infancy of most industries, you can always find a technological breakthrough as a solo entrepreneur,
a startup or an established company that came up with a technological innovation that gives birth to
an industry.
A good example of an industry.
In the introduction phase right now is consumer robotics and all products related to artificial intelligence.
Very few people know, and even fewer people have used the products offered in this field.
Eventually, more people will learn about the industry due to the word of mouth effect or to the marketing
efforts of the few companies operating in the industry.
Consumers will learn of the industry and sales will speed up.
When this happens, we would be in the growth phase.
Revenues grow faster, but as more competitors enter the market, companies must invest a lot of resources
to grow the business and reach even more consumers.
This is the phase where companies grow geographically as well.
They may expand to new geographic locations and even operate at a multinational level.
Companies aim to expand their market share and win as many first time buyers as they can.
Car sharing services within urban cities is an industry in its growth phase.
More people have started using these services and are buying subscriptions that allow them to hop into
a car and move from point A to point B and then abandon the car there.
A very exciting and innovative industry.
Sales reach their peak in the maturity phase and then slow down until the industry reaches its decline.
The maturity stage is characterized by a high level of competition, and companies try to do their best
to keep their market share intact.
This is when firms differentiate their products even more and concentrate on specific market niches.
The automobile industry is a good example of a mature industry.
Virtually all consumers know of the industry, the companies operating in it are profitable and the
competition between producers is fierce.
Things are less romantic in the decline period.
Competing industries have surpassed the pace of innovation, of the industry in decline.
Imagine that virtual reality glasses provide a better movie watching experience than cinema theaters
in the future.
This would probably lead to the decline of the cinema industry.
Sad.
But this is how things happen sometimes in life, isn't it?
Okay, in our next lesson, we will talk about the way industries evolve and how they change in each
of the stages they go through.
This will do for now.
Thanks for watching.


=== Strategy: Focusing on the inside of a business ===


--- The strategic importance of the industry life cycle model ---

Okay.
Excellent.
Now that we know more about the typical industry life cycle, we're ready to look at it from a strategic
perspective and consider its implications for the choices made by companies in each of the stages.
First, we should point out that the life cycle duration may vary widely across industries as each follows
its own trajectory.
The introduction phase of the automobile industry in America lasted approximately 25 years, starting
in 1890, while the introduction stage of the personal computer industry took only four or five years
starting in the late 1970s.
In addition, an industry could be at different phases of its life cycle in different countries around
the globe.
This is the case with the automobile industry.
Studies have shown the market is still in its growth phase in three of the BRIC countries, Russia,
China and India.
Competent top managers understand and exploit these trends.
They know the phase their industry is in and have expectations about how long it's going to last.
Ideally, they will enter a market in the growth phase.
Compete throughout maturity when cash flow generation is the highest.
And then look for new opportunities when the decline phase approaches.
That is why the industry life cycle model and the recognition of its stages is crucial.
This framework facilitates the understanding of an environment characterized by constant change, where
companies must adapt their strategies to survive.
Over the next few lessons.
We will explore some traits distinguishing the four lifecycle stages.
This is a fundamental exercise as it would allow you to understand the processes happening during each
stage and the mechanisms typically applied by managers when their business is in one of the phases.


=== Strategy: Acquiring a competitive advantage ===


--- The Introduction stage - A new industry is born ---

It is one thing to know the stages of an industry lifecycle and another to understand them from a strategic
point of view.
Over the next few lessons, we'll dig deeper and learn more about each of the four stages here.
Our focus will be the introduction stage.
The infancy of an industry as we already know it is characterised by low industry awareness among customers,
technological uncertainty, high capital needs and low levels of competition.
There are two major ways to approach the introduction phase, participate in it from the beginning,
or wait a while and enter later when customers know the products offered by the industry exist and there
is a market for them.
So what should a firm do?
Is it better to act as a pioneer, or is it preferable to be a late entrant?
Well, there isn't a right or a wrong answer.
Some managers see a lot of value in innovating.
First movers can assemble a team, mainly driven by the desire to build a special product, one that
has not been created yet.
Naturally, these will be individuals not driven by monetary compensation.
They are attracted by the idea.
Frankly, this is the type of motivation you want on board.
Initially, people inspired and willing to go the extra mile because they want to create something great.
In addition, first movers gain a valuable advantage in terms of know how.
They are the first ones to get their hands dirty and can learn from their own experience.
By the time competitors enter the market, first movers will have a well-trained team that understands
the product and has a clear idea of how it can be improved.
In some businesses, entrepreneurs are further incentivized by the opportunity to patent their innovations,
which naturally gives first movers an advantage.
The sooner they start working on a product, the likelier it is they can patent some of its features,
which will make things very difficult for competitors.
An example of a successful company that acted as a first mover in its market is Amazon.
It was the first major online bookstore and lead.
It never relinquished.
Most colleges use Amazon's competition against Barnes and Noble as a case study example of first class
strategy applied in practice.
Amazon grew its product offering, developed sophisticated algorithms to predict customer preferences,
and developed highly specialized logistics skills, coupled with the ability to manage a huge inventory
of goods.
They simply knew what they were doing.
However, the company acting as a first mover is not necessarily the one winning the race in the long
run.
Sometimes it's advantageous to be a late entrant.
The introduction phase is almost always unprofitable.
There are significant costs for product development and marketing.
Research and development teams need financing.
Marketing runs expensive informational campaigns.
It is very difficult to break even.
Technological uncertainties can be high, and there are no guarantees that a new technology that is
much better wouldn't come up from somewhere.
So can we agree that participating in the introduction phase is very risky?
Great.
Some managers prefer to be more cautious.
That's true mainly for larger companies that have an established core business and are unwilling to
allocate resources and deal with new concepts.
Instead, late entrants prefer to monitor industries in the introduction, Phase and Act.
Once customers are aware of the product and some are already purchasing it.
Entering at a later stage allows them to exploit mistakes made by first movers and learn from them.
Often larger firms will acquire a fast growing startup that has successfully gone through the introduction
phase.
That's another strategic option that could make sense and will combine some upsides for first movers
and late entrants.
Many companies have been really successful acting as late entrants.
For example, the Ford Motor Company was the first to use an assembly line for its production of the
mass market automobile model T, which was a huge success in 1921.
The firm sold over 900,000 Model TS in the first few years of operation and had a 60% market share.
A few years later, in 1931, one of its main competitors, General Motors, came up with a differentiated
product line and won 31% of the automobile market in the United States.
First mover advantage didn't impede General Motors to take over the market and remain its leader until
2005.
Okay, great.
This does it for now.
In our next lesson, we'll learn about some strategic subtleties in the growth phase.
Thanks for watching.


=== Strategy: The three main competitive strategies ===


--- The Growth stage - An industry in its expansion phase ---

Once the introduction phase is over and industry enters the growth stage, the price of products is
relatively high as customers become price insensitive.
When few competitors operate on the market and there are little or no substitutes available by keeping
the price high in the early stages, companies cover their initial investments relatively quickly.
However, this also makes the industry more attractive to new entrants.
Make sense, right?
If a person opens the first restaurant in a city, she should charge higher prices because there is
no competition.
This will be a very profitable business that will attract attention from other people who decide to
open restaurants, too.
So we can conclude that in the growth phase, sales grow and profits follow, which leads to increased
levels of competition.
The good thing is the pie is still growing, so sales increase for most participants in the market.
The more companies enter the industry, the more money is spent on advertising and more customers will
be reached.
So that's good news.
The challenge becomes winning a bigger portion of the pie.
This can be done in several ways.
Companies can spend on advertising increasing their own brand popularity among existing customers.
Alternatively, they can invest in production capacity, which would result in economies of scale.
A higher production capacity allows for the manufacturing of more units, so unitary costs will decrease
regardless of whether we sell 10,000 or 20,000 units of a product.
The salaries paid for accounting, administration, I.T. and top management are more or less the same,
right?
Well, the more units we sell, the lower the cost per unit we will have.
Given that fixed costs remain the same and suggested by their name.
Electric cars are between their introduction and growth phase.
And Tesla is preparing to build the biggest plant in the world, which would allow it to produce more
electric batteries than any other company in the world and cope with the demand for its mass market
model.
Three.
It is not infrequent for companies to expand internationally during the growth phase.
New geographies are an exciting opportunity as firms can market their goods abroad after they have done
the heavy lifting at home.
In the globalised world of today.
If an industry manages to break through and become popular in one country, it is more than likely that
many consumers would have heard of it and would try it in other countries as well.
Production wise, in some situations of the early stages of the life cycle, the demand may be higher
than the supply, which could result in capacity shortages.
We call this a seller's market.
We call this a seller's market, as it's easy for sellers to find buyers, while buyers have a tough
time finding the product.
Most efforts throughout the growth stage are directed towards one goal winning a higher market share.
A higher market share positions a company in a favorable way for when the business becomes profitable
and is close to maturity.
A sizable share would result in a strong brand awareness, favorable cost structure and bargaining position
and in more sales, which will ultimately translate in a very profitable business.
Stay tuned because in our next video, we'll discuss how to succeed in the maturity phase of an industry.
Thanks for watching.


=== Strategy: Corporate growth strategies ===


--- The Maturity stage - An industry at its peak ---

All major companies have entered the market.
There is a dominant product design.
Customers know of the industry and become interested in different brands.
Competition increases significantly.
Welcome to the maturity stage of industry development.
As we can see here, during maturity, sales will reach their peak and will stop growing.
So the pie we saw earlier will stop expanding.
This will leave companies only one way to grow their sales.
Now, when a bigger portion of the pie, but a bigger portion of the pie for one company means a smaller
portion of the pie for another company.
If somebody wants to win, somebody else must lose.
This gives rise to intense competition in the industry.
The direct result of competition is lower prices for customers.
In a mature industry, several companies will try to win a higher market share by offering a standardized
version of the product at a lower price.
This, of course, affects profitability.
The more price competition there is in an industry, the lower the profits of the firms in that industry.
At this stage, customers have a lot of information about the product, they know how to use it and
how it can help them.
This is why companies invest heavily in brand advertising.
They want to promote their own products and communicate to customers that their products are better
than the ones offered by competitors.
When growth opportunities have been exhausted, the consolidation of companies makes a lot of strategic
sense.
The main idea is by joining forces, two companies operating in the same industry will have a better
competitive position.
They can use the same distribution channels, consolidate their marketing expenditure and importantly,
eliminate redundant costs.
The two companies need only one headquarters.
Only one top management team.
Only one accounting department, and so on.
Cost optimizations are typical at this stage of industry development.
If the pie isn't growing, then cost optimization and process improvements are one of the few ways to
improve profitability and remain competitive.
A good example of a mature market is the personal care industry.
Companies like Beiersdorf and P&amp;G have experienced a slow, almost flat growth of sales over the last
five years.
Their advertising is brand oriented.
People have used shampoos for decades now.
Peng's goal is to convince them it's head and shoulders.
Shampoo is the right combination of price and quality they need.
In 2005, P&amp;G acquired Gillette to boost its sales and expand its product portfolio.
The acquisition led to several synergies in terms of distribution costs and marketing.
Smart, right?
In our next lesson, we'll discuss how companies approach the decline phase of an industry.
Stay tuned.


=== Strategy: The SWOT analysis framework ===


--- The Decline stage - An obsolete industry ---

Hi and welcome back to NBA in a Box.
In this lesson, we will start talking about the last phase of the industry lifecycle model, the decline
phase.
First, however, I would like to ask you a favor.
I would be really grateful if you go to the course dashboard, that's easy to do.
All you have to do is click here and leave a rating for the course.
This will mean a lot to me and will help other students know that this course is worth taking.
The reason I'm asking you to do this now is that this is a large course, and most people do not complete
all the lessons in one sitting, eventually forgetting to leave a rating.
So please do it now.
It should only take you a couple of seconds and it will really validate our efforts.
Thank you so much for this.
Okay.
Now, having said that, let's continue with our topic.
The industry life cycle model and its decline phase.
Naturally, the last phase will examine in more detail is the decline phase.
It is characterized by an absolute decrease of sales for at least three or more years.
Customers abandon the market due to changing tastes or an emerging new industry.
In the decline phase, the pie doesn't expand.
It becomes smaller.
Companies that used to sell 100,000 units of a product in a calendar year must deal with the situation
when they sell 70,000 and less.
Obviously, there will be overcapacity in the industry because firms have invested in plants that can
produce 100,000 units per year.
These plants are not used to their fullest potential, which increases unit costs and drives down profitability.
To sell more units, some businesses will be tempted to discount heavily and slash their prices, which
will lead to negative margins for companies with inefficient cost structures.
They will likely be the first to abandon the market.
Other companies will abandon the market too, as they will probably prefer to prioritize a new industry
which is promising in terms of growth potential.
There are four main ways to deal with the decline phase.
A company may abandon the market completely and disinvest its assets as fast as possible.
Or it may adopt the so called harvesting strategy, which is the most popular approach used by managers.
The harvesting strategy consists of reducing investments and expenditures no R&amp;D, no marketing expenses,
no investments in CapEx and so on, and focusing on cash flow generation.
This isn't a bad strategy.
Alternatively, it can decide to be bold and win a market.
Everyone else is quitting.
If it does that, it will gain other companies marketing share and become a market leader.
This is typically done by making a substantial investment in, let's say, marketing when everyone else
is harvesting.
Other companies will be unwilling to respond and will probably prefer to abandon the market.
The fourth way to address industry decline is by continuing the execution of a niche strategy.
More on that in one of our next chapters.
Most of you probably do not even remember vinyl records, but they were popular a few decades ago.
However, the invention of compact cassettes and then CDs led to the decline of the industry.
This doesn't mean the entire vinyl industry ended instantly.
Even when an industry is in decline, some companies operate and do well.
Such is the case with the Czech company GZ Media, which embraced the opportunity and became the world's
largest vinyl producer.
Its sales figures continue to grow in 2014, 2015 and 2016.
The company achieved a 40% year on year growth of revenues between 2014 and 2015, selling 25 million
records.
GZ Media's ability to win in a declining market proves abandonment isn't the only viable options for
managers at this final stage of development.


=== Strategy: Tesla - practical case study ===


--- Michael Porter's Five Forces model ---

One of the most famous and widely used frameworks that will help us analyze the competitive environment
in an industry is Michael Porter's five forces model.
His work is popular in both academic and corporate circles.
It states the level of competition in an industry is influenced by five forces.
The threat of new entrants.
The threat of substitute products.
The rivalry among existing firms.
The bargaining power of buyers.
And the bargaining power of suppliers.
This model is fairly easy to understand, isn't it?
The more competition we have in an industry, the lower the expected profitability of the companies
operating in it.
Clients have multiple alternatives and companies compete to win clients.
A competitive environment is typically characterised by lower prices, hence lower margins and by higher
spending for marketing and sales, which reduces bottom line profitability in a significant way.
The five forces model teaches us that competition has multiple dimensions.
It is important how many direct competitors we have, but it is also important if clients can use alternative
products or if new entrants are likely to enter the market and increase current levels of competition.
In addition, we shouldn't forget clients and suppliers can have a very strong bargaining power and
dictate the terms if the companies in an industry cannot do anything about it.
Understanding how each of these forces impacts an industry, helps us assess its profitability and attractiveness.
This framework is often used by top management teams when they need to decide a course of action and
adapt their strategy to the competitive environment surrounding their company.
In the next lesson, we will examine each of the five forces and will discuss their strategic importance.
Once we've done that, you will have a clear idea of how to analyse the intensity of competition in
an industry.
Thanks for watching.


=== Management: An Introduction ===


--- The threat of new entrants ---

The first of the five forces we will examine is the threat of new entrants.
When do you think new entrants will be tempted to move into an industry?
Usually this happens when the companies currently operating in the industry are doing well.
Profits are like a magnet.
The more money you make, the more people will look at what you're doing and ask themselves if they
can do the same thing and profit from it too.
Right.
It's the same with companies.
The higher the profits of the firms operating in an industry, the bigger the incentive for new entrants.
They want a piece of the pie and will be willing to join and compete to get it.
This is a threat for firms currently competing in the market as it means they will have to compete with
a new player which will likely steal some of their current and future clients.
And if the number of new entrants is too high, they may lose substantial business and produce losses.
In this context, it becomes critical for companies to assess the threat of new entrants and, if possible,
try to come up with mechanisms that would prevent the entry of new competitors.
There are several ways to create such barriers to entry, and here we'll examine a few of them, mostly
the ones that are legal.
First, the companies in an industry can have different cost advantages.
They may have low cost sources of raw materials, government subsidies, or less expensive localized
resources.
Such is the case with Saudi Aramco, which has access to the biggest and most accessible oil reserves
in the world.
The close relationship with the government of Saudi Arabia gives the company an absolute cost advantage
over competitors like BP and ExxonMobil.
Another barrier to entry related to costs is the achievement of economies of scale, which represent
a proportionate saving in costs gained by an increased level of production.
Industries with large scale operations may have high capital outlay or be R&amp;D intensive.
In such situations, new entrants may face high unitary costs if they operate on a small scale.
Imagine that a telecom must invest $50 million in its network before selling services to its clients.
Existing firms have tens of thousands of clients, but new entrants must start from zero, and even
then, the $50 million investment would be required.
This is a significant barrier to entry, isn't it?
Very few investors would put in $50 Million knowing they will have to sustain some additional losses
before they acquire a critical number of clients that allows them to break even.
Product differentiation is very difficult to achieve, but when a company creates a product perceived
as differentiated by its clients, the company can be much more relaxed about the entry of new competitors,
even if barriers to entry are low.
A typical example of this is Coca-Cola.
The entry barrier for the beverage industry is relatively low, but few new entrants can compete with
the company.
Coca-Cola can retain its market share because of its high brand recognition and because clients prefer
its products.
Another obstacle for new entrants could be the access to channels of distribution or the lack of them.
Not all companies that produce detergents can place their products on supermarket shelves.
P&amp;G can do so because it has an extensive product portfolio and invests billions in advertising all
over the world.
It will be very difficult to convince supermarket chains to replace PNGs products with a new product
that is unknown to customers.
Actually, that is one way the Internet has transformed so many industries.
It allows new businesses to overcome the established barriers to distribution and sell their products
online.
In certain situations, government policies could be another effective barrier to entry.
Such is the case with the telecommunication industry.
In many countries where new entrants need a licence from public authorities and the number of telecom
operators is regulated.
And last but not least, the expected retaliation the current industry companies will have can be an
impediment to entry if new entrants believe that after they start operating in the industry, existing
players will increase their investments in advertising and will offer amazing promotions to customers
to keep them.
They are much less likely to enter the industry.
All of these are valid tactics that can help protect the companies in an industry from the threat of
new entrants.
Often it is much more efficient to create these barriers before a competitor joins the industry rather
than competing for market share afterwards.


=== Management: The Planning function ===


--- The threat of substitute products ---

Okay.
Great.
We saw the threat of new entrants is one factor that can affect the level of competition in an industry.
But in this lesson, we will learn competition doesn't only come from companies operating in the same
industry.
If you operate a pizza delivery restaurant, who are your competitors?
All pizza restaurants making deliveries in town.
Right?
Right.
However, sushi restaurant sell food too.
So a person must choose if they want to have sushi or pizza first and then which pizza restaurant they
want to order from.
But sushi restaurants are a competitor as well.
They offer a substitute product.
It is equally bad if somebody decides to eat in a sushi restaurant or to take away from a competing
pizza place.
You've lost a client either way.
So substitute goods are goods that satisfy the same need as the one your product aims to satisfy.
Straightforward, right.
There are hundreds of examples of substitute products.
You are constantly choosing between substitute products when you decide whether to go to the cinema
or to the theater, whether to drink water or Coca Cola.
When you choose to wear jeans and not a skirt, all of these are substitute products.
In some industries, the threat of substitute products is low.
One example is the tobacco industry.
People smoking cigarettes are unwilling to switch to a substitute product like herbal cigarettes or
electronic cigarettes.
In other industries, there are several substitute products and the threat level is high.
In today's world, the oil industry faces competition from multiple substitute products.
There are electric cars, hybrid cars, cars running on hydrogen water and even vegetable oils.
The threat of substitute products is substantial, and this plays a role in financials.
Oil companies can sell their product for too high a price.
Otherwise, it would be much more convenient to switch to electric cars over the long term.
These dynamics are interesting, aren't they?
It is difficult to respond to the threat of substitute products for several reasons.
First, it is challenging to identify which substitute products take the most business out of your industry.
If you operate a cinema chain and think of substitute products, you would come up with a double digit
list.
Instead of watching a movie.
People may go to the theater, go to a concert, visit a conference, watch a sports game on TV, spend
an afternoon outside, eat in a restaurant.
There are so many options.
And then even if a company is aware of the substitute products it competes against.
It is much more difficult to create barriers of entry for products in different industries.
They have a different cost structure and can be subject to a different regulatory environment, and
clients perceive them in a different way.
This is why the analysis of substitute products is a fundamental exercise.
It determines the attractiveness of an industry and helps you decide whether this is the niche you want
to pursue.


=== Management: The Organization function ===


--- The intensity of current competition ---

The current level of competition force concerns the intensity of competition in the marketplace.
Powerful competitors are a big threat.
We already know that competing firms do not make life difficult just because they acquire new customers.
They also try to access our distribution channels, hire our key employees, and work with our top suppliers.
They are our true nemesis.
They do what we do and want what's ours.
Okay, I started using Godfather language, but it's true.
Intense competition makes an industry less attractive.
One of the most frequently used tools to determine if an industry is concentrated and dominated by a
few big players is the concentration ratio.
It is measured as the sum of the market shares of the four largest firms in an industry.
If the top four companies have a combined market share of over 80%, then an industry can be considered
highly concentrated.
Examples of highly concentrated industries in the UK are the sugar industry, the tobacco industry and
the gas distribution industry.
Examples of low concentration industries are the construction industry, the furniture industry and
wholesale distributions.
Depending on how fragmented the competition in an industry is, you should try to adapt your competitive
model and use a strategy that is likely to succeed under current circumstances.
If a few big players dominate the market, it will be hard to attack them directly and invest as much
as they do in marketing.
Perhaps it would be better to find a market niche and win significant market share there in an industry
that is not as concentrated.
It would be much easier to invest heavily in marketing and aim for market leadership.


=== Management: The Staffing function - Pre-Hire activities ===


--- The bargaining power of suppliers ---

The next two of Porter's five forces will examine are the bargaining power of suppliers and the bargaining
power of buyers.
In this video, we'll start with suppliers and in the next one will be ready to describe the client
company relationship.
There are several ways suppliers can exercise pressure on buyers.
They can raise prices, lower the quality of supplied materials, reduce their availability.
That can be disruptive for normal business operations for several reasons.
Price uncertainty results in an uncertain cost structure.
How are we going to build long term relationships with clients if we continue to raise prices because
of constant pressure from suppliers?
The quality of the supplied raw materials is critical to it transfers directly into the quality of products.
The moment customers notice we use low quality materials, their perception of the entire brand will
change.
Availability impacts our production cycle.
If suppliers do not send the materials on time, this automatically results in a delay for final customers.
All three issues are serious, so the relationship with suppliers is important and we have to pay attention
to this force.
Several factors determine the bargaining power of suppliers.
When suppliers are highly concentrated, their bargaining power is high.
For example, IKEA buys furniture from several small companies.
They have thousands of suppliers here.
The bargaining power of suppliers is low.
Gas stations buy fuel from one or maybe two refineries in their region.
Remember that transportation costs are too high to buy fuel from distant refineries.
Here, refineries have strong bargaining power as their clients do not have many alternative options.
If suppliers are vertically integrated and can sell an end product to customers, then their bargaining
power is even stronger.
In the example with refineries, if a refinery owns a chain of petrol stations, then that makes it
even more powerful when negotiating with other chains because it might sell the end product exclusively
in its own chain and close the business circle.
Finally, bargaining power is often determined by the availability of substitute products.
If there are several substitute products available, suppliers will be much more cautious when preparing
prices.
A company selling online courses will be very cautious when preparing an offer to a client, as there
are several alternatives to its product life training sessions, handbooks, in-house training and so
on.
The easier it is for a company to circumvent its suppliers, the higher the bargaining power it has
in negotiations about price, quality and terms of delivery.


=== Management: The Staffing function - Post-Hire activities ===


--- The bargaining power of clients ---

The last of the five forces will consider is the bargaining power of buyers.
Okay, so the client firm relationship is delicate.
Remember the old saying that the client's always right?
Well, there is a reason that exists.
Clients are where a company's business comes from.
When they buy our products, we generate revenue.
Without them, we're doomed.
So we have to keep them happy and make sure they get what they need if we want to stay in business in
the long run.
However, clients have a different bargaining power in different industries.
The reasoning will apply here is similar to the one we used when describing the bargaining power of
suppliers.
The more fragmented the number of clients is, the less bargaining power they will have.
It's one thing to negotiate with IBM on a $15 million contract and a whole different thing to sell a
$15 shirt.
Right.
The more important a client is, the more revenue we get from them, the higher the bargaining power
they have.
Clients cannot push down prices when they're small and their purchases insignificant.
Regarding the overall business a firm generates, if clients have multiple alternatives, their bargaining
power increases.
As we know, we have to compete harder for them or they will buy competitors products.
Microsoft need not worry about this too much as there are very few competitive operating systems for
personal computers.
Right.
Windows is one of the few options clients have.
In the car market, for example, clients can choose among several alternatives when they need transportation,
public transport, trains, buses, underground lines, bicycles going on foot, you name it.
Car dealers understand that and will push down prices to make a better offer for people in the market
for a car.
If cars were too expensive, they could just use an alternative means of transportation and not buy
one.
Okay, great.
Let's say you're running a company that has installed an ERP software that costs $300,000 to install
and $50,000 per year to maintain.
All employees in your company know how to use the software.
Let's say another I.T. company approaches you and offers to install an ERP software that costs $150,000
to install and $35,000 per year to maintain.
Which factors will you consider?
First, you will have to spend an additional $150,000 for installation.
You've already spent $300,000 for the current software, and then all employees must learn the new program,
which will probably result in significant inefficiencies.
There are high switching costs, which makes the new offer less attractive than it already is.
The moral of the story is that the bargaining power of clients is smaller if they face high switching
costs when choosing the services of a different firm.
It is worth mentioning that in the market for highly differentiated products, buyers are not interested
in pricing.
They want high quality products and therefore are not price sensitive.
Typically, these buyers will be well informed and can distinguish a high quality product from the rest.
A person who wants to buy a Ferrari or a Rolex wouldn't be price sensitive, but will require the finest
quality.
So we can say there isn't pressure towards prices.
We can wrap it up here now that we've described the five forces shaping an industry's attractiveness.
It will be great to use Michael Porter's framework and analyse the competitive environment in the sports
apparel industry.
Sounds exciting.
Great.
Let's do it in our next lesson.


=== Management: The Leading function ===


--- Porter's Five Forces framework applied in practice ---

Okay.
We're doing great.
So far, we've covered a great deal of important topics.
We learned about the rule of strategy, about the purpose of a company's goals, mission and vision,
statements about the different stages of the industry lifecycle model and about Porter's five forces
framework.
That's awesome.
In this lesson, we will reinforce what we've learned about Porter's five forces model by applying it
in practice.
Our task will be to analyze the global sportswear industry.
So let's get started.
The sportswear industry is a mature market that shows no signs of decline.
People need clothing and love wearing sports apparel in their free time, both outside and at home.
In the last few years, the industry has experienced a steady growth and the total market is expected
to reach $180 billion by 2020.
Globalization, economic growth, favorable fashion and style trends play a positive role for the current
growth the market is experiencing.
So if we have to imagine the industry life cycle graph we saw earlier, the industry is in this phase
right here where the market is still increasing, but the industry is in its mature phase and the companies
in it are profitable and generate stable cash flows.
The pie is still growing and most companies are happy.
Right.
Let's take off our rosy glasses now and look at the competitive environment in the sportswear industry,
shall we?
As you remember, that's the force at the very center of the five forces model.
These are the companies competing in the industry.
There are a few names recognizable at a global level.
Nike, Adidas, ASICs, Under Armour, Puma and Lululemon are some of the bigger firms out there.
Nike and Adidas owned several other notable brands that you've probably heard of.
Nike owns Converse while Adidas owns the Reebok brand.
There are probably thousands of other smaller firms competing at a local level.
Proof that the industry is not concentrated is in its concentration ratio.
Previously, we learned a concentration ratio of the first four players in an industry of over 80% means
the industry is highly concentrated.
Well, this is not the case for the sportswear market.
The combined share of the top four players in this chart accounts for 38% of the total industry turnover.
The two global giants in this industry are Nike and Adidas, with a market share of 20.1 and 12.4% respectively.
ASICs and Under Armour complete the top four list, but have a significantly lower amount of sales 2.7%
and 2.6% of the total market.
The fragmentation of this market and the presence of a few global giants mean the other competitors
can compete with established global brands at a local level only.
As with any mature market, brand names and marketing efforts play an important role, and smaller companies
cannot keep up with the level of investments.
Nike and Adidas can afford.
There isn't a price war in the industry, as the two top players offer differentiated products sold
at a premium compared to the goods sold by local producers.
The driving force in this competition is brand recognition and quality perception.
The threat of new entrants is relatively low.
A new competitor isn't likely to intimidate established players.
Nike, Adidas, ASICs and Under Armour have invested billions of dollars in marketing communication.
Their brands are global.
Therefore, a new entrant with significant ambitions must spend a lot of money to compete in the market.
In addition, the new company must invest in human capital in terms of designer teams, production personnel
and other employees who have the necessary knowhow for the creation of sports apparel sold worldwide.
Not an easy task, right?
So the barriers to entry are significant if we consider the global market.
However, new companies will have a much easier task if they want to compete at a local level.
Local competition requires limited amounts of resources, and usually it is much easier to access existing
distribution channels at a local level.
If a company aspires to be a global player, perhaps the way to do it is by entering a smaller niche
of the market.
This requires a smaller investment and allows targeting a specific need of customers.
We will explore this topic further in the marketing module of this course.
But it makes sense from a strategic perspective to write do not attack large and established companies
with a full scale attack because this is too difficult to organize and finance.
Instead, as a new competitor, target a particular market segment of customers and try to win a significant
market share there.
Okay, great.
What about substitute products?
Are they a big threat to the sportswear industry?
Yes and no.
There will always be demand for casual sportswear attire.
However, changing customer tastes and styles may influence this demand in a negative way.
Sportswear is trendy.
Who knows if ten years from now, this will continue to be the case.
Obviously, other clothing items are a valid substitute for sportswear clothing, but for now, there
isn't significant pressure coming up from this force.
Right.
The remaining two forces we must examine are the bargaining power of suppliers and buyers.
Let's start with suppliers.
The main raw materials purchased by sportswear producers are cotton, other types of fabric, ethylene,
synthetic materials and rubber.
Larger companies like Nike and Adidas can obtain cheaper prices from suppliers, which translates into
a better cost structure compared to competitors such as Under Armour who buy smaller quantities of these
products.
So smaller companies may have a harder time sourcing raw materials at a price that is competitive.
One way to circumvent this issue is by using alternative fabrics, which means also coming up with an
innovative product.
The number of suppliers in the industry is relatively high.
Therefore, we can conclude this force isn't creating too much pressure for companies operating in the
industry.
What about the bargaining power of customers?
Given this is a business to consumers market where end customers are millions of people, we can say
customers cannot negotiate prices.
However, it is also true that there are thousands of firms operating in the market.
Customers know they have plenty of choice.
So right now, the brand establishes global players have differentiated their products to create products
of high quality and invest heavily in brand recognition.
Companies understand this is a market in which the connection between sports fans and sports stars is
strong.
Recently, Nike signed a $500 million lifetime contract with LeBron James, one of its most famous testimonials.
By creating branded products, market participants differentiate their brand and relate to the outstanding
sport achievements of their testimonials.
Turns out the main driving force in the client firm relationship in the sportswear industry is perceived
value and brand recognition.
Okay, so in summary, we can say the sportswear industry looks very good at the moment.
Established brands like Nike and Adidas do not face too much competition at a global level.
They have a good relationship with both suppliers and clients and are not threatened by substitute products
and new entrants in the near term.
This will do for now.
We provided a holistic analysis of the sportswear industry.
This is how we apply Porter's five forces framework in practice.
An awesome exercise, wasn't it?
