=== Marketing: An Introduction ===


--- An introduction to Game Theory ---

All right, great.
Now that we know how to work with Porter's five forces model, we're ready to add more tools to our
arsenal.
Porter's framework is great as it provides a holistic view and considers multiple layers of an industry's
dynamics.
However, it has a slight drawback.
It is static and does not consider the interactions among competitor companies.
What happens in the real business world in practice is that the decisions made by one firm depend on
the decisions made by the other companies in the industry.
The game theory framework allows us to model these interactions to a certain extent.
Game theory could be considered the science of competitive strategy.
It is a tool that allows us to explore decision making.
Situations where the choice is made by one party have repercussions for the other parties.
The decision making situations are called games, while the individuals and groups are called players.
The pioneers of game theory are John von Neumann and Oskar Morgenstern.
Game theory did not exist as a separate field until von Neumann published a paper in 1928.
Soon after, it became clear it is a widely applicable instrument with uses in many spheres such as
business, politics, international relations, war psychology and so on.
Game theory studies, human and corporate interaction, cooperation and conflict reactions.
In a competitive situation, there are two types of games cooperative and non cooperative.
In the next few lessons, we'll examine both types.


=== Marketing: Building a Marketing Strategy ===


--- Zero-sum games - approaching situations with a win-lose perspective ---

We call a zero sum games the situations in which one party must lose if the other party will win.
Poker is a classic example of a zero sum game.
If two people play poker, one of them will win and the other will lose.
There isn't any in between.
The two players can't cooperate.
They're against each other.
So if Player A wins $100, then this automatically means Player B has lost $100.
The money they own collectively didn't change.
Hence why we call this a zero sum game.
The amount won by player A plus, the amount lost by player B equals zero.
There are many examples of zero sum games in life tennis, basketball, gambling games.
Often, businessmen perceive negotiations as a zero sum game.
Their reasoning is, if I am going to win, then the other party must lose.
These are called win lose negotiations, but we will cover this in the negotiation module of this course.
Regardless, this is a wrong approach.
In the long term, we cannot build fruitful relationships if we don't create value for our counterparts.
Please remember that zero sum games are the exact opposite of win win situations, which is how business
should be perceived.
Both parties should be better off after a business transaction.
The fundamental requirement for a win win situation is communication and cooperation.
Techniques completely absent in the zero sum game approach.


=== Marketing: How to set up an effective Marketing Mix ===


--- Non-zero-sum games - considering both cooperation and confrontation ---

Most real world situations are not zero sum games with a clear optimal choice in a zero sum game.
Both parties have a preferred outcome they want to win, and there are no doubts.
In reality, things are different.
There typically isn't a universally accepted solution or a dominant winning strategy.
A non-zero-sum game is different because it allows participants to choose whether they want to cooperate
or to compete.
Remember, the two players can have common and opposing interest at the same time.
The most classic example of a non-zero-sum game is the prisoner's dilemma.
Here, two players must decide what they will do, and the outcome of for both depends on their own
actions.
So we have two criminals arrested, suspected in a robbery.
Police officers hold the two criminals in different interrogation rooms and offer them the following
options.
If one of them confesses the crime and the other one doesn't, the one who's confessed walks away free
while the other one receives a ten year sentence.
If both confess, they will receive a five year sentence each, and if neither one confesses, they
will both walk away free.
Okay.
So this is how the typical non-zero-sum game with two players looks like.
At first sight, it appears the situation in which the two prisoners cooperate and do not confess is
a clear, optimal strategy.
Right?
However, please remember the two prisoners are held separately so they wouldn't know the decision of
the other party until they make their own.
It becomes very dangerous, trusting the person in the next room with five or ten years of your life.
If a prisoner confesses, then the worst that can happen is being sentenced to five years.
But if he doesn't confess, he may end up in jail for ten years.
Or even better if he confesses and the other prisoner doesn't.
He walks away free.
Communication is not allowed.
What do you think ought to happen?
John Nash, the famous Nobel Prize winner portrayed in the movie A Beautiful Mind, came up with a solution
known as Nash's equilibrium.
He proved that if a player in the context we described earlier has chosen a strategy and other players
can't benefit by changing their strategies, then we would have an equilibrium in the prisoner's dilemma.
If one of the prisoners confesses, he would face a zero or a five year sentence, that's much better
than facing zero or ten years of prison, right?
Yes.
The optimal global solution is to deny confessing.
However, given that prisoners cannot communicate, they will choose a solution optimizing their own
utility.
In our next lesson, we'll provide an example of how game theory and Nash equilibrium can be used when
analyzing a business case.
Thanks for watching.


=== Marketing: How to set up an effective Marketing Mix - Product decisions ===


--- Tobacco companies - a real-life example of Game Theory application ---

Game theory is widely applied in practice.
It helps us analyze and understand a number of situations that happen in business.
Let's examine the following real life example.
In 1971, the American government banned cigarette companies from advertising their products on TV.
One would think this would hurt their business as less consumers would see their brand advertising,
and this would have a negative impact on sales.
However, what happened is surprising.
All four major tobacco companies registered higher profits than before.
But how come?
First, we need to point out that people who smoke tobacco will continue to do so, even if they don't
see many advertisements on TV.
However, advertisements are not useless as they shape clients brand perceptions and influence their
preference for one brand over another.
It turns out that tobacco companies would be better off without advertising, but only if all companies
don't do it.
Otherwise, the companies that invest in advertising will grow their brand recognition and will expand
their market share, which would allow them to earn more.
Let's use game theory to describe the situation.
Well, imagine there are only two companies operating in the tobacco market.
The outcomes they may choose are the following.
Win 50 million each without spending money on advertising.
If Company A advertises and Company B doesn't, then Company A will win $60 Million and Company B will
win $25 million.
In the opposite scenario, if company B advertises and company A doesn't, the outcome reverses.
And finally, if both companies advertise, each wins $40 million.
This is a great illustration of what happened in reality before the government's ban on TV ads.
The dilemma faced by the two tobacco companies was very similar to the prisoner's dilemma we saw earlier.
Both players are better off if they cooperate.
However, they cannot be certain the other party will adhere.
So they opt for a solution that guarantees them the best possible outcome in all states of the world.
Nash's equilibrium indicates companies will choose to advertise, and that's precisely what happened
in practice.
Each player maximized their own utility, which ultimately resulted in the situation we observed in
real life.


=== Marketing: How to set up an effective Marketing Mix - Pricing decisions ===


--- Focusing on the inside of a business - An Introduction ---

Hi, and welcome back to the next chapter of the NBA in a Bucks course.
So far, we've studied several important concepts, which should show you how businesses make decisions
when it comes to external issues and issues related to their industry.
Starting from this chapter, we'll learn about a company's internal strategic decision making process.
We'll learn about the different stages in a company's life cycle, about the way firms appraise their
resources and how they use them to obtain a competitive advantage about the popular SWOT analysis and
about the different strategies companies use to acquire a competitive advantage.
These lessons will help us understand what happens inside a company, which is among the most important
factors that determine the success of a business.
Let's dive in.


=== Marketing: How to set up an effective Marketing Mix - Place decisions ===


--- A company's life cycle model -  what should be done at different stages ---

Earlier in this course, we talked about industries going through a life cycle.
Well, companies go through different development stages over time.
And nowhere is it said these phases should coincide with an industry's development stages.
That's why it is very important for managers to recognize these stages and the possible challenges and
obstacles they will likely face in each phase.
This would help them manage change more effectively and apply different approaches to be successful.
We can identify seven development stages in a company's life cycle.
Each is unique and represents a challenge from a different perspective.
Typically, a company's life cycle starts with a seed stage.
Then if things go well, a startup will be formed.
Once the startup grows, will be in growth stage, followed by the establishment and expansion phases.
The final two stages of a company's development are maturity and possibly an exit.
Let's dig deeper and describe each of these stages in more detail.
The seed stage starts when a business idea is born.
This is when you decide to set up a company.
However, before starting it, it would be reasonable to verify the feasibility of your business idea.
Discussions with other people can be fruitful.
Industry specialists, business associates, and even family and friends can help you formulate your
idea and decide on a plan of action.
An entrepreneur must imagine the whole process and make sure there is a match between the business scope
and his personal background, experience, skills and passions.
You will have to make several decisions related to business planning, ownership structure, staff recruitment
and financing.
Having a clear idea how the venture will be financed and what resources you will need in the first months
of operation is critical, given that during this period the new company will likely operate producing
no revenues and will incur significant costs.
If you plan to contact investors, present to them a well thought out business plan that includes a
3 to 5 year projection of financials.
If everything goes well in the seed stage, you can form a startup company.
Studies have shown this is the riskiest stage of the entire life cycle.
90% of startup companies fail.
So founders must be careful in this phase.
Usually during the startup phase, the firm can take one of its products or services to market and see
if clients perceive it in a positive way.
Active listening allows for much needed fine tuning and product improvement.
In addition, the firm must focus on building a lasting relationship with its first clients.
Using cash flow in a reasonable manner is high on the list of priorities.
In addition, the time to take products to market is crucial at this stage, as it would allow the company
to register revenues and cover some of its expenses.
Once a company takes its product to market and start selling it to an increasing number of customers,
it enters the growth stage.
This is when the majority of companies reach breakeven and to generating sufficient revenues to cover
their expenses.
Two conflicting phenomena occur here.
The company needs its founders attention for multiple issues like customer satisfaction management,
administrative tasks, management of daily activities and employee feedback.
But she shouldn't miss the big picture and underestimate her role as a company head.
That's why it's time to think about hiring personnel who can take over some of the founder's responsibilities
once they are on board.
The founder must think about how to define and communicate the firm's goals and how to motivate staff
towards the firm's vision.
It's highly likely the company must invest in new management and accounting systems at this stage to
cope with increased competition.
The company should continue to invest in innovation and advertising.
By the onset of the establishment phase.
The company's operations are standardized and it has a stable stream of revenues.
The focus falls on implementing sound business practices and on boosting productivity through automation
and outsourcing.
It is likely the founder must delegate some further decision making authority to division, heads and
consultants.
Companies that have done a good job in the growth and establishment phases will probably enter the expansion
stage.
It consists of geographical expansion or expansion across other products or distribution channels.
When going internationally, a new set of topics arises.
You have to wonder if the existing capacity is going to cover the expected demand you will have in the
new markets.
What are the competitive advantages of your products in these markets?
How are you going to deliver your products locally?
Will you have a website in English only?
Are you going to accept online payments?
Of course, having a successful business locally does not mean this will transfer into an immediate
international success.
After a while, a company that has successfully gone through the expansion stage enters maturity, which
is characterized by stable sales and profits.
Growth of revenue slows down, but that doesn't mean these aren't good times for the firm's owners.
The beginning of the maturity phase is when they make the most money.
However, the maturity phase can also be characterised by a strong level of competition, which results
in losing market share.
Many mature companies try to sustain their business by finding new business opportunities and fuelling
the growth of the mature part of their business.
At this stage, shareholders must decide whether to expand the business even further or exit the investment.
An exit typically consists of selling the company to another firm operating in the same industry or
to financial investors.
These are the lifecycle stages a company typically goes through.
Understanding what can be expected in each is a must for anyone who wants to start a business.


=== Marketing: How to set up an effective Marketing Mix - Promotion decisions ===


--- The quest for a competitive advantage - An Introduction ---

One of the most fundamental strategic concepts we will learn in this course is competitive advantage.
A company has a competitive advantage when it provides buyers with comparable value more efficiently
than other firms in its industry, or performs activities at comparable cost but in unique ways creating
more value for its buyers.
Put simply, you either need to produce the cheapest goods or to create more value for your customers.
Be cheap or be different.
This is a great framework that helps corporate strategists ask the right questions when they try to
prepare a plan about how to win against their rivals.
Often, company executives have misconceptions about competitive advantage.
They believe their great products, talented people, large distribution networks and high brand recognition
are a source of competitive advantage.
Well, they can be.
This much is true, but the fact that they are great doesn't automatically translate into a competitive
advantage.
You have a competitive advantage if and only if one of these aspects of your company is better than
that of your competitors.
If clients believe your products are better than the alternatives on the market, then you have a competitive
advantage.
If your employees have a higher know how than the employees of competitive firms, then that could result
in a competitive advantage.
It's the same with brand recognition.
It isn't just necessary for you to have a brand that is highly recognizable.
You need a brand that is more recognizable than the brands of competitor companies.
Then you would have a competitive advantage.
Michael Porter's idea of competitive advantage depicts a company as a chain of activities.
Every business is different.
However, one thing is clear.
If a company wants to succeed in the long run, it needs to excel in at least one of the elements of
the chain of activities.
It has to be better than competitors in at least one aspect and capitalize on such strength.
The chain is very large and companies will try to find their source of competitive advantage in different
places.
Here are a few examples of companies that have found a competitive advantage in different areas of the
value chain.
We already said Saudi Aramco, the Saudi Arabia state company, secures a cheap provision of oil, which
is its raw material.
Given that the company buys the oil at preferential prices regarding its competitors, this is a source
of competitive advantage for Saudi Aramco.
The Italian company Ferrero is world famous for its kinder surprise eggs, the Nutella, liquid chocolate
and Tic TAC Mints.
Customers perceive its products as the best in class and as the most delicious.
Ferrero excels in innovation and in creating great products that customers see as better than the ones
offered by competitors.
This is a source of competitive advantage.
Remember, competitive advantage is about being different and being better.


=== Marketing: How to set up an effective Marketing Mix - A dynamic concept ===


--- The importance of building a sustainable competitive advantage ---

A few years ago, BlackBerry was one of the most popular business phone brands in the world.
It seemed that every executive had one.
Things change, though.
If you walk in an office district today, it will be very difficult to find someone using a BlackBerry.
In just a few years, the famous keyboard, which allowed executives to type e-mails faster, became
obsolete.
We live in a dynamic world where companies may acquire a competitive advantage, but they can also easily
lose it.
Competitive advantages do not last forever in a dynamic environment.
As soon as competitors see a firms performing better, they will take a deep, hard look in their business
and try to understand the reason for its success.
And once they figure it out, they wouldn't hesitate to imitate and level the playing field.
Some competitive advantages are easy to replicate, while others are not.
Companies focused on the long run are interested in building a sustainable competitive advantage, which
will be the cornerstone for their future success, one that will help them win in the next 20 years,
not just today.
And usually the competitive advantages that are durable are of intangible nature.
They're not related to physical assets or resources like cash the company owns.
Actually, they have to do with intellectual property.
A technological breakthrough or a know how that can be legally patented.
Having something of the sort automatically protects the company from competitors eager to copy its innovations.
Know how that can be legally protected?
Is the best example of a durable competitive advantage.
In addition, proven teams like Apple's R&amp;D unit are a great asset a company can build over time.
The people working there are highly specialized and capable of creating some of the finest technology
products in the world.
It's difficult for other companies to replicate this advantage because there are very few world class
specialists like the ones employed by Apple.
And even better, Apple employees are unwilling to leave the company and join one of its competitors,
even though they have been offered higher salaries elsewhere.
They are committed to the company's mission and ideals.
This is a true source of competitive advantage.
Not every company can have that.
Some firms come up with business models that are disruptive for existing business practices and are
very difficult to replicate.
Facebook is a great example of that.
It is the largest social network in the world.
Other companies are not even close to challenging its dominance.
Even an Internet giant like Google was unsuccessful to do it.
Google Plus targeted the very same market with a very similar product.
However, Facebook has more than 1 billion active users where people can find all of their friends.
So having the largest group of users in a winner takes it all market is definitely a sustainable source
of competitive advantage.
Okay, great job so far.
In our next lesson, we'll learn about a company's resources and capabilities.
See you there.


=== Marketing: Marketing strategy in the long run ===


--- The role of resources and capabilities ---

Two factors are essential when a company wants to achieve superior profitability over the long term
industry attractiveness and competitive advantage.
In recent years, the second variable has become more important as internationalization of businesses
has increased competition within most industries.
That's why companies must focus even more on acquiring a competitive advantage within their industry
and on outperforming their peers.
But how are they going to find a competitive advantage?
Is there a framework that would allow us to understand this process easier?
Yes, there is.
And that's what we'll study in the next lessons.
In this video, we'll explain the concepts of resources and capabilities which will help us learn how
to distinguish the resources and capabilities that can help us build a competitive advantage.
Okay, let's do it.
What is the difference between resources and capabilities?
Resources are assets acquired with money, while capabilities are the ability to do something well.
We can distinguish three main types of resources tangible resources.
These can be financial cash and securities and physical property, plant and equipment, intangible
resources such as technologies, patents, copyrights, brand awareness and so on, and human resources
such as the know how motivation and communication abilities of the people working in a company.
Capabilities are a firm's ability to deploy resources towards a certain goal and use them in the best
possible way to achieve the desired result.
We can talk about distinctive competence, also known as core competence.
When we want to describe an activity, the firm does well even better than its competitors.
For example, one of the core competencies of Honda is the development and manufacturing of engines,
while Canon's core competencies are laser control, optics and imaging.
When a company has the resources and matches them with an internal capability to use these resources
in an optimal way, it can develop a core competence which can then be used to obtain a competitive
advantage.
We shouldn't forget to mention core competencies can be found in the different functional lines of a
company marketing, R&amp;D, sales, finance operations and so on.
Here are examples of companies with core competencies in different areas.
Google is strong in strategic innovation, while P&amp;G excels at brand management and GE is famous for
its outstanding management development programs.
The moral of the story is that companies should try to build a resource based strategy that fully exploits
their current capabilities.
A firm strategy probably will succeed if it uses its resources and capabilities to build core competencies
that enable it to compete successfully and take advantage of the opportunities that arise in the company's
industry.


=== Tesla's marketing ===


--- Acquiring an actual competitive advantage ---

Hi and welcome back.
Now that we're familiar with the concepts of resources, capabilities, core capabilities and competitive
advantage, we would like to use this lesson to provide a holistic view of how entrepreneurs and CEOs
can approach the quest for competitive advantage.
First, it is essential, essential that you know what resources you have at your disposal.
Assessing the resources available to you and your competitors correctly will make your strategic judgment
a lot better.
Have you heard the famous Chinese General, Sun Tzu quote, If you know the enemy and know yourself,
you need not fear the result of a hundred battles.
It's the same in business.
A good understanding of what you have at your disposal and how it differs from the resources available
to competitors is critical for successful strategic planning.
Okay.
So step number one for finding your competitive advantage is to identify and assess resources.
However, depending on what business you're in, resources are not all created equal.
World class engineers are much more needed in Apple, one of the leading tech companies in the world.
Then in McDonald's, a fast food chain, the ability to create a competitive advantage is closely related
to a company's ability to recognize the key success factors in its industry and use it to resources
to address them.
For example, if you're selling white goods online and have a superb logistic system, then developing
this resource will probably help you gain a significant cost advantage.
Companies can establish a competitive advantage only when using resources or capabilities not easily
available to all other participants in the industry.
The scarcer a resource, the likelier it would allow you to acquire a sustainable competitive advantage
and one that will last in the long term.
Then we can say step number two in our process is appraisal of resources.
These are the resources related to a critical success factor for the industry in which we operate.
They're not widely available and are difficult to imitate.
Identifying and securing these resources might result in a competitive advantage.
And step number three is to develop these resources, invest in them, but also make sure their potential
is used to the fullest and in sync with the firm's competitive positioning strategy.


=== Accounting: An Introduction ===


--- The three main competitive strategies ---

All right.
Excellent.
So far, we've described an industry and a company's typical life cycle with Michael Porter's five forces
framework that helps us determine industry attractiveness.
In addition, we learned about game theory and described it as one of the tools that can help us predict
competitive outcomes.
Then we discuss the role of resources and capabilities and how companies aim to gain a competitive advantage.
This is great.
In the next few lessons, we will study another major contribution of Michael Porter's his theory about
competitive strategies that can be adopted by the companies in a given industry.
Michael Porter firmly believes every company must choose between one of three fundamental strategies
cost, leadership, differentiation and niche.
Also known as focused strategies.
So in the next several lessons, we'll learn about each of these strategies and how they can be applied
in practice.
It is important to say the best way to set up a business would be to start by deciding which of these
strategies will be pursued.
This is a decision that depends on the resources the company has and other factors.
And once the resources have been assessed and the decision about the strategy to be pursued has been
made, the company must establish a competitive advantage in sync with the strategy chosen.
So if you've opened an e-commerce website selling white goods and you're set on going with cost leadership,
then it would make sense to pursue a competitive advantage related to lower costs, such as a more efficient
logistics network or a lower cost of personnel.
Starting from our next lesson, we'll examine each of the three competitive strategies in detail.
Stay tuned.


=== P&L, Balance Sheet and Cash Flow - The three main financial statements ===


--- Cost leadership - sell cheap ---

The first competitive strategy will examine is cost leadership.
It's straightforward, isn't it?
You have to be able to produce products or provide services that are cheaper than the ones offered by
competitor companies.
If a company adopts this strategy and sustains costs lower than its peers, then it will succeed.
Typically, cost leaders will sell at prices lower than their competitors.
This way they will win price sensitive customers, the ones looking for a bargain.
And given that price sensitive customers are the absolute majority.
Costly to firms can win a sizeable market share and sell a lot of units of the goods they're selling.
However, the drawback is that the low prices of these products mean lower margins for the firm.
A margin is formed by the revenue obtained from the sale of a product, minus the cost sustained to
produce the product.
So a cost to leadership strategy means more units sold and a lower margin per unit.
Selling at prices lower than competitors is a valid idea.
However, companies must find a way to do that.
If you want to sell at a lower price, then you have to do something differently than your peers.
There are several ways to establish a competitive advantage related to lower costs, and they all have
something to do with the concept of efficiency.
A cost leader must be efficient.
Let's see how companies can do that.
One of the fundamental ways to push down unitary costs is to leverage economies of scale.
The more units of a product are sold, the lower is its unitary cost.
The cost of one unit.
How come?
Well, there are several costs which are sustained only once.
These are the so called fixed costs.
There is an inverse relationship between the quantity of output and unitary costs, meaning the higher
the output level, the lower the fixed cost per unit.
The reason is that fixed costs are spread over a large number of products and services.
Economies of scale are the main reason for the predominance of large corporations in most industries.
Let's provide an example with the airline industry, where the cost for development of a new aircraft
is huge.
Boeing seven for seven is one of the most successful aircraft projects because the company built 1415
aircraft.
Concorde turned out to be unprofitable.
Only 20 planes were built.
However, the cost of research and development of the two projects is comparable.
Right?
Both required engineering teams who worked for years to design the two planes.
However, in the first case, the cost was spread among 1415 sales and in the second among 20 planes.
This is a very important concept.
The more units we produce, the lower the unitary cost we have sustained.
Some companies excel at achieving economies of scope if they operate in more than one business.
Certain firms can often achieve economies of scope, which pushes down their costs even lower.
Economies of scope consist in a proportionate saving gained by producing two complementary products.
The cost of producing the two goods under the same roof is lower than the cost of producing them separately.
A good example for an economy of scope is PNG's acquisition of Gillette.
Once placed under the same roof, the two companies could use the same distribution channels and lower
their logistics and sales expenses.
Another good example is the production of milk and cheese.
It makes sense to produce both products under the same roof as the materials required for their production
can easily be combined.
Right.
Another cost driver companies pay attention to is process design.
We can obtain a cost reduction from a change in the way a process is being carried out when less input
is needed for each unit of output provided, a new process may reduce costs dramatically.
The assembly time of Ford's Model T is a great example.
Initially, it took 106 hours to assemble a Model T.
Eventually, when the assembly line was improved and made much more efficient, the time was reduced
to 6 hours.
Remember, for a new process to lead to efficiency, it has to be combined with changes in a company's
organisational structure, product design management, control systems, HR management and so on.
Success stories of companies such as Wal-Mart, McDonald's and Toyota, which matched their process
technologies with their structures, management systems and product design, are proof this is true
for prospective.
GM has been singled out as a company that implemented a new process technology without the management
and organizational changes.
GM did not become the world's most efficient car manufacturer, which was its primary goal.
Moving on, firms with lower input costs can have a significant cost advantage over their rivals.
Lower input costs derive from lower wage rates, access to low cost raw materials, or an ability to
negotiate well with suppliers and to excel at procurement.
In today's globalized economy, several companies have taken advantage of lower cost of personnel in
developing countries.
The outsourcing industry in these countries is flourishing as call centers, customer support centers,
finance and accounting divisions are being transferred to these lower wage countries.
The result from these operations is a lower cost of personnel over the long term and a possible source
of cost advantage.
Cost leadership is a strategy pursued by several large companies Wal-Mart, IKEA, Amazon, McDonald's,
Dell Computers, Zara, H&amp;M and Southwest Airlines are some established names you've heard, and they're
all focusing on costs.
So, for example, IKEA, the Swedish furniture retailer sources its furniture from countries where
the cost of personnel is cheap.
In addition, the company offers a very basic service.
It doesn't assemble or deliver the furniture it sells.
McDonald's uses inexperienced staff instead of trained cooks.
The company hires few managers and saves from cost of personnel.
Southwest Airlines minimizes the time its planes spend at airports by organizing more flights and filling
up its schedule.
Acquiring a cost advantage is definitely one way to go.
However, it's difficult in today's highly competitive markets.
Companies that choose this path should focus on efficiency in all its forms and in a continuous redesign
of their operating processes.


=== Revenue and cost recognition principles - This is how we register financials ===


--- Differentiation - be different ---

According to Porter's framework, the alternative to concentrating on costs is focusing on quality,
which is the differentiation strategy.
Companies choosing this path must be different from their competitors, but not only do they need to
be different, they also need to be better.
Customers should perceive their products as superior to the others offered at the marketplace.
Otherwise they wouldn't buy them right.
They would simply go for the cheapest option and acquire the product offered at the lowest price.
Fortunately, people are not just interested in prices but also in quality.
So companies good at creating amazing products can charge higher prices for them.
Apple is a classic example of a firm that has adopted a differentiation strategy.
Apple's products cost more than those offered by other brands.
However, customers love them because of their design, integrated operating system applications and
brand.
There are two main types of differentiation product differentiation and brand differentiation.
Product differentiation consists in creating a better product than what is offered by competitors.
This is not an easy task in any industry.
There are only a handful of companies that can attract top specialists in their fields and produce the
best products.
In recent years, differentiation has gone far beyond the physical characteristics of the product or
service, and now it is reflected in everything from interactions with customers to the product value
they receive.
Even extra attention and superb customer service can be perceived as elements of differentiation by
customers.
Put briefly, product differentiation is achieved when people prefer your products because they find
them better than the ones offered by competitors.
Brand differentiation is the art of creating an image symbols and an overall positioning that reflect
the way the company wants to be seen by its customers.
The idea is to position the brand image differently than competitors and to convey a certain set of
values which should inspire customers and have them create an affiliation with the brand.
Nike has done an outstanding job in differentiating their brand.
They celebrate superb athletic performance by signing endorsement contracts with the biggest stars in
the world of sports.
This creates an immediate affection in the eyes of customers.
They perceive the brand as the natural choice of superstars and are happy to buy Nike's products.
If a company wants to succeed in the long term, it needs both types of differentiation.
Product differentiation is a great start, but brand differentiation commands customer loyalty and higher
sales volumes.
If a company cannot differentiate its product, then it will have a hard time keeping customers, even
if it is great at brand differentiation and communication.
You need both great products and a strong brand.


=== Accounting: The fundamental Accounting principles you will need in business ===


--- Niche (Focus) strategy - find your niche market ---

A focus or a niche strategy is the third competitive strategy companies can pursue, according to Michael
Porter.
It consists of targeting a specific market segment and creating specialized products tailored towards
the needs of the people in that segment.
The advantage of companies that have chosen this strategy is they specialize in understanding the needs
of a specific group of people under better prepare to create the products these customers desire.
Often clients notice these efforts, and niche companies can establish strong brand loyalty and affiliation,
which strengthens their position within the selected segment.
Even more niche strategies are usually employed by startups or small companies aiming to avoid the competition
of bigger players.
And that makes perfect sense, right?
They have less bargaining power when negotiating with suppliers and due to low volume purchases and
have a disadvantage in terms of costs and marketing investments to compensate for that.
Small firms apply a focused strategy.
Focus strategy differs from differentiation, and we need to know how the distinguishing happens.
With a focus strategy, companies aim to offer a differentiated product within the market segment they've
chosen while going for differentiation means they aim to create the best possible product.
Swatch is a great example of a company that offers differentiated products within a niche.
Their target group is individuals between 15 and 34 years of age who prefer creative, fashionable and
innovative accessories and enjoy an active lifestyle.
These individuals cannot spend $5,000 for a Rolex and prefer several good looking watches.
They can change frequently.
Swatch has served this market segment by offering watches that typically cost between 100 and $250.
Repetitive sales make the segment much more appealing than it seems.
This is a great example of a niche differentiation strategy.
Swatch offers watches of good quality that look good and are still not too expensive.
Sometimes a focus strategy applied with cost leadership can succeed.
Such is the case of Southwest Airlines, the fifth largest carrier in the U.S..
The company specializes in short haul point to point flights in combination with low fare services.
Southwest aims to make flying affordable for everyone, providing a cost effective service.
They can do that because they are good at minimizing the time their planes stay on the ground.
This strategy turned out to be successful for the carrier.
It has been profitable in an industry where many other airline carriers have been struggling.
The successful business model includes efficient operations, motivated employees, a focus on customer
experience and low cost pricing.
One of the disadvantages of focus strategies is that consumer preferences in the targeted group or segment
could change with time, which would incur additional costs for focus firms trying to adapt their offering
to the new environment.
Another disadvantage is the higher bargaining power of suppliers because of the lower volumes typically
purchased by smaller, niche oriented companies.
One of the main advantages of focus companies is they develop very specific core competencies, which
makes entry barriers to the selected industry segment high and enables them to charge higher prices.
In addition, the bargaining power of buyers is lower because of the few specialized alternatives on
the market.


=== A complete Case Study from scratch - Bookkeeping, P&L, and Balance Sheet ===


--- The danger of hybrid strategies ---

Steve Jobs, one of the most successful entrepreneurs we've ever seen, is famous for saying, do not
try to do everything.
Do one thing well.
Recently, Sheryl Sandberg, Facebook's chief operating officer, said, We're focused on doing one
thing incredibly well.
If you look at other companies, all of these companies are doing a lot of different things.
But we're still, as we grow, doing exactly one thing.
Both are world class businessmen, and they value highly of firms ability to specialize in its business,
to decide on a strategy and pursue it consistently.
Cost leaders should be focused on prices, while companies selling differentiated products should build
a strategy around their products and brands.
It can be dangerous if companies pursue a mix of strategies, creating a differentiated product and
aiming at cost leadership at the same time, or trying to offer multiple products, some differentiated
and some cost efficient, according to Michael Porter.
If the company decides to pursue a sustainable, competitive advantage by employing a mix of all three
strategies, it is highly likely the company wouldn't be able to acquire an advantage.
Adding cheap and differentiated products in the same product portfolio is problematic from the point
of view of customers.
They already have too much information around them.
There are hundreds of producers offering their goods and selling them online on the street, at work,
and even in their homes.
So customers use shortcuts, they associate prices and brand reputation with the quality of a product.
So a great product sold at a cheap price leaves them confused.
Michael Porter argues the one option for companies that want to use hybrid strategies a mix between
a cost leadership, a differentiation and a niche strategy would be to create separate business units
and brands to carry out each of these strategies.
This would allow the implementation of different policies and will create a different culture depending
on the strategy of each unit.
This kind of solves the problem from a customer's point of view.
They wouldn't be worried if a different brand sells cheaper products than the one they're used to purchasing.
Fashion House Armani has tried to do that with its cheaper brand aimed at junior clientele Armani jeans.
By creating a separate brand.
Armani is indicating to customers these are products aimed at a niche target group.
The take home lesson from this lesson is it is much better to focus on a single goal and to do your
best to achieve it, as opposed to spending your energy to pursue many directions.
That's one of the most valuable pieces of advice in business.
People who try to do many things risk losing their focus.


=== Accounting: Understanding how to calculate cash flow ===


--- The types of growth opportunities companies pursue ---

All right.
Excellent.
Do you remember in the very beginning in lesson number two, we learned there are two important dimensions
of strategy, corporate strategy and business strategy.
Yeah.
Great.
So far, we've been mostly focusing on business strategy, the way a firm competes in an industry to
gain a competitive advantage over its rivals.
Now it's time to take a deeper look at some interesting concepts related to companies corporate strategy.
If you recall, corporate strategy is about decisions that determine a firm's geographic scope, the
markets it is present in and the way it intends to grow.
Now we'll learn about concepts like outsourcing, diversification, M&amp;A and vertical and horizontal
integration.
Sounds great, right?
Let's dive straight in.
Growth is a very important topic in business.
It is considered one of the two fundamental drivers of value for a firm shareholders.
The other one is profitability and all else being equal.
The faster a company grows, the better right?
More opportunities arise at each stage of its growth.
The bargaining power with suppliers and distributors improves significantly.
The company can produce more units and lower unitary costs through economies of scale.
Talented employees would be interested to join a large and prestigious corporation.
There are several strategic advantages for a company that's growing fast.
So naturally, this is one of the most important topics for managers.
Okay, which are the two main growth strategies we can have?
We can either grow sales by expanding our existing business, in which case we say we are growing organically,
or we can acquire another company and merge its operations in our company.
This is an example of inorganic growth.
Both approaches are valid and applied in practice.
In the next few lessons, we'll talk about them in more depth.


=== Accounting: The importance of timing when registering financials ===


--- Organic growth - building a solid foundation ---

Usually businesses start as a small venture, primarily driven by the efforts of a single entrepreneur
and a few employees.
Financing is limited, and the company pays significant attention to costs.
The first few months of activity can be critical because the firm prepares its first products and has
no revenues.
Then once it takes a product to market, the company earns revenue and expands its activities by hiring
new personnel and improving its sales efforts.
As soon as production and sales reach a certain level, the company will be profitable because of an
improved cost structure and higher sales volumes and can invest in marketing.
This would allow it to grow its brand presence and reach a larger customer base.
This is how businesses grow organically, a type of growth that is the fruit of internal efforts.
When Mark Zuckerberg started Facebook, it was just him and a few college classmates.
They received financing and created their own firm.
The website they created continued to grow in popularity and its user base expanded fast.
This attracted the attention of more investors who were happy to provide the money that allowed the
company to hire more personnel and continue to grow its existing business.
This is what organic growth is about.
Grow the business from the inside instead of growing it through acquiring an existing business.
Organic growth can be slower.
It requires time to grow a business, reach new markets, create inexistent products and access distribution
channels.
However, usually it's also the safer bet.
If a business reaches a certain size organically, it probably has the management and organizational
structure that would allow it to handle growth and get the most out of it.
There are several ways to grow organically.
Companies could grow by selling more of an existing product to an existing market, develop a new product
and sell it to an existing market.
Promote an existing product in new markets, or diversify their business by selling a new product in
a new market.
When a company wants to sell more of an existing product in an existing market, we have two possible
situations.
If the market is in its growth phase, then the company will have a much easier task because the whole
market is growing and it would be sufficient to be part of it and perform as well as the other companies
in that market.
If the market is mature and is not growing, then the company must win market share from its competitors.
That can be achieved through superb marketing efforts, product innovation and niche targeting.
A great example of a company that does that successfully is Zara, the clothing retailer, which grew
its market share in a mature market, superb product and distribution.
Innovation with an excellent marketing strategy, allowed Zara to grow in an environment with plenty
of competitors.
When a company can identify unsatisfied existing consumer needs, it can grow by launching a new product
in an existing market car to go.
Daimler's urban car sharing service is a great example of that.
The automobile transportation market exists and is a mature market.
Car To-go offers an innovative solution to people living and working in urban centers who do not need
a car throughout the day.
But only sometimes it is expensive and difficult to park a car in city centers.
However, if many people use one, this issue can be solved.
Daimler grew its revenues by offering a new product in an existing market.
The other option for companies that want to grow is to offer their existing products or services in
new territories.
Domino's Pizza is a great example.
The company can grow fast because it enters new countries and applies its existing business model,
which needs zero or little adaptation.
The same menu with the same pizzas offered everywhere.
By doing this, companies can expand their client base and realize several synergies.
And finally, another way to grow would be to offer a new product in a new market.
When a company does that, we talk about diversification.
Examples of famous diversified companies are General Electric and Siemens.
GE runs two broad divisions industrial and financial.
The industrial division includes oil and gas, power and water, transportation, health care, aviation
appliances, lighting and energy businesses.
The financial services arm of the company comprises commercial loans, leases and middle market finance
solutions.
The advantage of a diversified business is that the holding company would be protected if one of its
businesses slows down or its industry enters a decline phase.
However, the cost to pay is organizational complexity and lower transparency for shareholders and investors.
As we learned in this lesson, there are various ways to grow a business from the inside.
In the next few lessons, we'll learn how a business can grow inorganically.
Thanks for watching.


=== Accounting: An introduction to Financial Reporting ===


--- Inorganic growth - leveraging M&A transactions ---

In the previous lesson, we saw how companies can grow organically through their own efforts as opposed
to this approach.
Inorganic growth consists of expanding a business through an M&amp;A deal.
M&amp;A stands for mergers and acquisitions.
These transactions provide a faster way for companies to grow rather than start from the foundations
and build their businesses organically.
By acquiring an existing business, companies gain immediate access to its customer base, distribution
channels, production capacity and know how.
We talk about an acquisition.
When a company buys another company shares our assets and we have a merger.
When the buying company absorbs the target company, the target company ceases to exist after the transaction
as it is merged into the buying company.
In every M&amp;A process, there are at least two parties.
One of the companies is called the buyer or the buying company, and the other one is called the target,
which is the firm to be acquired.
The buyer company can offer a compensation to the target company's shareholders in several ways.
They can offer a cash compensation, a stock package of the new entity or a combination of both.
The technical name of the amount paid is called consideration.
There are several reasons M&amp;A deals play an important role in a company's life.
Top managers understand sometimes it is cheaper to acquire something already created than to generate
it internally.
Or the businesses are so complementary that their combination can unlock a great deal of savings, efficiencies
and opportunities.
There are two main ways to grow through an M&amp;A process expand vertically or expand horizontally, also
known as vertical and horizontal integration.
In the next two lessons, we will describe both and will explain why it could be beneficial for a company
to grow vertically or horizontally.


=== Accounting: Financial statement analysis in practice ===


--- Horizontal integration ---

Hi.
In the last few lessons we've been talking about the different growth strategies.
We said a firm could grow organically and or inorganically.
Now that we know about the different organic growth strategies, we will learn more about inorganic
growth strategies.
Horizontal integration is one way to approach inorganic growth.
It consists of acquiring or merging with another company that operates in the same industry as ours
and performs the same activity we do.
Basically, this is the purchase of a competitor company.
Why would a firm be interested in purchasing one of its competitors?
Well, there can be plenty of reasons.
At certain points in time, it makes sense to acquire competitors or merge with them.
A large company can be interested in acquiring a small company because of its growth potential and because
it has developed a great product growing at a faster pace than the industry is growing.
A few years ago, Facebook acquired Instagram because the mobile application service had become a competitor
of its services.
Facebook was worried Instagram had a better positioning among younger audiences and decided to acquire
it for $1 billion by doing so.
The company merged Instagram into its existing structure and secured the ownership of the photo sharing
application with the fastest growth in the social network industry.
Sometimes large companies merge with other large companies because it makes sense from a strategic perspective.
When Disney acquired George Lucas's studio, Lucasfilm, which owns the rights to the entire Star Wars
franchise, it acquired a company from its industry.
The $4 billion acquisition produced the following effect Less competition for Disney.
It doesn't have to put its movies against Star Wars movies because now it owns them.
An extended product offering Disney can offer Star Wars merchandise in its own merchandise shops and
Star Wars characters in its theme parks.
And finally, Lucasfilm would have access to Disney's world class team of specialists who would help
create the next generation of Star Wars movies.
The synergies between the two businesses are so many.
It is believed Disney broke even as soon as it released the first two Star Wars movies, The Force Awakens
and Rogue One.
Pretty great, right?
The main idea of horizontal integration is to gain market share and to expand into new markets by saving
the high expense of starting a business from scratch.
That's it, plain and simple.
In our next lesson, we'll talk about vertical integration and the way it can help a firm's business.
This will do for now.
Thanks for watching.


=== Negotiation: An Introduction ===


--- Vertical integration ---

In the previous lesson, we describe the process of horizontal integration, the combination of two
companies operating in the same industry and performing the same activity.
It's what happens when a retailer acquires a retailer, a wholesaler buys another wholesaler, or when
a producer merges with another producer.
In this lesson, we'll focus on a different type of integration called vertical integration.
This is when a company expands the activities it performs across the supply chain.
Understanding new aspects of the business.
Think of the domestic appliances industry.
The suppliers in this industry provide raw materials like iron and aluminum and components such as relays,
electronic displays and so on to the companies in the industry.
Very rarely the companies produce these on their own.
The actual companies that produce domestic appliances, such as Haier and Whirlpool invest in R&amp;D and
have the know how that allows them to create these products and justifies investing heavily in production
plans.
Once the products have been produced, typically a wholesaler or a mass market retailer takes them over
to do that.
Logistics firms must be hired for the physical delivery of the products.
A wholesaler would then send the goods to a retail reseller.
These are the smaller shops or chains selling domestic appliances.
Their customers can buy the goods and take them home.
Usually they have a warranty and can return the products they just bought if a problem occurs within
one or two years after the purchase.
Frequently, producers do not carry out repairs themselves, but instead hire a specialized local service
operator that can take care of onsite repairs at a lower cost.
This is a good example that shows us several roles exist within an industry.
We can have firms that are suppliers, producers, wholesalers, retailers and firms offering customer
support services.
Great.
So whenever a company decides it wants to carry out more than one of these activities, we can talk
about vertical integration.
Vertical integration allows the firm to play a larger role in terms of value delivered to final customers.
Why would a company want to do that?
Well, several advantages come with vertical integration.
Let's use Apple as an example again.
First, if Apple produces its own semiconductor chips or at least oversees the process, then it can
be certain the quality of these chips will match the intended quality of the entire product.
Often, technology companies integrate vertically by producing the components required for their products.
Another link in the vertical chain Apple owns is a chain of Apple stores.
By doing this, the company insures the premium image of its products will be accompanied by a premium
environment with competent personnel specifically trained to sell these products and explain their benefits
to customers.
So as you can see, vertical integration gives you more control and makes you less dependent on suppliers
and resellers.
It also improves the company's bargaining power regarding resellers and suppliers if it continues to
work with them.
So Apple has a stronger bargaining power when negotiating with its premium resellers because it sells
its own products in Apple stores.
If the company reaches no agreement with resellers, it can always open a new Apple store and sell its
products there.
That's a credible threat when negotiating, and resellers understand that.
To be honest, vertical integration isn't the most popular strategy nowadays.
It takes a great company to apply it.
Firms like Apple and Tesla have succeeded with vertical integration, but the number of companies applying
this strategy unsuccessfully is greater than the ones who succeeded it.
Some drawbacks of vertical integration are increased organizational complexity, investment of valuable
resources in non-core competencies and activities, and a more rigid cost structure.
Entrepreneurs are oriented towards a leaner structure that allows them to be flexible in the long term.
This doesn't mean vertical integration isn't an effective strategy.
It means it is difficult to apply and should only be implemented when required.
For instance, when industry forces put too much pressure on a company and it wants to improve its bargaining
power.


=== Negotiation: The negotiation toolbox ===


--- An introduction to SWOT analysis ---

Hi and welcome back.
We know strategy analysis has two main branches analysis of a firm's external environment and analysis
of a firm's internal environment.
In our previous lessons, we learned how to analyze the internal and external environment of a firm.
SWOT is a famous framework that allows us to combine the two types of analysis.
SWOT is sometimes referred to as an internal external analysis.
The acronym SWOT stands for Strengths, Weaknesses, Opportunities and Threats.
The first two strengths and weaknesses are related to a firm's internal environment.
While the last two opportunities and threats consider its external environment, we can use a SWOT analysis
when analysing industries, companies, products, new initiatives, policies or even people.
It is a simple and useful framework that allows us to group and visualise strengths and weaknesses and
to identify potential threats and opportunities that exist in the external environment.
In addition, the SWOT framework could be useful when you want to understand the areas in which your
firm performs well and the ones that need improvement.
The four elements of SWOT analysis are usually placed in a table which looks something like this.
Internal Strengths and external opportunities.
A vertically paired is helpful elements, while internal weaknesses and external threats are paired
as harmful elements.
Right.
So if we perform a company analysis under strengths, we would expect to see its core competencies,
the areas where the business excels and has a competitive advantage over competitors.
An example of a firm's strength could be its strong brand recognition.
Weaknesses are areas that need improvement.
Such vulnerabilities place a company at a disadvantage when competing against other firms.
Usually the disadvantage results from other companies having a competitive advantage.
An example of a weakness is higher cost structure.
Opportunities can be seen as favorable factors existing in a company's external environment and the
industry where it operates and have the potential to improve its current result and competitive positioning.
Taking advantage of opportunities depends on how skilled a company's top management team is to take
advantage of an opportunity.
The management must be able to recognize opportunities when they arise and then act accordingly when
the time is right.
An example of an opportunity is the advent of a new technology the company can exploit to expand its
product offering.
The last of the four elements of SWOT analysis is threats.
Threats arise in a company's external environment and might harm its current business.
But if you can identify a threat, you can lessen its impact by taking precautions.
An example of a threat is the entry of a strong competitor with access to advanced technology.
This is a great overview of the SWOT framework.
In our next lesson will conclude the strategy module of the course by applying SWOT in practice.
Thanks for watching.


=== Negotiation: The importance of preparation ===


--- SWOT analysis in practice - Starbucks ---

Okay, great.
So in this lesson our goal would be to apply the Swot framework in practice.
Shouldn't be that difficult right.
The company will examine is Starbucks, the well known US coffee company and coffeehouse chain.
It was founded in Seattle in 1971 and operates in several countries around the world.
Let's start with the company's strengths.
First, almost everyone knows its brand, right?
So it definitely has a strong brand recognition.
Starbucks has been proclaimed the most recognized coffee brand in the world.
Quite an advantage regarding other companies in the industry.
The firm's brand is one offering of a differentiated product to customers, which is coherent with its
strategy and pricing.
Clients have expressed they love the atmosphere in Starbucks coffee houses.
They can use free Wi-Fi and enjoy the coffee drinking experience to the fullest.
The company owns thousands of outlets around the world, and its clients can find it even when traveling
overseas.
That's a strong advantage as it ensures loyal clients can remain such, even overseas.
And finally, Starbucks supply chain management ensures the quality of coffee served to customers is
excellent.
The company chooses its suppliers carefully, which improves the quality of its products and ensures
clients receive a super coffee experience.
This is another choice in sync with its differentiation strategy.
Okay.
Very good.
Let's take off our rosy glasses and consider some of the firm's weaknesses.
One could argue Starbucks offers the same product in all countries where it operates.
It does not customize it according to the different tastes of people in different geographies.
For some customers, this is an issue, and they prefer competitor products tailored to their specific
geographical preferences.
In addition, Starbucks has a premium price policy, which is not in line with most customers expectations.
Not all countries have enough middle and high income class individuals who can afford to pay extra for
their coffee.
Weakness number three would be the high employee turnover that the chain's coffee shops have.
It is difficult to serve coffee as a premium product when the company's baristas have been on the job
for only a couple of weeks.
In terms of opportunities, we can say the following.
Starbucks can access new countries easily.
It simply has to apply its existing business model in these new geographies.
Emerging markets can be a great source of growth for the company.
Starbucks has also entered agreements within the entertainment industry and partnered with movie producing
companies.
That can be a great opportunity for the firm to.
One of the biggest threats for Starbucks is the saturation of the American market, which is where most
of its outlets are located.
Consumers are increasingly invested in healthier lifestyle, and some substitute products are better
positioned to capture the attention of the health conscious clientele.
Okay, we'll stop here.
This is what a Swot analysis looks like in practice.
The depth of the observations included in the analysis depends on the information we have about the
company.
I'm sure an industry practitioner would be capable to prepare a much deeper analysis.
However, the important point is you now know how to perform a Swot analysis on your own and you can
distinguish the important questions from the rest.
When it comes to analysing a company, Swot analysis is a great tool that allows us to visualise and
concentrate on some of the most important topics when analysing a company.
This will do for now.
Thanks for watching.

=== Negotiation subtleties that will help you in the long run ===


--- Tesla's mission and vision statements ---

Okay.

This is a much broader mission statement and encompasses the production of transport vehicles, Tesla
solar roofs, energy storage, electric energy and most recently, Tesla homes.
The common denominator is sustainability, and this is one of the main reasons why people are so excited
about the company's products.
Tesla is not perceived as a giant corporation that puts profit above all else.
Its mission is to help humanity thrive and save planet Earth in the process.
This gives the company's business a solid foundation.
It allows Tesla to recruit some of the best engineers in the world who desire to work for a company
that is making a real difference.
And of course, it helps Tesla win customers who are sensitive to environmental issues.
Tesla's vision statement goes like this to create the most compelling car of the 21st century by driving
the world's transition to electric vehicles.
Every vision statement needs to be specific and measurable.
This is a perfect example, and Tesla aims high with it, committing to become the most compelling car
company of the 21st century.
The firm envisions a future in which it becomes a leader in the electric vehicles market.
Well, in all fairness, it is already a leader in that market, as Elon Musk tweeted.
Tesla was first, second and third in EV sales for August 2018.
However, things are expected to change when traditional car companies, which have so far been focusing
on fossil fuels, become more and more involved in the electric vehicles market.
Think of it like this.
In 2018, the global automobile industry expects to sell 90 million new vehicles.
Tesla sold a little above 100,000.
It dominated the electric vehicles market, but the established firms in the global auto market will
soon enter the scene.
This makes Tesla's vision to be the most compelling car company of the 21st century that much bolder.
