=== Negotiation: A complete Negotiation case study ===


--- Industry analysis - The electric vehicles market ---

The automotive industry is in its mature face.
More than 90 million new vehicles are produced each year.
The numbers are even higher when we take into consideration commercial clients ordering pickups, vans,
buses and trucks.
Of course, this is a market dominated by global players with significant economies of scale and multi-billion
marketing investments made each year.
So how does Tesla hope to penetrate a mature market and become a true global player?
Well, it doesn't.
Or at least the company is not doing it directly.
Tesla is the first major auto producer with complete focus on electric vehicles.
There are two possible interpretations.
One is to say that Tesla created a niche in the automobile industry market, which is certainly true.
Or alternatively, we can consider that the market for electric vehicles is an industry of its own.
Both points of view have their own merit.
I personally prefer the second one.
So if we say that the electric vehicles market is an industry of its own, which would be its current
stage of development according to you.
Introduction.
Growth or maturity?
Let's look at it like this.
The introduction stage consists in technological stabilization and going through multiple product iterations
to come up with a product version that is ready to be sold in mass production.
Back in 2003, when Tesla was founded, the industry was clearly in its introduction phase.
There were mostly low speed, tiny electric vehicles around the world, but none of them could be compared
to fossil fuel cars.
Then in 2008, Tesla released their first product, the Roadster Sports Car.
The firm strategy with the Roadster was to build an electric car by cheaply converting Lotus Elise cases.
Later on, Elon Musk claimed that this was a very wrong.
His exact word was stupid strategy based on two false premises.
First, it wasn't cheap to convert the Lotus Elise and use that as a car platform.
And second, at the time, Tesla assumed they were going to be able to use technology developed by a
company called AC Propulsion for the Electric Drive Train and the Battery.
The problem was that the AC propulsion technology failed during production and then the lotus chassis
were invalidated by the heavier weight of electric cars.
After adding batteries and electric motors, everything had to be redone.
According to Elon Musk, the final version of the Tesla Roadster had less than 7% of parts in common
with any other existing device at the time, which means that all these parts were very expensive as
they were not produced at scale.
These are precisely the types of product iterations that take place in the introduction phase of an
industry, when the first mover player tries to come up with a stable version of the product.
Later on, however, Tesla managed to introduce car models like Model S, Model X and Model three,
delivering more than 100,000 vehicles of Model S and Model X in 2017.
And we can now assume that the electric car industry is entering its growth stage of development.
The price of electric cars is still high, as there are many customers who are price insensitive, and
currently there are only a few good products offered on the market.
This, of course, attracts interest from traditional car producers.
Some of them, like BMW and Volkswagen, announced they will focus on electric vehicles and will introduce
their first models in 2018 and 2019.
Sales are expected to grow really fast over the next 5 to 10 years in the electric vehicle industry.
Of course, Tesla's job is to claim a bigger portion of the pie and become profitable, especially if
they would like to become the most compelling auto producer in the 21st century.
Competition will be tough, but Tesla has several competitive advantages, given that it is a first
mover in the electric vehicles market.
It will certainly need such advantages.
As Elon Musk pointed out very recently, the only previous US car producer startup company that did
not fail was founded 100 years before Tesla.
The Ford Motor Company.
In the history of American car companies, there are only two firms that have not gone bankrupt.
That's Ford and Tesla.


=== Influence: The power of persuasion ===


--- More than an auto producer ---

The noise and excitement around EVs could easily distract someone from the fact that Tesla isn't just
an automotive company anymore.
It became an all around, sustainable energy firm when SolarCity merged with Tesla in 2016, adding
several additional product lines to its offering.
The slogan of Tesla energy is Power Everything.
Here's how the company envisions this happening.
Tesla offers two main products solar panels and roofs to convert sunlight into energy and Tesla power
walls to store the energy.
All of this will happen in a Tesla home, which will have 24 seven access to backup power.
Although the idea is to use sunlight as the primary source of energy besides home products, Tesla is
one of the most innovative companies when it comes to huge power storage installations.
I am sure most of you have heard of the company's involvement in Australia and Puerto Rico during recent
electricity blackouts.
Such installations are critical for the large scale use of renewable energy.
Tesla builds these huge batteries to store energy generated by solar panels, wind farms or other renewable
sources in Australia.
The world's largest lithium ion battery installed by Tesla over an area the size of a football field
at a wind farm, is saving consumers millions of dollars by making the grid more reliable.
The battery product line has a huge potential and could very well surpass the Automobiles division in
a favorable future scenario as the world is about to transition to renewable energy.
Tesla has ramped up critical knowhow in an industry that is still in its infancy.
Costs of battery storage keep falling, and at a certain point in time, this would allow for massive
growth in revenue.
As the technology becomes more and more accessible, energy storage and generation are key pieces of
the puzzle to achieve sustainability.
The way Tesla sees the future is to have homes that are equipped with solar panels and solar roofs.
Batteries store the energy that has been generated during the day and allow homeowners to use it at
night.
And of course, homeowners will also be able to charge their electric vehicles using solar energy.
This is a truly integrated vision, keeping the big picture in mind.
Of course, given that this is still an emerging industry, we cannot expect strong financial results
from it for the next several years.
Given that plenty of investments are necessary, nevertheless, this is a business line with tremendous
potential because Tesla is a first mover and an innovator in this market as well.

--- Porter's five forces analysis for Tesla ---

Porter's five forces analysis is an invaluable framework we can use when we would like to assess a company's
positioning within an industry.
In other words, it allows us to examine the attractiveness of a business.
Here we will consider Tesla's automotive division.
The five forces are bargaining power of suppliers, bargaining power of buyers, threat of substitute
products, threat of new entrants, existing competition.
Okay, perfect.
Let's analyze each of these forces by bearing in mind what we said previously, the market for electric
vehicles is an industry entering its growth stage.
We'll start with the bargaining power of suppliers.
Tesla is a vertically integrated producer.
Most production processes are carried out in-house, which means that they rely on suppliers for components
rather than buying ready made technologies and products.
This allows the company to be more independent in its processes and improve its bargaining power.
Suppliers of components are relatively easy to substitute.
A complete technology outsourced externally can be much more problematic.
Furthermore, Tesla's bargaining power with respect to suppliers is expected to improve even further
when production scales significantly and the company is able to place large orders when negotiating
for Model S and Model X components, the company had one significant disadvantage.
Its short track record, combined with low production volumes, were not that compelling to suppliers.
Things are about to change now.
Model Three's production target of 5000 per week was achieved in the middle of 2018, so suppliers will
be increasingly interested in doing business with Tesla and improving conditions offered from 2018 onwards.
Moreover, what is very interesting is that someone on Tesla's team, likely his name starts with E
and ends with HN suggested suppliers should return money back.
Tesla requested suppliers to return a significant amount of the payments they have received since 2016.
This is unusual.
As in 99% of cases, companies ask for price reductions going forward, not retroactively.
There are two possible interpretations.
Tesla is desperate for cash, which is a bit too obvious, according to me.
Or alternatively, the company feels that it is in a strong position to ask suppliers for such a gesture.
From Tesla's perspective, when suppliers offered them prices for Model three components, they did
that based on Model S and Model X production figures, which were surpassed significantly.
So to sum up, Tesla has a pretty strong bargaining power when negotiating with suppliers now that it
has scaled and will continue to increase its production.
Bargaining power of buyers in the automobile business to consumer markets.
Tesla sells its products to hundreds of thousands of people.
Therefore, it is safe to say that customers cannot really negotiate prices as they do not have enough
bargaining power.
However, it is also true that there are many firms operating in the market.
Depending on the type of car being sold, Tesla can either charge a premium for vehicles such as Model
S, Model X and Roadster or has to offer a very competitive price like when it tries to win a mass audience
with the Model three in a couple of years, Tesla will enter the business to business market.
Things are a bit different there.
In 2017, Pepsi pre-ordered 100 semi trucks.
This was Pepsi's biggest public preorder to date, so it is reasonable to expect that when such large
orders are made, Tesla and Pepsi representatives negotiated prices a bit.
However, the bargaining price of customers in the B2B market is not too strong either.
It allows corporate clients to obtain a few percentage points of discount from list prices.
OC Next on our agenda substitute products.
The main alternative to electric vehicles.
Vehicles using fossil fuel, right?
Correct.
The growth of the electric automobiles market largely depends on the speed of transition from fossil
fuel cars.
Besides their environmental benefits, EVs have significantly cheaper maintenance costs but also lack
significant key infrastructure such as.
Charging stations and mechanics.
Also right now, electric cars, including Tesla's vehicles, tend to be sold at a premium over fossil
fuel cars, even when incentives are considered.
This will probably change in a few years down the road when electric car producers manage to achieve
significant economies of scale.
A very interesting development in the automotive industry are ride and car sharing services becoming
more and more popular in big cities over the last few years?
Firms like Uber and Lyft redefined car ownership in metropolitan areas.
Many people prefer using an application and simply ordering a ride compared to owning a vehicle.
A person using ridesharing does not have to cover the cost of buying a car.
It's maintenance, parking and other related expenses.
In a similar way car sharing services like car to go allow people to do hourly car rentals.
It is reasonable to expect that the uprise of shared car services will likely transform a portion of
the existing automobile sales market.
At this point, the entrance of new players in the electric car market seems imminent.
Almost all auto producers have announced their plans to release electric versions of existing portfolios.
This is a significant threat.
It is also true, however, that most of these firms are behind on the learning curve.
It is yet to be seen how they'll adapt their vehicles to the different weight acceleration and most
importantly, electric battery systems.
Tesla's experience with lotus chassis could be a very interesting case study, as there are a great
number of difficulties faced when trying to convert a regular car into an electric vehicle.
Nevertheless, we can conclude that competition in the industry is expected to intensify in the near
future.
The rapid growth of the market will likely protect Tesla in the mid-term, but at some point it will
have to find a way to compete with established producers like BMW, Volkswagen and Toyota.
At the very center of the Five Forces Framework is the level of competition in the industry at present.
We already mentioned that there aren't that many companies competing in the electric vehicles market
right now besides Tesla.
Some of the producers who have released their electric cars are Kia, General Motors, Mitsubishi,
Toyota, Renault's and BMW.
But the fact that tens of thousands of people are willing to wait for a Tesla model three for a year
and deposit 1000 or more tells us that competition is not that fierce at the moment.
So in summary, we can say that the electric vehicles industry looks precisely like an industry at the
beginning of its growth phase.
Tesla is a first mover and does not face significant competition right now.
They have solid know how sufficient bargaining power with both suppliers and clients and are not that
threatened by substitute products.
The threat of new entrants is much more real and is something the company will have to deal with.
Porter's five forces analysis indicates that Tesla is a leader and first mover in an industry that is
about to experience significant growth.
This is a good starting position, but does not guarantee they will manage to win the race with traditional
auto producers in the long run.
Thanks for watching.

--- The company life cycle model ---

The company life cycle model is a lot like the industry life cycle model.
Its major premise, however, is that a company's stage of development does not always coincide with
the stage of development of the industry where the company operates.
Therefore, it makes sense to consider the entity's stage of development and see how it fits with the
context in which it operates.
Both the electric vehicles and sustainable energy industries are at the beginning of their growth phase.
Tesla, on the other hand, is a 15 year old company whose sales exceeded $11 billion in 2017 and are
on route to reach $18 Billion in 2018.
The company was valued at more than $65 billion in different moments of 2017 and 2018, and it has more
than 37,000 full time employees.
So we can safely say that Tesla isn't a startup firm anymore.
But where is it in its company life cycle journey?
Well, it has to be in the growth phase, right?
Please keep in mind the following.
Heavy investments have been made so far.
Cash flow has been negative for quite some time.
And this is why we cannot say that Tesla is mature.
One of the necessary prerequisites for mature companies is positive cash flow.
Nevertheless, the company appears to be well equipped to take advantage of the expected growth of the
industries where it operates.
It has the necessary dimension know how and volume of business to compete successfully.
Some frameworks divide the growth phase in two young growth and high growth.
If we do that, we can definitely say that the electric cars industry is still in the first phase.
Young growth Tesla, on the other hand, is ahead of the curve as a company and is about to experience
its high growth.
This phase involves becoming profitable and cash flow positively, providing fuel for future reinvestments
and additional growth.
In our next lesson, we'll examine the most important question from a strategic perspective.
What are Tesla's competitive advantages?
Stay tuned and thanks for watching.

--- Competitive strategy ---

All right.
Excellent.
So far, we've described Tesla's mission and vision, the industry life cycle of the electric vehicle
sector.
We carried out Porter's five forces analysis.
We consider Tesla's company life cycle as well as its competitive advantages.
We are doing great.
In this video, we'll talk about Tesla's strategic positioning.
Michael Porter, the famous strategy academician who created the five forces model, firmly believes
that every company must choose between one of three strategies cost leadership, differentiation and
niche, also known as focus strategies.
We can have cost focused strategies and differentiation focused strategies.
So let's consider how this framework applies to Tesla and which competitive strategy the company has
decided to pursue.
If one takes a look at the types of vehicles Tesla has delivered to market, they'll see the following
picture.
Tesla started with the Roadster sports car model, which cost north of $120,000.
Then the Roadster was retired and the Model S and Model X came on the stage.
Their starting price is at $80,000 but becomes more expensive as extras are added, and this could increase
the price significantly.
Finally, in 2017, Tesla unveiled Model three, which is a mass market car that has a starting price
of $49,000 for its most basic version.
Tesla communicated that their strategy was to introduce more expensive models to the market first,
which allowed the company to charge more to cover early stage R&amp;D and production inefficiencies.
And then when the company gained sufficient experience, it started producing Model three.
Okay, but how do these prices compare to the rest of the market?
To assess that, we'll need to compare Tesla's existing models with the prices of some other electric
cars offered by traditional producers.
Let's start with the model three, a four door sedan that starts at 49,000.
Please bear in mind that we are not considering tax incentives and gas savings in this comparison.
Model three is significantly more expensive than the other two popular electric vehicles offered in
this class Nissan Leaf at $22,500 and Chevrolet Bolt at $26,000.
If we compare Model three with BMW I3 model, which starts at $44,500, then we will be much closer.
So even Tesla's cheaper Model three is more comparable to a BMW rather than a Nissan or Chevrolet.
Jaguar's electric SUV that was introduced in 2018 starts at $69,500.
This makes it cheaper compared to Model X.
And of course, Jaguar has been perceived as a premium brand historically.
Finally, an interesting press story in 2018 was that Maserati, the premium Italian sports car producer,
announced that they will launch Maserati Alfieri, a sports car that will take on Tesla's Model S in
2020.
Well, if an ultra premium brands CEO sees Tesla's Model S as competition and wants to target its customer
base, then we can deduce that Model S is positioned in the premium segment as well.
I think these three comparisons allow us to make a conclusion.
The competitive strategy Tesla pursues at the moment is differentiation.
To implement such a strategy successfully.
Tesla would need high quality products and premium brand positioning.

--- Tesla SWOT analysis ---

Okay, great.
In this video, we'll use the SWOT framework to analyze Tesla and organize our thoughts about the company.
Hopefully a 360 degree analysis of its strengths, weaknesses, opportunities and threats will improve
our idea about the firm's future outlook.
Tesla was founded in 2003 and is on the mission to become the first successful pure electric vehicle
producer in the world.
Also, it aims to be one of two US auto producers that have not filed for bankruptcy, the other one
being Ford.
Let's start with the company's strengths.
First of all, everyone has heard of Tesla, right?
So it definitely has strong brand recognition.
Actually, Tesla is the most recognized electric car producer in the world.
Quite an advantage with respect to other companies that are about to enter the industry.
Moreover, many customers appreciate Tesla's innovators, spirit and the fact that it is a first mover
in the automotive industry.
Such originality, coupled with aesthetically pleasing design and positive customer experience with
the product, justify Tesla's premium pricing and choice of differentiated competitive strategy.
Elon Musk, Tesla's notorious CEO and major shareholder, is one of the most famous and appealing businessmen
around the world.
His ability to tell a story and involve the public is unmatched.
By doing this, he wins over many people who actively follow his businesses and will likely become customers.
What is very important?
Such customers can be very loyal if handled correctly, which is priceless in the long run.
Tesla is attractive to people because it doesn't simply sell cars.
The company sells a story, the story of an organization which is in business with an ideal purpose
to preserve the global environment.
Probably this is Tesla's most valuable strength.
Along with that, we can mention several other important advantages the company has.
Its cars are ahead of the competition in terms of battery range and experience with battery production
and recharging.
Tesla has put in motion a plan that would make it the biggest battery producer in the world.
The company intends to construct several gigafactories three in the US, one in Europe and one in Shanghai,
resulting in a significant cost advantage against competitors who outsource battery production.
Tesla's autopilot feature has been operational for several years now, accumulating more and more data
and testing in real life conditions.
In a future where we will increasingly depend on autonomous vehicles.
Tesla's experience with autopilot would be extremely valuable.
Of course, we also need to mention the company's supercharger network, allowing Tesla owners to charge
their vehicles for free in a very fast way when on the road.
In a perfect world, this would do.
However, we live in a fossil fuel dominated world, so let's consider Tesla's competitive weaknesses
to number one and quite important.
Tesla continued to burn cash in 2017 and 2018.
The company has not turned a profit ever since it was founded.
That's understandable, considering that it grew rapidly with the goal of becoming a true global auto
producer.
Plenty of investments were made to achieve that.
Negative cash flows are a problem given that Tesla's stock price is under significant pressure from
short sellers who believe the company is overvalued.
The fact that Tesla isn't a traditional mass market car producer became obvious in late 2017 and 2018
when the company started producing Model three.
The automatic production line it had constructed didn't function properly, and Tesla was unable to
reach its target of 5000 automobiles per week for about a year.
This proves that it can be very hard to make the transition from being a premium sports car producer
delivering several thousand cars per month to a mass market company.
It is very likely that the company will experience similar challenges when it attempts to realize cost
efficiencies and improve margins.
Traditional car companies have the advantage that they have been in the business of producing at scale
for decades.
Tesla is an innovator in its field, but some may feel that Elon Musk's idea to make all of their patents
open source was a bit naive and idealistic, endangering the company in the long run as it wouldn't
be able to protect its inventions from imitation.
The last weakness will add here is Elon Musk's divided attention.
The fact that Tesla has a CEO who is responsible for four separate billion dollar entities is a bit
disturbing.
First, is he going to be able to sustain such workloads in the long run?
And second, how much of Elon Musk's time is spent working on Tesla?
Besides strengths and weaknesses, we should also consider future opportunities to write.
Let's go ahead and list some possible developments that could help the company succeed in the long run.
The electric car market is the future of transportation.
Most people agree on that.
How fast will the transition to electric vehicles be?
Most people disagree on that.
Nobody knows.
Some experts believe it will happen over the next ten years.
Others, especially the ones involved with petrol refineries, claim it would take at least 40 years.
One thing is certain, though, for Tesla, the faster the transition to electric vehicles, the better.
Currently, the company is well positioned to win a significant market share of people switching to
electric vehicles in a favourable scenario.
This could be great for Tesla as very few of the other automobile producers are ready to compete in
the electric vehicles market at full scale.
So that's definitely one opportunity.
Another one is that Tesla expanded its product range, remember, because of their merger with SolarCity.
The company now offers batteries, power walls and other equipment related to production and storage
of renewable energy.
A rising demand for renewable energy could mean more business for Tesla.
Furthermore, Tesla's huge industrial batteries will become a very interesting product line in the event
the renewable energy market continues to grow at this pace, if it speeds up even further, the demand
for storage of electricity produced by renewables will rise sharply.
Of course, there are some very enticing opportunities ahead of the company in terms of economies of
scale, increasing volumes of production and hence lowering the cost of production.
Think Model three, for example.
Economies of scope, producing different types of vehicles will probably be more efficient when the
range of products Tesla offers expands and R&amp;D costs can be shared between different models.
Another exciting opportunity is factory automation.
Elon Musk shared that at some point the production hell the company experienced was caused by too much
automation and the level of automation of its model three production line had to be reduced.
That was a painful experience.
But now that Tesla has the scar tissue and has learned a valuable lesson, it will probably try to build
up on what was attempted before removing flaws from the automation process.
This could result in improved production volumes and lower costs, especially in terms of production
personnel.
Okay, great.
One of the weaknesses we mentioned was that Elon Musk divides his time between several companies.
Well, this can be a problem, but it also can be highly beneficial in the right circumstances.
Elon Musk is involved with firms like SpaceX.
The boring company, neurotechnology firm Neuralink and a non-profit organization, Open A.I..
At some point, it is highly likely that synergies between these organizations could help Tesla improve
its competitive position and know how providing a valuable advantage against traditional auto producers.
The last piece of our SWOT analysis are the threats ahead of the company.
As we mentioned before, the gradual transition to electric vehicles can happen in ten, but it can
also happen in 40 or more years in a favorable scenario.
Slower transition is a significant threat because it strips Tesla from its first mover advantage and
puts it in a position to operate in a market that is not growing fast enough.
Such a scenario isn't very likely, given that most research indicates the electric vehicles market
is about to experience tremendous growth over the next several years.
Of course, the entry of traditional producers is a significant threat.
As we discussed, firms like BMW, Volkswagen and Daimler will undoubtedly enter the market as Tesla
continues to release new models.
There is no guarantee production difficulties would not appear again.
This is another threat.
Finally, one has to consider tax incentives for electric vehicles that are allowed in the US and around
the world.
At the moment, governments subsidize electric vehicles through tax deductions and other similar incentives.
In the US, for example, automakers are allowed to sell up to 200,000 subsidized vehicles at the maximum
incentive rate of $7,500 per vehicle.
Then incentives are halved to $3,750 per vehicle and so on.
Although people from the automotive industry have asked for these incentives to be kept intact even
after reaching the 200,000 threshold, US politicians seem reluctant to do that.
So discontinuing incentives for electric cars is another threat for an electric producer like Tesla.
OC Perfect.
I believe this wraps up our extensive SWOT analysis in which we looked at Tesla's strengths, weaknesses,
opportunities and threats.
Hope it was interesting for you and thanks for watching.

--- Why are managers needed in an organization? ---

In the first part of the course, we studied business strategy, and it's not a coincidence.
We're now focusing on management.
There is a strong relation between the two.
Briefly.
Strategy is the master plan.
It is the way we intend to win the battle.
Management is about how we will use the resources at our disposal to achieve this.
This is how we fight the battle, how we execute to ensure an organisation is on the right course to
achieve its goals as planned.
In the same way a ship plans its journey before leaving the harbor.
And once it does, it needs strong leadership to navigate through the sea, deal with unforeseen circumstances
and reach the desired destination.
In the business world of today, managers have outgrown the role of supervisors and have become those
who recruit, retain and inspire the people working in a company.
Given that the people who work in a firm are often considered its most valuable asset, the role of
managers becomes even more critical.
They shape the company's future by attracting new talents and empowering them to grow and flourish inside
the organization.
Research has shown the most important factor in employee productivity is the quality of the employee
manager relationship.
Talented young people are not just looking for a person to report to and contact when they can't resolve
a tricky situation.
They're interested in tutor ship guidance and shared decision making responsibilities.
They want to establish a relationship that would allow them to grow professionally by being actively
involved and continuously learning.
A good manager knows these needs and is constantly eager to challenge her employees while ensuring they
are productive and useful to the company.
If we think of a company as an organization with a pyramid structure, which is correct for most firms,
the firm's CEO will be at the top.
Right below him will be managers with different seniority.
We will typically have vice presidents, directors, senior managers, managers and then employees.
This structure is necessary because it defines areas of responsibility.
Vice presidents will coordinate the work of several directors.
Directors will have several senior managers reporting to them and so on.
Organizations need managers because they serve as a link between divisions, units and teams.
Often it is managers of different teams who communicate among each other and clarify how their teams
can work together.
If too many people are involved in every discussion, the communication becomes ineffective.
Can you imagine what it would be like if there were no managers?
Companies would have a very difficult time organizing their workflow.
Coordinating the efforts of hundreds and thousands of employees would be impossible.
So we can conclude that managers play an important role in the way companies function.
Their role encompasses a wide range of responsibilities.
We can point out a few workload organization, staff management, strategic planning, cost control,
personnel, training and development, critical issue identification, project management.
All of these areas require their leadership and confident involvement.
I'm sure you'll agree with me.
This isn't an easy job, right?
But this makes it more interesting.
In our next lesson, we'll learn what makes a good manager.
Thanks for watching.

--- What makes for a good manager? ---

In the previous lesson, we answered the question Why are managers needed in a company and peeked at
some responsibilities they have?
In this lesson, we will look at what makes a manager great.
This requires a very broad skill set.
It's one thing to be an employee that specializes in an area of expertise and another to be the manager
responsible for a team of individuals in that area of expertise.
The skill set a person needs to succeed as a manager is wider.
A good manager must be a leader, someone who inspires and motivates employees to engage with the firm's
mission and vision.
Maximizing employees output shouldn't be the sole goal.
It is important to engage the minds and hearts of people and show them their contribution.
Makes a difference for the company.
The good manager coaches the staff to be more effective at work.
She's honest, competent and forward looking.
These qualities and intentions are a foundation for building credibility and employees respect managers
who are credible.
They have to believe in their manager's ideas and trust their manager will consistently pursue them.
That's how employees become proud of the firm and stay committed.
That way they develop a sense of ownership and do their best to help the company succeed.
Critical traits of a good manager are self-discipline and consistency.
Employees must respect their work ethic and see it as a positive example that reinforces their own motivation.
Good managers will admit their shortcomings and work hard to better themselves.
They are open to constructive feedback but aren't easily swayed by any random comment.
They create their own management style consistent with their character and personality.
Because we live in a highly competitive and fast moving environment, managers also must be very adaptive.
While they have to be understanding at times, at other times they have to be decisive and able to make
tough calls.
What do you think is an employee who is very good at her technical job going to make a good manager?
Not necessarily.
Technical expertise is one requirement, but it is not sufficient.
The opposite is also true.
People who are not great technically can be great managers.
My favorite example is one about Steve Jobs, one of the finest managers of our time.
It is widely known he wasn't a programmer and his technical skills were not even comparable to those
of the engineers working for Apple.
But whenever somebody brought this up in conversation, he would calmly reply.
Musicians play their instruments.
I play the orchestra.
This is an excellent metaphor.
Good managers are good because they can organize the work of others and make an entire unit more productive.
This differs from being able to do large quantities of work without delegating.
Most people with strong technical skills struggle to delegate because they're good at what they do and
they don't trust.
Others will do as good a job as they would, and that's precisely what a manager cannot afford to do.
She needs to trust her team and be able to delegate work to others.
There's no way around it as she can't handle everything on her own.
Besides, others should also be trusted with difficult tasks.
After all, they need to learn and grow.
Right?
Okay, we're getting there.
One final remark.
A lot of managers struggle to answer the following question in the first few years in a management role,
should managers be loved or feared by their employees?
There are multiple perspectives and possible answers, but a good manager should neither be loved nor
feared by her employees.
She should be respected.
Managers must make tough decisions every day, which puts them all over the popularity spectrum.
Some are happy with them, others not.
Managers have to embrace not being loved.
They have to be comfortable in situations involving discontent and disagreement.
Regardless, they have to keep the course of their decisions.
As long as their choices are fair and others understand the rationale behind them, they will be respected.
They have to let discontented employees openly express their opinions and show them manager employee
respect.
Eventually, a manager who proves their decision making is unbiased and follows a clear reasoning line
will earn the respect and trust of her team, which is even more valuable than being loved.

--- The different types of managerial roles ---

We already said most companies have a pyramid structure of governance.
In such a structure, we can distinguish three management levels of hierarchy top level management,
mid-level management and first level management.
This distinction is useful because it will allow us to understand the seniority of managers within an
organisation.
The three levels are ranked in order of importance at the top of the pyramid.
We have the top level management.
The company's chief executive officer is CEO, its chief operational officer, CEO, the chief financial
officer CFO, the Chief Technology Officer, CTO, presidents, vice presidents and managing directors.
These are the people who run the company.
Their decisions determine its competitive strategy and business model.
Given they've moved up the ladder, top level executives have a solid understanding of the firm's business
and can make decisions that consider the full picture competitive environment, internal resources and
capabilities, and core strategy.
The decisions made at the top are then implemented by middle level management, which is the second
level in the firm's hierarchy.
Among the typical job titles from mid-level management are general manager, regional manager, plant
manager, divisional manager, project leader and so forth.
These are executives engaged in the day to day operations of the company and handle the practical implementation
of the decisions made by top management.
Mid-level managers set objectives to first line managers and assist them in their daily tasks and activities.
In addition, they frequently apprise top level managers of how operations are proceeding, depending
on the firm's size.
Middle managers may supervise many first line managers or a small group of employees.
In many companies, mid-level managers have a direct responsibility over the financial figures of the
business unit they are running at the bottom of the pyramid.
We have the first line level of management.
The typical positions for this level are department manager, store manager, office manager, shift
supervisor, and so on.
These people handle the non managerial employees directly involved in the production of the firm's goods
or services.
They don't have significant decision making power but are important for any organization.
First line management interacts with staff on a daily basis, which shapes the performance and motivation
of most of the personnel.
All right.
Let's look at a real life example.
I would like to show you the LinkedIn profiles of three managers working at Apple.
This is Angela Ahrendts, who works as a senior vice president of retail.
She runs the firm's online and store sales and was one of Apple's top paid executives in the last few
years.
Her role is obviously at the top of the pyramid.
The next profile we will look at is Jamie Twizzlers, who is a regional manager.
Jamie grows Apple sales and solutions in a five state territory by leading a team of 15 to 30 direct
reports.
This is a mid-level management position.
And finally, we'll have first line managers, right?
Charlie Wang is a store manager.
He's the supervisor of staff in one of Apple's retail stores.
The people under him are sales personnel in direct contact with clients.
These are examples of management roles we have in most companies.
In our next lesson, we'll dig deeper into the specific functions of these roles.
Thanks for watching.

--- A manager's main functions ---

In this lesson, we'll focus on the main functions that managers have in a firm.
They've been described as early as the beginning of the 20th century when Henry Pfeil, a French mining
engineer and one of the first people to examine the general principles of business administration,
formulated his theory about the five major management functions.
Planning, organizing, staffing, leading and controlling planning consists of defining goals and figuring
out a way to achieve them with the resources and capabilities the firm has.
Managers consider the strategy of the firm and define the course of action to implement the strategy.
Given that planning is both the short term and a long term process, this function is an ongoing effort.
For example, Apple's long term strategy could be related to diversifying its sales channels by expanding
its retail network.
Its short term plan could consist of allocating additional resources to local branches to open new retail
stores that will be coherent with Apple's major goal and would likely even advance it.
Right.
Have you heard the, quote, plan your work and work your plan?
Well, once we have a plan, we have to put it into action.
And to do that, we need organization, which is another major task managers have.
This position involves getting people together and observing how they interact and work with each other
to achieve a common goal.
Organizing the workflow in a firm is a lot subtler than it seems at first.
A successful manager considers the personalities of the people who will work together, their skills
and previous background with other available resources like technology, money and equipment.
The way all these variables are organized and put into use shapes the quality of the final output the
company produces.
Another critical long term process that occurs in every firm is the recruitment and development of human
resources.
The people who join a company today are who will build its future tomorrow.
Therefore, good managers should always look for the best talent available.
In addition, it is important to train and develop personnel often.
This not only enriches their skills, but makes them more able to contribute to the company in a meaningful
way.
To piece this together, a good manager excels in providing employees with honest feedback.
If the people working for her understand she's interested in their own growth and the success of the
company, she will likely be even more successful.
The other main function managers have is to lead their teams.
Leading a team does not directly translate into influencing others behaviors, but this plays a role.
Often managers who are good leaders can inspire and motivate team members to attain the firm's goals.
Being an effective leader consists of understanding employees emotions, attitudes, values and personalities,
and building an environment that helps nurture their talent.
Not an easy task.
It is unnecessary to be as charismatic as Steve Jobs, to be a successful leader of a team.
In my experience, the most important trait of good leaders is to align individual's personal development
with the success of the entire company.
They can build a culture of trust in which individuals work together without looking over their shoulder
because they understand the real competition occurs outside the firm, not inside.
Finally think about accountability.
It is one of the main drivers of performance.
People can be held accountable for their results by defining key performance indicators and measuring
their development.
The fifth management function is known as controlling and comprises quantifying the results obtained
by teams and individuals within the organisation.
In doing that, managers have a valuable tool at their disposal which allows them to rank performance,
making it easier to encourage top performance and address underachievement.
These are the five core functions managers must address in their job.
Depending on where they are in the pyramid structure we described earlier, the distinct functions may
have a different importance.
But all managers must deal with these tasks to a certain degree.

previous lesson.

the planning process involves significant decision making.

--- The objectives pyramid ---

All right.
In this lesson, we'll learn about the objective pyramid, and we will study the two main ways to approach
goal setting.
The objective pyramid looks like this.
It shows the traditional approach to goal setting at the top of the pyramid.
Top management have their objectives, which are then communicated to mid-level management.
Mid-level managers then come up with their own objectives, which ought to be in line with the direction
set by top management.
Finally, mid-level management objectives are communicated to the frontline management, who, based
on the guidance from above, also create objectives of their own.
Does this sound familiar to you?
Like a famous children's game, perhaps this process resembles the Chinese Whispers game, in which
one child whispers a message in the ear of another child who whispers it in the ear of the next one.
Until the last child says the message they heard out loud to the rest of the group.
The fun part is that often the original message and the one heard on the other end are very different.
Guess what?
The same thing can happen in the working world.
One of the main risks of traditional goal setting and the objectives pyramid is that the strategy and
goals designed at the top can be misunderstood by mid-level or first line management.
Superb communication and extra effort to explain the original message is needed for this process to
succeed.
Managers nowadays are concerned about telling people what to do without considering their input.
The idea that lower level managers and employees will buy into decisions in which they participate easier
and this would improve.
The decision making process led to the formation of a novel style of goal setting the management by
objective style, typically abbreviated as MBO.
MBO consists of defining mutually agreed upon objectives to reach goals one step at a time.
Mid-level management works with first line managers.
They listen actively to their input and then elaborate it with top managers to come up with actionable,
strategic objectives that all management levels have actively participated in.
In our next lesson, we'll learn one of the best practices that enables managers to formulate effective
objectives.

--- Creating smart objectives - the SMART framework ---

One of the most widely used frameworks for efficient goal setting is called Smart, an acronym for Specific,
Measurable, Achievable, relevant and timely.
It is helpful, especially for people who have just started working as managers, to use this tool as
a checklist when working on a new objective that must be communicated to employees or upper level management.
One of the most important traits of well thought out goals is they are specific.
Specific means actionable and concrete, something that is easy to understand and sends a clear message
to employees.
Otherwise, the goal will be ineffective because employees won't understand what is expected of them.
An example of a specific goal is our target is to attract 100 new clients every month until the end
of the year.
The second trait of a well thought out goal is measurability.
If a goal isn't measurable, it is hard to understand if it has been successfully achieved right.
That's precisely what managers should avoid.
A good manager uses the numbers because she understands everything.
Quantifiable, sends a clear message and leaves little space for interpretation.
Bonus points for numbers.
They also allow us to see who performed well and deserves praise.
In our example, we set a clear goal to add 100 new clients every month.
We also set a concrete timeline until the end of the year.
The tracking points are clear and measurable.
We can easily keep an eye on the progress and see which sales agents found the highest numbers of new
clients.
Third goal should be achievable.
When setting a goal, we should ask ourselves, Is the goal achievable with the resources and capabilities
we currently have?
We should consider previous business performance and set our goals in line with it.
In our example, a valid question the manager should consider is Do we have the skills and resources
to add 100 new clients each month?
Is the current business environment favourable enough to do that?
The fourth important characteristic of smart goals is they are relevant.
Their accomplishment should lead to concrete results that are important for the firm's success.
In our case, adding 100 new clients will cause higher turnover, which would mean improved financial
results and an opportunity to reinvest in the business.
And finally, an effective goal is timely.
It contains a well specified deadline for when it should be achieved.
In the example we provided, 100 new clients should be added by the end of each month.
This needs to happen in all months until the year ends.
All right.
Excellent.
This is how we can make sure the goals we are about to communicate to our colleagues are well designed.
The smart framework can be helpful in such situations.
Remember that.

--- Performing environmental scanning - competitor research at its best ---

One of the key variables that determine a company's success is its surrounding environment.
In this lesson, we'll learn about the tools managers use to study the environment and improve their
planning.
The three techniques we will cover are forecasting, benchmarking and environmental scanning.
Forecasting is a method where we use past data to predict future business developments.
There are two main ways to perform a forecast qualitative and quantitative.
Qualitative forecasting relies on the knowledge and judgement of managers and employees that carry it
out.
Typically, we use qualitative forecasting when there is little or no historical data.
This can be the case when launching a new product or entering a new business.
Remember that qualitative forecasting is subjective because it depends on the perception of the people
performing it.
When Apple designed its first iPhone, it had no historical data.
Comparisons with the sales of other phones were unsuitable because the original iPhone contained a great
deal of innovation.
In addition, that was the first phone ever offered by Apple.
Their initial forecasts relied mostly on market sizing and executives expectations on how consumers
would react to the product.
Quantitative forecasting predicts outcomes based on historical data.
Past data is a good predictor of the future.
If we have observed monthly revenues in the region of 9 to $10 Million for the last three years, it
is reasonable to expect that in the next three months revenues will be within the same range.
Quantitative forecasting is less biased by the person carrying it out.
There are factors that could distort even quantitative findings.
First, quantitative predictions aren't suitable for a fast changing environment.
It becomes pointless to use historical data if circumstances evolve rapidly and our past observations
are not relevant anymore.
Forecasting could also be hampered by seasonal or one off events.
Sometimes the average of historical data may include certain spikes or non-recurring events, and if
we don't adjust accordingly, our forecast about the future won't be precise.
When making long term forecasts, it can be useful to implement different forecasting techniques and
to average their results.
This improves precision a lot.
Managers should also know forecasting is an organizational capability which usually improves with time.
If forecasting isn't very precise the first time when predicting sales, a good manager would take corrective
measures and adjust the models accordingly.
The goal of this exercise isn't to obtain the precise number of sales, but to build a reliable estimate
that would facilitate the supply chain management, production and working capital needs of the firm.
Okay.
So that's forecasting.
Another way to assess the external environment of the firm is to apply benchmarking.
It consists of comparing a company's performance metrics against those of industry peers.
Standard metrics in every industry are easy to compare.
Some examples are revenue growth, market share, development, average selling price, number of units
sold, revenue per employee, gross profit margin and EBITDA margin.
The idea is to compare a company's current results against those of its industry peers and assess how
it is performing.
This approach may identify certain areas that need improvement or highlight what works well.
Successful companies apply benchmarking on a continuous basis.
It isn't a one off exercise.
Another method that can be helpful for learning more about the external environment is the environmental
scanning technique.
Today we live in the world of big data and almost all businesses can benefit from a deeper analysis
of external data.
Consumers use the Internet all the time, and their mobile phones have location tracking.
Several applications use this data and elaborate it to come up with improved business intelligence that
ultimately leads to enhanced decision making.
Environmental scanning is a technique that was very difficult to apply in the past with less sources
of data available.
But modern managers should pay attention to it.
With the development of business, intelligence systems and enhanced data collection techniques, companies
can make a real difference in upcoming years.
A subsegment of environmental scanning is gathering intelligence about a company's competitors and studying
their annual reports, press releases, promotional and advertisement materials and other types of public
information.
Buying competitors products, dismantling them and analysing how they've been made is in the same ballpark.
This is called reverse engineering and is a powerful way to catch up with the progress of competitors.
Almost all large corporations do it, so we've covered a few techniques that enable managers to study
the surrounding environment.
This will do for now.
In the next lessons, we'll study how managers allocate financial personnel and tangible resources.
Thanks for watching.

--- Financial planning - break-even calculations ---

The other technique frequently used by managers when they need to plan how to allocate resources and
whether to invest in a new initiative is called Breakeven Analysis and is the topic of this lesson.
The basis for breakeven analysis is the following equation.
Revenues minus costs equals profit.
Good.
To carry out this analysis, we must consider that we can have two types of costs variable and fixed
costs.
Variable costs are those that vary with the level of production output.
If we sell more units of a product, variable costs will be higher.
If Apple wants to sell an additional iPhone, they have to buy a fixed amount of microchips that will
be built into the iPhone.
The money spent to acquire these chips varies because it depends on the number of iPhones the company
will sell.
Fixed costs are independent of the volume of goods sold.
They incur regularly.
Such costs are rent, depreciation, administration personnel, insurance and others.
So if Apple produces an additional iPhone, is it going to pay more for admin personnel?
No.
These costs are fixed and do not depend on the firm's output levels.
However, the more units a firm sells, the lower its fixed cost per unit is right.
Fixed costs remain fixed and the denominator the unit sold increases, which drives fixed costs per
unit down.
Perfect.
We are one step closer.
Break even analysis consists of calculating the number of units a firm needs to sell to break even and
cover its fixed costs.
The break even point is easy to calculate.
We can use the following formula.
BEP in units equals total fixed costs divided by the price minus variable cost per unit.
Now let's illustrate with an easy to understand example.
A small bakery shop is opened around the corner and they offer a new type of bread.
The owner of the bakery wants to know how many loaves of bread she needs to sell monthly to break even.
The unit sales price of bread, she charges, is $2.
The fixed monthly cost of her operation is $450, and the variable costs per loaf of bread is $0.50.
So if we plug these numbers into the breakeven formula, we would obtain the following break.
Even point in units of bread will be equal to $450 divided by $2 -$0.50.
After we carry out the calculation, we get that we need to sell 300 loaves of bread to break even.
Another option would be to increase the price and sell the bread at $2.50.
Here, the break even point would be slightly earlier.
We would have $450 divided by $2.
$50 -$0.50 equals 225 loaves of bread.
This is the principle behind breakeven calculations.
When applied in a corporate finance setting of a large corporation, calculations are much more difficult.
Several costs must be considered, along with forecasting the demand for the new product at different
price points.
However, what we learned here remains valid.
Managers want to understand how many units must be sold to break even, and then compare this number
with the number of units sold on the market.
Such analysis improves their decision making and makes them more informed and prepared.

--- Budgeting ---

Okay, great.
One of the most popular planning techniques companies use is budgeting.
Our task for this lesson is to explain what a budget is and how companies use it.
So a budget contains the goal set for the next year or the next couple of years and includes a detailed
plan of how to achieve these goals.
Planning identifies potential opportunities and bottlenecks in advance and helps management make important
decisions when leading the company towards its goals.
Almost every company uses a budget in its daily operations.
The gelato shop on the next corner does it, and so does Walmart.
Budgeting is an essential part of corporate finance and a key tool for any firm that wants to stay in
business.
Once a budget is created, executives and the firm's financial analysts have a clear idea of where the
company sales must be throughout the year.
They have a guideline of how the company should perform in any month.
They can also track whether their costs correspond to those planned in the budget.
So this tool provides a significant level of accountability and can be a reality check by business unit
managers.
To understand the reasoning behind budget preparation.
We need to think about the following questions What is the main goal of a business?
What is the most important thing it must do?
The one thing without which it would cease to exist?
Well, yes.
Every business must make money, and if it doesn't, it won't be able to pay salaries to its employees
or any other costs that arise and will go bankrupt.
So we can agree that a company's main goal is the generation of cash and therefore its budget must contain
a detailed projection of the cash the business will generate during the year.
We have to focus on the amount of money that will go in and out.
This is precisely what the cash flow statement shows us.
We will need to calculate a profit and loss and a balance sheet because profit does not always equal
cash for most businesses because not all payments will be made in the period under consideration.
There are always clients who ask to pay their bills a few months later, and so do companies when they
need to pay their suppliers.
This generates a difference between profit and cash.
You could have sold goods to clients and registered sales in the profit and loss statement, but if
the clients don't send the payment, you won't see a difference in the cash flow statement.
We've not generated cash.
When preparing a budget, we'll have to monitor all three statements the pal, the balance sheet and
the cash flow.
Typically companies prepare their budgets by using the expertise of people working in their marketing
and finance divisions.
Marketing executives can provide their sales forecast, which serves as the basis of the entire budget,
and then financiers model the development of the rest of the expenses and investments.
Once a budget has been prepared, then comes the hard part trying to achieve the set goals.
Sales teams push forward to achieve the top line while the Finance Department controls expenditure.
The whole company is working towards reaching the planned results, but almost always unforeseen events
occur and our initial plans must be changed.
For example, the sales division can be negatively surprised if one of the company's larger clients
moves their business elsewhere, like when Apple stopped buying microchips from Intel and chose a different
supplier.
Did this impact Intel's budget?
Absolutely.
Did the world end there?
No, it didn't.
Intel took a hit on its expected sales numbers, but could also win other major accounts, such as Huawei
and its expected sales figures for the year change, but only slightly.
These things happen for every business in any year.
Clients come and go.
Sometimes the firm wins more than it loses.
Other times it does not.
And that's normal.
What I'm trying to say is the budget could be a static model, not adjusted throughout the year.
However, most companies use a flexible planning model which allows them to review their results on
a monthly, quarterly or bi yearly basis and submit forecasts that correspond to the current situation
and are updated with the latest estimates for the period.
This method allows companies to correct their budgets due to newly emerged circumstances and to plan
how to deal with what has happened as they go forward.
A firm's top management should not use the budget as a whipping stick.
It should aim to sell the numbers to the rest of the employees.
Everybody will have to work together to achieve these numbers.
And for that to happen, employees must believe in the budget and sort of own these projections.
The right way to achieve that is to include up to date and realistic information in the latest budget
forecasts.

--- Project management ---

All right.
Excellent.
We're doing great.
To recap, in this part of the course, we're focusing on planning techniques.
So far, we learned about several planning tools, such as environmental scanning, breakeven analysis
and budgeting.
And here we will study a planning methodology that has been very popular in recent years.
Project management companies have grown in size and complexity over the last few decades.
It's much more difficult to get things done when so many people are involved.
Sometimes the employees working in one department are assigned to multiple projects and must contribute
to all of them at the same time.
This situation is not easy to manage, especially when all projects have tight deadlines and many people
involved, each with their own schedule.
Conflicts may arise.
Deadlines could be missed and the outcome of some projects could be compromised.
This is why project management became a popular discipline in recent years.
It is the modern way of managing a project and ensuring everybody involved can do their part.
And the project has all necessary resources to succeed.
Project, scopes can be very different.
A company could launch a new product, implementing a new ERP system, developing a new corporate website,
restructuring parts of its business, acquiring a new company.
There are many possibilities.
Every project involves a specific group of people and has a beginning and an ending point.
Project management has four stages planning, executing, controlling and closing of a project with
a specific goal.
It is a temporary effort of a team of people with a clear kickoff and completion date.
The typical project management process looks this way a project owner announces the project and clearly
defines what needs to be achieved with the project.
Then every department that must be involved nominates their representative, a person who will participate
in the project.
It is critical to get people on board with enough free time and expertise.
Sometimes the project owner will act as a project manager, but in others he will assign the project
to another manager with experience in project management and will get updates from him.
Whoever acts as a project manager will identify all activities that must be completed and secure the
resources needed for the success of the project.
Once the activities that must be carried out have been identified, they will be organized in a sequence.
Some tasks can be done simultaneously, while others cannot commence before something else has been
completed.
The project manager will bear these considerations in mind and will create a schedule containing a deadline
for each stage of completion.
Often, project managers use flowchart diagrams such as the Gantt chart or Pert network to help with
the visualization of these steps.
Attached to this lesson is a PDF that would allow you to learn more about these tools.
Feel free to download it and take a look.
Once the project schedule is prepared, it is compared with project goals and if there are deviations
from the scope adjustments are performed.
It is the project manager's job to control how each stage proceeds and decide whether to allocate more
resources.
If an activity is a bottleneck that develops too slowly and puts the realization of the project at risk,
we can wrap it up here.
In the last few lessons we learned several planning techniques.
Planning is one of the critical functions that allow managers to allocate resources and position their
company for success.
Next, we'll learn about the organization of Human Resources.
Keep up the good work and thanks for watching.

Recruiting, developing and empowering employees is one of the critical functions managers have.

company.
Organizing people with diverse backgrounds is a complex task.
Managers must find candidates with the right background and make sure they can work together productively.
Once they hire good employees.
Managers must make certain they stay on with the company.
Good managers are always interested in the personal development of people and how they can motivate

--- The role of Human Resources ---

The term human resources describes the people who form a company's workforce.
They are a vital part of any business because their skills and capabilities ensure the company keeps
being.
It is important to pay special attention to all issues that have to do with human resources.
Otherwise, we risk decreasing the value of our most important asset.
By now we know personnel know how, and HR capabilities are one factor that enable us to build a sustainable
competitive advantage over the long run.
Many studies suggest a relationship between a company's success and the way its workforce is managed
and treated.
That's why world class organizations focus on issues like open communication between employees and managers,
management by objectives involving input from employees and first line managers, performance based
compensation, access to information for employees through frequent internal reports and high quality
training and development programs.
The goal of these tools is to increase employees motivation and engagement, build their skills, and
help them climb the corporate hierarchy.
Employers who treat their employees well and make them happy are often recognized as employers of choice.
Most people want to work for such companies because they recruit the best talent and retain the most
talented employees.
Among the best global companies to work for in 2016 are Google, Adecco, Cisco, three M, American
Express and Hilton.
Google is the best global employer for four years straight in Fortune's rankings.
Its employees say, We love to work for the greatest company in the history of the world because everybody
in the company is world class at something.
Everybody has heard about Google Perks, but employees have shared that.
What makes the Internet giant from special is the people.
They're smart, hardworking, motivated and socially responsible.
An employee of Hayat, another employer of choice, said, I feel valued here.
The brand is great and the people who work here are great.
I feel that my managers and colleagues are supportive and that everyone is treated fairly and equally.
Hayat recognizes dedicated workers and will help you progress.
You see, this is how good HR management policies are built.
Successful companies hire talented candidates, constantly enhance their knowledge and skills and compensate
them fairly based on their contribution to the firm's success.
If we have to recap the three primary goals of air management, they would be, number one, hire the
right people.
This involves HR planning, recruiting and selection.
Second, manage the people hired.
Introduce employee training and development.
Performance appraisal mechanisms and stress management mechanisms.
And last, ensure all employees are motivated, engaged, compensated fairly and fulfilling their duties.
Before we wrap up, we need to mention employee termination.
This is a measure of last resort, which also falls under the category of HR management.
We will study these topics in more detail in our next lessons, so stay tuned.

--- Planning HR needs ---

All right, great.
Now that we understand his role and importance, it would be good to dig deeper and study the core HR
activities that help companies succeed.
In our previous lesson we set the first goal of H.R. is finding the right people.
Remember great to understand how that's done.
Will study the process of HR planning, recruitment and selection.
Here we will address HR planning and in our next lesson we'll learn more about recruitment and selection.
HR planning is about understanding the short and long term needs of the business and making sure it
will have sufficient human resources to run its operations smoothly.
That's not an easy task, given the business environment is very dynamic and business's needs evolve
rapidly.
Often HR managers work closely with division heads to determine their future HRA needs.
If the firm plans to grow its business significantly, start a new project, open new sales locations,
start a new business division or acquire a competitor.
It will have to hire new personnel to support its future operations.
When Apple decided they would invest in building their own car, they hired experienced engineers from
the automotive industry, would support staff who would help them in their research and development
activities.
Air managers will likely be informed well before the start of the initiative and made a plan of how
many employees must be hired, what their role will be, and some candidates that can be approached.
Planning provides clarity.
When recruitment starts, the company must hire the right number of people.
If it hires more people than needed, that would mean losses due to inefficiencies and would cause organizational
problems.
Hiring less people than needed is a recipe for slow progress and missed opportunities.
Planning allows companies to focus on the competencies the people they will hire must have.
When a consumer electronics company builds a car without previous experience in car building, it is
obvious that they will need the finest engineers in the industry.
And there aren't that many high calibre automotive engineers who will relocate in switch companies.
This is a complex process that must be planned thoroughly before it starts.
In our next lesson, we'll learn how companies approach the recruitment of candidates and some of the
best practices they use to hire the right people.

--- Recruitment of personnel - finding the right people who will help you succeed ---

Recruitment is about finding the right people with the right skills, placing them in the right roles,
and doing it at the right time.
When the stars align and we've done an excellent job during the selection process, recruitment will
succeed.
However, it's super difficult to find people who are smart, passionate, competent, dynamic, motivated,
curious, and work well with others.
Everybody's looking for them.
And truth be told, there aren't as many as you'd think.
Recruiting is a lot like dating.
When a person first thinks about it, it shouldn't be that hard to find the one if there are 7 billion
people in the world.
Right.
But soon we find out only half of them are of the gender we're interested in.
Only a fraction lives close enough to consider dating you.
And then there's the matter of similar interests, hobbies, likes and dislikes, aspirations, lifestyle,
and so on.
You get the point, right?
In the same way companies struggle to find somebody with the right background.
Sufficient work experience shows proactivity and is motivated to contribute to the firm for the right
reasons.
Successful matches don't happen often, and unfortunately nobody has created Tinder for job recruitment
so far.
Okay.
Recruiting the right people is difficult, but that doesn't mean we should give up.
We have to try even harder and make sure recruitment is carried out professionally.
Let's describe how recruitment usually works, shall we?
The first thing the HR department prepares before starting the recruitment process is a job description.
The typical job description contains four main elements.
Job title, key responsibilities, job location and candidate profile requirements.
The idea is to explain the position and convey what knowledge, skills and qualifications are required
for a person to succeed in it.
Before announcing the open vacancy, h.r.
Will create the job description by either carrying out interviews with analysts and managers through
observations, in questionnaires, or by reviewing job descriptions for similar positions in other companies.
OC What is the next step?
The company is ready to announce the opening of a new position, but it needs to find the right means
to get its job posting to suitable candidates.
Today, besides traditional job board listings with hundreds of openings.
Companies have started using social media like Facebook and LinkedIn to find their dream candidates.
There are two ways to do this.
Post job ads to receive applications from active job hunters, people who want to find a new job or
contact professionals not in the market for a new job, but could be tempted by an attractive opportunity.
Sometimes companies don't even need to look on the outside, depending on the heterogeneity of the people
working within their organization.
Chances are that a large firm can find candidates internally.
The promotion of existing employees improves satisfaction levels significantly.
It also gives the right example for others in the company.
This way they can expect their hard work won't go unnoticed and they can climb their way up to find
and invite suitable candidates.
It's even more important to select the best ones, the ones who will be a great fit in skills, personality
