--- The selection process ---

Have you heard the saying?
The more you sweat in training, the less you bleed in battle?
That's valid during the selection process, too.
The more rounds of selection and the more complete the process, the higher the chances of selecting
the right candidate.
But we must be careful.
Too long of a procedure might discourage candidates, or even worse.
Other companies can make a concrete job offer and steal them from us.
However, short selection processes are something companies should avoid as the costs of hiring an incompetent
person are high.
There are several ways to evaluate if a candidate will succeed on the job.
One is to apply selection activities.
This is a great technique that shows us whether the candidate has the right skills and personality to
do the job.
When designing the selection process, we should remember the top requirements for the position.
If the candidate shows she is competent in these areas, we can be a lot more comfortable making an
offer.
If I'm looking to hire a finance manager, I would center the selection process around testing her knowledge
of enterprise, resource planning systems, accounting, financial analysis, skills and leadership
qualities.
It is not practical to test over four or five top requirements for the job because we risk making the
selection process chaotic and substantially increasing its duration.
Okay.
How do we test these skills?
There are several ways to do it.
I am a firm believer in assessment tests and their ability to provide an overview of the candidate's
skills.
These tests are not sufficient to select a candidate, but they can be very useful as they give a good
indication about a candidate's numerical, verbal or analytical skills for more experienced positions
where a certain level of knowledge is required, we can use case studies and open end questions to assess
a candidate's technical skills.
h.R.
And division managers should interview the people applying for the job too.
There are different ways to organize interviews.
One on one interviews consist of having a conversation with a manager or an HR representative.
Here, it depends who conducts the interview.
An HR would try to assess a candidate's motivation and personality, ask her questions to understand
whether she will be the right fit for the organization.
A manager will try to imagine whether she will be a good colleague, whether she is sufficiently independent.
They will try to assess her technical skills.
If a candidate is interviewing with her future boss, the boss will be eager to hear what skills the
candidate brings to the table and how these skills will enrich the team.
The manager will also want to evaluate the candidate's growth potential.
Then there are panel interviews.
It is difficult enough to interview with one person, but when a person does a panel interview, they
must deal with two, three or more people.
The benefit of a panel interview is that multiple opinions will help assess a candidate, which eliminates
subjectivity.
This process will also test how the candidate handles the stress of interviewing with several people.
Case interviews are becoming increasingly popular.
A case interview is the analysis of a business question or resolving a logical riddle.
It is a technique which differs from most other interview questions.
The candidate is presented with a situation or task for which she should express an opinion.
Most case interviews have no specific answer.
It's the thought process behind the candidate's solution the interviewer is interested in.
In a group interview, the candidate will be put in a room with other people applying for the job.
The aim of the exercise is to measure her social skills and how that person fits in a group of people.
This is how the selection process for an open position can be organized.
Next, we'll talk about the actual selection of candidates and their onboarding, period.
Fantastic job so far and thanks for watching.

--- Meeting the candidate ---

Once the selection process is designed, meeting the candidates begins.
We will test their skills and personality.
This is our chance to get to know them as much as possible and what ultimately determines whether we
select the right person for the job.
In this lesson, I would like to share observations I learned the hard way.
Don't pick the most skilled or experienced candidate.
Pick the right one.
Now somebody can rightfully intervene.
Wait a minute.
Isn't the most skilled or experienced candidate the right one?
Sometimes the two can be different people.
Often, candidates with more experience than required apply for jobs.
That can be a good thing because it should be easier for them to pick up their duties.
However, a manager should always consider things in perspective.
It is difficult to motivate these people and keep them longer.
Acknowledge that clearly and discuss what motivates them to apply for a position that is not really
challenging considering their seniority.
Lesson number two Be cautious around charismatic people.
They perform well at interviews and can impress recruiters overselling their abilities.
We should try to be unbiased and acknowledge they are good communicators, but remain aware that their
technical skills must be tested to.
Try to find a person who really wants the job and who is eager to work hard to succeed.
Motivation and positive attitude are an intangible quality that is very difficult to teach.
If you pay attention to little details throughout the process, you can notice if a candidate is motivated
and how much they want the job.
If their resume is impeccable, if they have written a customized cover letter for your company, if
they show up on time and are interested during the interview, then they probably want the job badly,
which is a good thing for the company and them.
Once you have selected the right person, don't be shy to add a trial period to their work contract.
This is an additional insurance that allows you to maneuver if things go bad in the first few months.
You probably will not need such a clause, but a good manager considers all options.

--- Onboarding of selected candidates ---

All right.
Excellent.

And in this lesson, we'll talk about onboarding new hires.
Once we have found the right candidate and she's accepted our offer, we need to put in some work to
make her feel part of the company.
From the start, every good manager plans ahead and make sure a few important tasks are taken care of
before the new hire enters the firm.
Some of the pre hire activities that must be carried out are sending a welcome kit, if that's included
in the firm's policies, making sure the new hire becomes familiar with the company's mission, vision,

It is important to arrange all credential issues like access, batch issuance, preparation of login
access and admin approval for all systems the new hire will work with.
You don't want them to be unprepared in their first days on the job and have them sit around doing nothing.
The best approach to onboarding is to structure a schedule and synchronize it with the timetables of
people in the team.
Fix appointments in which the new hire can observe their work and understand who does want this will
help them socialize with others and will break the ice between people.
Research shows the effectiveness of the onboarding process is one of the critical factors that determine
a person's success in a company.
Make sure you approach it in the right way, please.

--- The importance of Learning and Development opportunities ---

In 2014, the Boston Consulting Group surveyed over 200,000 people around the world and one of the most
comprehensive studies on job satisfaction ever carried out.
One of the findings was that learning and career development rank higher than attractive fixed salary
as a factor for on the job employee happiness.
Studies like this helped many companies realize it is ever more critical to train and develop their
own workforce.
There is an old tale managers tell each other.
A CEO told a CFO their company must invest more in the training and development of employees.
The CFO responded, okay, we're going to do that.
But what if we invest in our employees and then they decide to leave the company?
The CEO replied, What if we don't invest in them and they stay?
The moral of the story is you want the people working for your company to be as prepared as can be.
They should be the best at what they do and feel their development will be rewarded.
Fairly formal career paths within the organization help employees envision opportunities and decide
how they want to build their career.
GE is one of the best at implementing a leadership development program.
Its employees can apply and be a part of the program for two years.
The goal is to prepare them for managerial roles, and this approach benefits the company and its employees.
GE prepares highly motivated personnel and challenges them before they become managers.
While the people who are part of the program are happy, they'll make a big step forward and will be
rewarded for their work.
Another recent phenomenon is that modern companies encourage their employees to get official certifications
issued by external bodies like the PMP Certificate for Project Management Professionals or the CFA Certificate
for Financial Analysts.
Firms are happy to pay their employees for exams and exam specific training if they accept a promotion.
Once they complete the exam, there is so many opportunities to grow and develop employees on the job
training through mentorship, online course training, seminars, webinars and online chat training
are only some options firms have.
Online education will probably be the biggest source of innovation in the coming years.
Courses like the one you're watching right now are great because they allow people to study at their
own pace and according to their own needs.
Many companies understand this and are happy to invest in online education for their employees.
The take home message is that education and personal training are the best investments firms make.
A good manager can always pay attention to the development of our employees and work with them to establish
an actionable career plan.

--- Giving productive feedback to employees ---

Right.
Once a person starts working in your organization, the way they're doing their job will determine their
contribution to the company's business.
Some employees output is excellent, others is average, and there are also those who are not very good
at what they do.
Your job as a manager is to get the best out of people, to work closely with them and establish an
open line of communication.
Otherwise, you won't be able to influence their performance.
A lot of managers struggle to provide honest feedback to their employees, and that's understandable
as this isn't an easy thing to do.
When discussing somebody's performance, you will have to touch on a few aspects that are important
to their self-esteem.
Our motivation, interpersonal skills, capabilities and career potential play a major role in the way
we see ourselves and perceive the world.
Performance feedback is not to be taken lightly.
Managers must prepare for it and should remember honesty and constructive dialogue win in the long run.
Managers must provide feedback in two types of situations.
They either do it ad hoc when a situation needs to be addressed, or they do it during scheduled performance
appraisal sessions.
Ad hoc feedback is necessary when an issue must be addressed immediately.
Some situations must be addressed as soon as they arise.
You can't simply wait for the next semester meeting to resolve a conflict between two of your employees
or to address lack of concentration or motivation.
It is better to solve the problem now.
Word of caution though count to 50 and back before jumping into a sensitive conversation.
You need to be calm, collected and have thought out the situation well.
Scheduled performance appraisal meetings are the systematic way many organizations evaluate their employees
contribution.
These meetings are important because they provide an opportunity for the manager and the employee to
sit together and align their points of view.
Often, their outcome determines bonus compensation and promotion perspectives.
Ambitious employees are always looking forward to these meetings, hoping they will receive guidance
on what they need to achieve to earn a promotion.
Different companies organize their performance appraisals differently.
We can distinguish between these approaches 360 degree feedback.
Employees receive feedback on their performance from multiple raters, managers, colleagues, direct
reports, and so on.
The readers fill out anonymous questionnaires, resulting in a holistic and less biased evaluation.
Once they consider the results, managers can make their conclusions and determine which areas must
be improved and praise what's being done well.
MBO The management by objectives technique consists of defining objectives, mutually agreed on by managers
and employees.
Employees are evaluated based on these objectives by coordinating the objectives with employees beforehand.
Managers get their buy in and results show a higher employee engagement and motivation to accomplish
set objectives.
HP is one of the pioneers of this technique.
When asked about MBO, Bill Packard said No operating policy has contributed more to Hewlett-Packard
success.
MBO is the antithesis of management by control.
The latter refers to a tightly controlled system of management of the military type.
Management by objectives refers to a system in which overall objectives are clearly stated and agreed
upon, and which gives people the flexibility to work towards those goals in ways they determine best
for their own areas of responsibility.
The third method for performance appraisal is A.B.C. grading of employees.
Most organizations assign employee to one of the three brackets A, B, and C, where A means outstanding
performance.
B means high middle performance and C standing for need of improvement.
This method is often criticized as it evaluates employees performance regarding the performance of their
peers.
What if everybody is doing well though?
Who will be part of the top 20% in bracket?
A.
Critics of the method underline it is based on subjective judgments and discriminates against employees
who are different but not necessarily worse.
Despite these critiques, when properly used, the ranking system can improve employee performance in
the short term and therefore shouldn't be dismissed as an option.
Okay, great.
We've said a lot about performance appraisal and ad hoc feedback, but how do we approach the performance
appraisal meeting?
Stay tuned as this is what we'll learn in our next lesson.

--- Organizing the performance appraisal meeting ---

One of the most difficult aspects in a manager's job is holding the performance appraisal meeting with
their employees.
Not everybody can be promoted and not everybody can be paid a maximum bonus.
Frankly, not everybody deserves to be promoted and to be paid a maximum bonus.
However, that's difficult to communicate.
It's something you learn to do with training and experience.
But remember, it needs to be done right.
The following process makes things easier.
First, start the evaluation period by outlining what will be appraised and how the employee can achieve
a high evaluation.
It will also be great if you get to hear what their aspirations are and what their motivation is for
the upcoming period.
Once a performance appraisal meeting has been scheduled, you can work on your feedback and cross-check
it with the expectations established earlier.
It's also good practice to ask for an employee's opinion about a goal establish between you and then
provide your feedback.
The outcome of the meeting in terms of remuneration, promotions or evaluation should be communicated
at the end.
Otherwise, the other person will lose focus and become more interested in the tangible aspect of his
evaluation.
OC Performance Appraisals.
Not an easy task, but this is how we approach them.

--- Introducing fair compensation mechanisms ---

Although research shows compensation isn't the top motivating factor for employees, it is one requirement
for most people.
Let's be honest about it.
It is far more common to see people working a job because it pays well than to see people giving up
a substantial part of what they could have made elsewhere and working for a company that pays less.
Every company must establish an effective compensation system that allows it to attract and retain employees.
However, compensation isn't just about paychecks at the end of the month.
It's made of the total package of benefits employees receive from their company.
There are two main types of benefits monetary and non-monetary.
Monetary benefits come under the form of salaries, performance bonuses, social insurance payments,
health insurance payments, retirement plans, food vouchers, vacation vouchers, company car payments,
and so on.
These are payments companies make to compensate their employees monetarily.
The idea is to pay employees at different levels according to their contribution and merits.
The seniority system, which was modern towards the middle and the end of the previous century, is
not adequate anymore.
The current school of thought recognizes people should be compensated based on their merits and contributions,
not on their age and years with the company.
The non-monetary benefits of working for a company are one of the strongest factors that drive engagement.
Examples of such benefits are reputation, meaning in terms of company purpose learning and development
opportunities and the opportunity to be a part of a talented team.
These are benefits employees like to have and consider before joining and while working for a company.
Now consider employee mobility.
It has increased significantly for our generation.
We're well past the time when employees started in one company and climbed the ladder there.
It is now normal to see people change their job every one or two years, so companies interested in
keeping their talented and well trained employees for longer have to step up and offer excellent compensation
packages with extra perks matched with non-monetary benefits.
Here are some interesting examples that have worked well.
Adobe is good at building a team culture.
The company organizes parties.
Its offices have indoor pools, ping pong tables and computer gaming rooms, and it closes shop for
one week in December and one week in the summer.
Sounds awesome, right?
Salesforce gives its employees $1,000 annually to donate to a charity of their choice.
This is how employees feel their company has a sense of purpose.
Twitter is my favorite.
Their policy is to provide employees with three catered meals a day and onsite acupuncture.
Amazing, right?
And the list goes on.
Providing excellent compensation and benefits packages is one way to motivate and retain employees.
But we need to know alternatives.
This is what we will cover in the next two lessons.
So make sure you watch.

--- Motivating employees - the core principles ---

So far, we've talked about what a manager needs to deal with in their job, and we've mentioned several
ways employees can be motivated and therefore retained by companies for longer.
This is critical for a company's success.
Motivation is the fuel employees need to be productive and create value for the company.
In this video, we'll talk about motivating talented employees.
This is a topic that often worries managers.
They struggle to come up with means to motivate their team.
Typically, the first thing that comes to mind is an increase in compensation.
But given that companies have limited resources and there are ceilings to salary increases, this tactic
is not sustainable.
Managers must realize compensation is not the sole factor that motivates people.
Career growth is a much stronger motivator.
Empowering people by developing their skills.
Challenging their abilities with interesting tasks and difficult problems.
Creating opportunities for them to interact with senior leaders and openly sharing information about
the business of the company with them is what inspires talented individuals.
They want managerial roles one day, or if they already have one, they are eyeing more senior positions.
Therefore, they would appreciate if you show them, you are preparing them for the role.
They must pick up certain skills before they're ready for the transition.
It makes perfect sense for both sides.
You as a manager get their buy in in current activities and they benefit by receiving more responsibilities,
solving challenges and getting the chance to shine in situations.
A regular manager wouldn't entrust them with.
The motivation is caused by the lack of success.
If a person feels they are not successful in their job and their career is going nowhere, they will
likely lose their motivation and become less productive and disengaged.
The lessons where we discuss training and development and performance appraisal of staff seem even more
important now, don't they?
Motivated employees want to grow as professionals and take their careers to the next level, and they
need honest feedback from you as a leader to achieve it.
We already noted one of the critical factors that determines employee satisfaction is the quality of
the relationship with their manager.
If they understand you're interested in their development and growth, they will be motivated to follow
your guidance and do what's necessary to succeed because they trust you and like their chances of succeeding.
In our next lesson, we'll talk about additional practices for motivation applied by companies in recent
years.

--- Proven employee motivation tactics ---

All right.
So far, we've talked about employee motivation and its main driving principles, but we haven't covered
some of the useful tactics you can try to incorporate in your management style.
The techniques will study in this lesson are proven to be effective and their popularity is growing
fast among managers.
Useful tactic number one Empowering employees by providing them with decision making authority.
This technique consists of allowing personnel to choose how a goal will be achieved.
Many people have potential they're eager to use.
People have immense potential, and they're eager to put it into use.
By giving them decision making authority, you're making sure they have a stage to shine.
Bear some things in mind, though.
To empower employees, you will have to provide them with full access to operational and financial information.
They need to make the right decision.
Leverage training programs that improve employees leadership and decision making skills.
Allow employees to modify standard work procedures if necessary, and finally compensate them based
on their performance and the results they have obtained.
These mechanisms are necessary if you truly want to inspire people and allow them to realize their fullest
potential.
Useful tactic number to increase engagement.
How to companies engage their employees by making them feel effective and valued and by instilling a
sense of meaning and importance to their work.
This way employees enjoy work and are enthusiastic to do it.
They become committed to accomplish the firm's mission.
Good managers understand this and try to point out frequently how the contribution of their team makes
a difference, what the tangible results of their work are.
That's why FMCG companies like to organize field trips for their employees.
When visiting supermarkets, employees can see the products they're working with on supermarket shelves.
It's cool to see the brands you're working with daily are used by millions of people, isn't it?
Okay, great.
Useful tactic.
Number three, alignment of employee contributions and compensation.
There are different ways to do that, but the most effective is to include a variable component in an
employee salary, which measures directly how well they're doing their job so sales personnel can be
remunerated according to the sales figures they achieve.
Right.
Production staff can receive a bonus if they increase productivity by a certain percentage.
This technique is effective and should be implemented whenever possible.
Unfortunately, there are roles for which a variable component is harder to include.
How can you measure if a payroll employee did their job well?
It's difficult, right?
These are some techniques used by managers today.
Remember?
And use them to achieve higher employee motivation.
Chances are if you do that, you'll be a successful manager.

--- Stress relief tactics ---

A manager is responsible for many people, herself included.
Whenever we work with many people, we think about their general well-being, too.
Building a business isn't a sprint.
It's a marathon.
You need to make sure everybody is in the best shape possible to endure the heavy workload in the long
run.
We know the modern, dynamic environment can be stressful, tight deadlines, new activities to be learned,
presentations for top executives and conflicts with others.
These processes can cause stress, and that can be a very negative thing.
Elevated stress levels lead to negative consequences, like developing a heart condition, high blood
pressure or sleep disturbances.
People may even use alcohol and drugs to relieve their discomfort.
Employees exposed to too much stress can increase their absenteeism from work, decrease their productivity,
and may worsen the communication with their coworkers.
Stress needs to be avoided as it hurts individuals and organizations.
The way people react to stress depends on their personality and on the resources they have to cope with
it.
Some are more vulnerable to stress than others.
We can call the two kinds of people type A and type B.
Type A.
People are those who are impatient, aggressive and devoted to work.
While Type B are more balanced and less conflicted.
Naturally, Type A people are more vulnerable to stress and the stress related illnesses than type B.
And although type A people are usually innovators and great leaders, they create unhealthy stress not
only for themselves, but for those around them.
What causes work stress, though?
One reason is the nature of the work tasks.
Decisions that must be made under time, pressure or with limited information but have important consequences
are very stressful.
Often employees are stressed because they do not know what is expected of them.
Another reason for stress is the interpersonal relationships or expectations at work.
Often conflicts arise because the expectations of a manager clash with those of an employee.
Employees prefer to be lightly supported and given freedom in their work.
Some managers, however, have a very rigid idea of how things must be done.
Some managers behaviour that has proven to be excessively stressful is the failure to address conflicts
and aid their resolution.
A stressful working environment is unhealthy and costs businesses a lot of money.
Think about decreasing levels of productivity.
Higher health insurance costs, absenteeism and increased employee turnover.
Both people and organisations must try to manage stress.
But managers should set the tone, avoid hurtful practices and implement proven stress relief tactics.
Here are examples of how organisations can decrease stress levels among their personnel.
Companies may offer wellness programs in which health professionals work with employees and teach them
how to implement a healthier diet, how to rest better, and the importance of regular exercise.
They can also ensure employees work schedule is not too heavy and implement flexible working hours or
allow employees to work from home on certain days of the week.
Some companies organise quiet rooms within their office facilities where employees can have a break
if needed.
By implementing a variety of stress management techniques, companies protect their most valuable asset
in the long run.
They are also showing employees they care about their well being, which automatically wins their loyalty.

--- Terminating employee contracts ---

Right.
We've studied how companies recruit, select on board, train and develop and appraise employees.
In this lesson, we'll close the cycle and we will discuss the situations when firms terminate employee
contracts.
There are two main ways for this to happen.
Employees can leave the company voluntarily.
They could join another firm, leave for a sabbatical period or have reached retirement age.
These are the scenarios when employees leave the company voluntarily.
We will not focus on them in this lesson because they are standard situations that need little to no
managerial insight.
Less pleasant are the situations when employee contracts are terminated by the company.
This happens for two reasons.
Reason number one, the company's business is in crisis and it needs to reduce its cost structure,
resulting in laying off some personnel.
It can happen to anybody.
During the banking crisis several years ago, some banks went through several rounds of layoffs.
It was painful for the employees because they didn't know if they would be next in line to lose their
jobs.
Companies that have trouble with their business and a burning cash are in painful situations because
there are hundreds, even thousands of jobs in play.
If a round of layoffs is imminent, we should try to approach it like this communicate the bad news
openly and quickly.
You need to inform the employees who will be laid off and those who will stay with the company.
They need to know they're staying and should do their best to keep the firm alive.
A good manager would explain the reasons for downsizing to all employees and discuss which positions
will cease to exist.
They need to clarify.
The reason for the layoff is that the specific role will be eliminated and with it the person who does
the job doing this ensures employees wouldn't feel so bad and discouraged.
Another important aspect of a manager's job is to make sure the company assists employees and facilitates
the payment of their severance packages.
It would be great if the manager could spend time with laid off employees who ask for additional information
or career advice.
Once the process has been completed, the manager must reassign the responsibilities of those laid off
among the remaining employees, depending on their background and previous experience.
Okay, so this is the situation when a firm lays off a significant part of its workforce terminating
multiple contracts.
The other case we will consider is the termination of a single employee's contract.
This isn't a frequent occurrence, but it happens sometimes.
Companies must fire employees due to systematic underperformance, lack of motivation, disengagement,
violation of corporate policies, misalignment between personal values and company culture, or even
because of violation of ethical principles.
Nobody likes firing people, but it's necessary.
It can even be beneficial for both parties if somebody is doing poorly at their job and isn't influenced
by conversations, poor ranking and even a trial period, then perhaps the best thing to do would be
to fire them and have them look for a job they will be motivated to do.
Keeping a person who is not good at what they do and lacks motivation to learn and grow is detrimental
for all personnel.
Have you heard the saying that a chain is only as strong as its weakest link?
If you've worked for months to explain to the weakest link, they must do better and you see no signs
of improvement.
It's time to change the role of that person when that's possible or terminate their contract with the
company.
Making a compromise with this principle means endangering your entire team.
If the situation arises, please do everyone a favor.
Terminate when necessary.
Thanks for watching.

--- Introduction to Marketing ---

So congratulations on completing the first two major blocks of this course.
In the first chapters, we learned about strategy and management some of the most important areas in
business decision making.
Starting from this lesson, we'll deal with another crucial aspect in a business marketing.
This is where important decisions are applied in practice and find their validation.
That's exciting.
Let's dive in.
So first, what is marketing?
We can think of marketing as a process.
This process starts with studying people's behavior.
It's important to understand how people live and what needs they have.
And then once we've identified a set of needs, this knowledge can help us offer products people want.
Once we have created these products, we can focus on communicating their value to people and explain
how they've been created precisely for their needs and establish a long term bond with clients.
It's not a coincidence that the most successful companies in the world can note patterns in customer
behavior and create products that satisfy these needs.
Firms become profitable when they build a loyal fan base.
Customers who believe in their products and identify themselves with the brand.
Think of Coca Cola.
There are thousands of companies selling soft drinks around the world, but we all know who Coca Cola
is, right?
The firm has established itself as the true leader in its market, and that's mainly due to its world
class marketing efforts.
It has been great in understanding and identifying people's needs, learning how its products are consumed
and how consumer tastes evolve.
When many people became interested in a healthier diet and a lower calorie intake, the company recognized
that and offered Coca Cola zero Coca Cola Diet and Coca Cola life.
These are products with fewer or zero calories containing less sugar.
The firm adapted its product offering through actively listening to the market.
Coca-Cola created a brand that's become part of our daily lifestyle.
Its advertising is oriented towards family holidays, such as Christmas, pleasant lifestyle moments
and good times.
And that's how people perceive the drink as a reward in their daily routine.
The company is doing a great job communicating the value of its products and establishing a lasting
relationship with customers.
Another strong area of their marketing efforts is that they can continually refine their offering and
provide to people exactly what they need and where they need it.
Supermarkets and discounters are mainly supplied with large packs, while local bars and restaurants
prefer selling cans.
These trends are observed by hundreds of analysts, and the insight from their work is implemented in
the firm's marketing decisions.
The products offer to the firm's customers the place where customers can find these products, the money
that customers will pay, and the way value will be communicated to customers.
This is the essence of great marketing.

--- What is Marketing's role? ---

As we already said in the previous sections of this course, The World Today differs from the world
20 years ago.
And this is not just a cliche.
For the first time in the history of mankind, business conditions have evolved at a rapid pace.
The Internet, coupled with the evolution of logistics, transportation, distribution, communication
and sales channels, have shaped the environment in all developed countries and created a globalized
economy.
People can choose from products being produced all over the world.
National borders are thinner and multinational companies are even larger.
Marketing's role has become even more important under these circumstances.
Firms must break out of commodity status and communicate the value of their products to customers.
But that's easier said than done.
It is marketers main task to identify the needs of a target group of people and communicate these findings
to the product development team, ensuring they will create a product satisfying customers needs.
And then once the product is ready, the marketing department must communicate to customers that the
firm offers a solution to their problems.
Great marketing communication makes selling efforts superfluous.
Ideally, customers wouldn't need much convincing as the firm's product solves their needs.
Once a person buys a company's product, he or she becomes one of its clients.
And a key difference between successful and mediocre firms is the focus on client retention.
Successful companies understand the value of long term client relationships under focused on providing
an excellent client experience.
Building a recognizable brand and a loyal client base is the ultimate accomplishment for marketing professionals.
So we can see marketing encompasses many activities throughout the entire value chain of a company.
It's not just about sales and advertising.
Usually marketers start their work even before product can be sold.

--- Who works in Marketing? ---

A career in marketing is very challenging and full of opportunities.
It is the perfect fit for passionate individuals interested in business and eager to grow professionally.
The advantage people working in a marketing department have over those employed in other functions is
significant as they are involved in almost all processes throughout a firm's value chain.
Marketers have a direct input in a company's business research, product development, customer targeting,
pricing, distribution, promotion, communication, client satisfaction, and client retention activities.
Philip Kotler, one of the most celebrated contemporary marketing authors, says marketing is not the
art of finding clever ways to dispose of what you make.
Marketing is the art of creating genuine customer value.
Marketing is involved with all activities that add value for the customer.
It's not just about sales and advertising.
Virtually all mid-sized and large companies have a specific marketing function representing one of the
main divisions within the organization.
This is good because companies focus on attracting well-trained, talented individuals who can specialize
in their role and can offer expertise across all divisions.
The potential downside is that other divisions could show resistance to the input coming from the marketing
team.
Leadership must be alert to this danger and continue to emphasize the firm's main goal is creating value
for its clients in a profitable way.
There can be several roles within a marketing division.
Let's name a few.
Typically in most companies there are account managers, product managers and marketing managers.
Usually, account managers are marketers in day to day contact with customers.
They are the main point of contact for customers and a working hard to build a lasting relationship
with them.
So the account managers of companies like Facebook will be involved with firms that have an advertising
spend above a certain threshold.
They will be experts in Facebook solutions for businesses and will adjust their recommendations to suit
varying client needs.
Product managers are marketing professionals who focus on a specific brand or product.
They manage product portfolios and contribute to brand development, implementing new product launches,
contributing to local adaptation of brand communication, applying in practice promotions approved at
a central level, and listening actively and communicating with customers.
In addition, a product manager is typically responsible for the marketing budget allocated to his brand.
The other position we mentioned is marketing manager.
This is primarily a decision making role responsible for both short and long term marketing strategies.
The marketing manager handles all marketing employees.
He also needs to make sure all departments of the firm are focused on creating value for the company's
clients.
Ideally, the marketing manager is a person with strong communication skills who can align the work
of R&amp;D production, supply chain sales and marketing.
We can wrap it up here.
In our next video, we'll talk about the key processes in marketing.
Thanks for watching.

--- Marketing's key processes ---

A good valuation exercise consists of several important parts industry analysis, company analysis,
product positioning analysis, gathering of financial information, building hypothesis and scenarios.
Financial modeling.
Let's say a few words about each of these components of the valuation exercise industry analysis.
To gain a solid picture about a business, we need to understand well the mechanics of its industry.
We have to trace which are the industry players and what is their value proposition.
It is also important to acquire an idea of what is expected to come next in the given industry.
Is it mature?
Is it a growth industry?
Is it a cyclical business?
All of these are important topics.
Very often the five forces model of Michael Porter is used in order to describe a given industry, according
to Porter's model.
The intensity of competition in an industry is determined by five forces.
Threat of new entry pressure from substitute products, bargaining power of buyers, bargaining power
of suppliers, and the degree of rivalry among existing competitors.
Company analysis.
Given that we will be valuing the future cash flows of a company, we need to understand it well.
Which are the prospects ahead of its business?
Is the company's competitive formula a valid one?
Are there sustainable competitive advantages on which the company can rely?
Very often, in order to answer these questions, practitioners use Swot analysis, which stands for
strengths, opportunities, weaknesses, and threats.
Product positioning.
According to strategy guru Michael Porter, there are three types of positioning a company can choose
for its products.
cost, leadership, differentiation and niche products.
Significant value is added to the analysis if the strategic positioning of a company's products is analyzed.
For example, it is questionable to make hypothesis of increasing margins if the company is oriented
towards cost leadership, gathering financial information, and preliminary analysis.
We have to collect the company's financial reports and analyze them.
We need to be certain that the data between various years is consistent.
Examples of events which can pollute the quality of data are significant write offs, extraordinary
business M&amp;A activity, and others.
In this part of the exercise, we have to try to understand whether there are nonoperational items and
exclude them from our valuation because they are valued separately.
Usually, it is very difficult to carry out this task without a proper due diligence with access to
the company's management.
Once financial statements are clean and ready to use, we need to calculate historical ratios for P
and L and balance sheet items.
Sometimes history is a good proxy for the future building hypothesis and scenarios.
After all of these analyses have been carried out, we can go ahead and start building some hypothesis
about the future business of the company.
The most important element of the whole prediction is the view for the top line of the business.
Revenue development is key and very often used as a driver for the rest of the items within the model.
Based on historical data, product positioning, and market research, analysts can build an idea of
future revenues.
Quite often, the top down and bottom up approaches are used.
The difference between them is that the first estimates what portion of the total market will be taken
by the company, while the second starts from low.
Estimating how many pieces will the company sell at a given price?
Multiplying that by the number of potential customers and thus arriving to revenue.
Depending on how sophisticated is the model, there could be some scenarios in it.
Scenarios could approximate the outcome of different events.
For example.
Scenario one successful internationalisation of the firm.
Scenario two unsuccessful internationalisation of the firm, etc..
Hypothesis need to be made regarding many items such as cost of capital, working capital needs, property,
plant and equipment investments, etc..
Financial modeling.
Financial modeling is all about providing a correct mathematical output of the work which has been done
previously.
A financial model has to be flexible, clear, precise and easy to understand by external users.
Usually, a valuation model includes also sensitivity analyses, which help decision makers to gain
a better understanding regarding the influence of different parameters.
A quality financial model allows its users to work with different scenarios.
This is precisely what we will do in this exercise here.
We will start directly from the financials of a company and assume they are clean and ready to use.
We will not do any strategic analyses, as our goal is to become familiar with the modeling mechanics
of the DCF exercise.
We will develop a user friendly valuation model which contains multiple scenarios.
In the next videos from this section of the course, you will understand well how to build financial
models and see the practical application of some of the Excel formulas that we saw earlier in the course.

--- What is a marketing plan? ---

Most companies use a formally written marketing plan.
It is a comprehensive document containing information about several important areas with the direct
impact on the firm's marketing strategy.
One of the main benefits of creating such a document is it collects important information in one place
and facilitates the alignment of decision makers such as top management and division heads.
Different companies have different marketing plans, but we can say usually a marketing plan will include
several main sections.
Let's provide a quick description of each of these sections.
First, we will have an executive summary representing a brief review of the information inside the
rest of the document.
Then there is a situational analysis containing a SWOT analysis which breaks down the firm's competitors
and its customers.
Here is an interesting fact some companies use Porter's five forces model to perform a situational analysis.
The next part of the document will contain a strategic analysis.
It is about the market segment that the company has selected about its target customers and the way
the firm plans to create value for its target customers.
The tactical section of the marketing plan recaps the way the firm plans to apply the four P's of marketing,
price, place, promotion and product.
Then in the finance section, we'll usually see a forecast panel by brand or division.
Marketing budgets that can be used to achieve the forecasted level of sales and profitability analyses.
The last section is about practical implementation.
It can be useful as it defines who handles what in the actual implementation of the marketing plan.
The implementation section contains a detailed timeline with deadlines and action points, which tracks
responsibility and make sure that what was planned will get done.
This is the typical structure of a marketing plan.
This isn't a static document.
Several changes will be made once the actual implementation of the plan commences.
However, if updated properly and used by key decision makers, the formally written marketing plan
can be a great tool in all modern companies.

--- The psychology of customers - needs, wants, and demands ---

Okay, great.
In this lesson, we'll talk about one of the most interesting aspects of marketing, customer behavior
and the psychology that drives buying decisions.
This is an important topic as we need to understand how customers act before figuring out what product
they will need.
Right?
Perfect.
Let's start by defining these three fundamental concepts needs, wants and demands.
All of us need food, water, shelter, clothing and security to survive.
In addition, we need education, recreation, and perhaps some entertainment.
So these are our needs.
Wants our specific preferences directed towards a concrete object.
Some people want a modest two room flat, while others want a luxury penthouse.
Both places serve as a shelter, but the difference in their price is substantial.
And finally, demands are wants that buyers have for an item they want to purchase.
Demands are usually backed by an ability to pay for the object.
There can be a person who needs entertainment and wants to see the new Avengers movie.
However, if he's unable to buy the cinema ticket, he can't demand to see the movie.
And we'll have to think of some other form of entertainment.
So how is this going to help you when making marketing decisions?
Well, bear in mind the product you're offering to your customers.
Is it satisfying one of their primary needs?
Is it something they can choose not to have?
Then attempt to answer this question.
If customers were to buy such a product, are they going to want to buy what we're offering?
If you're about to open a sushi restaurant in Italy, ask yourself the question Is this what people
want?
They must eat food.
But is sushi the food they would like to have?
And are there going to be enough customers who want to eat sushi and can afford to pay the price for
eating sushi?
So that's what we intend buy needs, wants and demands in marketing.
Okay, let's explore other principles usually applied by experienced marketers.
However, please note these shouldn't be used in a way that seems too pushy and aggressive.
We should know consumers today are smart and prepared.
Often they will recognize these tactics immediately.
We can confidently say there are several principles that have an impact on customer behavior.
The first is about scarcity.
People have a higher desire for products in short supply.
It really increases their sense of urgency and they think more seriously about buying the item.
Deadlines have a similar effect.
They force customers to make a buying decision in or out today or never see, and it works almost all
the time.
Our brains perceive missed opportunities as something very negative.
Therefore, people act and buy products when they know they have to make a decision before a deadline
expires.
Let's talk about perceived quality.
Do you think pricing affects the way people feel about a certain product?
Well, it's been proven it does.
A scientific experiment showed that people in France could not recognize cheap and expensive bottles
of wine.
They assigned cheap and expensive bottles with the same quality score.
However, once they had information about the price of each bottle of wine, they immediately placed
a higher score on the bottles that were more expensive.
So what does that mean?
People attribute high quality to expensive products.
Simple as that.
These are some of the fundamental principles marketers remember when collecting marketing information,
screening for new business opportunities, and preparing their campaigns.

--- Conducting marketing research ---

A solid understanding of consumers is at the heart of good marketing before creating a product.
Companies should know two things.
Who is the product for and what problems is it going to solve for that customer group?
Many examples of unsuccessful business initiatives have failed due to lack of marketing research.
The chances of launching a successful venture without carefully studying the market are slim.
Depending on the industry, 60% to 95% of new products fail.
It's been proven that 50% of advertising has no effect and 85% of promotions lose money well carried
out marketing research helps companies prevent such costly mistakes.
It tells a great deal about the needs and wants of customers if they like driving cars with four wheels.
Car produces should understand that and continue to produce cars with four wheels.
If customers want to hear the engines noise car produces should focus on that feature and ensure a great
client experience.
Marketing research can take many forms.
It can be carried out through the observation of customer behavior, focus groups, surveys, collection
of behavioral data and other experiments.
The goal is to collect enough data to be able to process it and decide there is a pattern in the behavior
of a certain group of consumers.
When Coca Cola surveyed a group of millennials, they found that younger consumers have a higher propensity
towards a healthy lifestyle.
They were concerned about their diet and the calories and sugar in their daily intake.
So the company used this information and launched a new product line, Coke Life.
It was intended for people who enjoy soft drinks but are also interested in bio ingredients and fewer
calories.
This is a great example of how market research helps a company cope with changing consumer tastes.
Let's look at their competitors at Pepsi, shall we?
Years ago, when Pepsi entered the Chinese market, it launched a massive ad campaign with the slogan
Pepsi brings you back to life.
Which sounds cool, doesn't it?
However, marketing managers didn't realize that in Chinese.
The ad read Pepsi brings your ancestors back from the grave.
Not so cool.
What went wrong?
Lack of research.
The money invested in this campaign was enormous, and some preliminary research would have helped to
prevent this mistake.
A focus group of several native speakers who expressed their opinion on the firm's slogan would have
avoided the awkward situation.
Think of marketing research as insurance it costs.
It isn't cheap, but it might prevent damages that outweigh its costs tens or hundreds of times.

--- The different stages of marketing research ---

Given that marketing research is a technique that most companies have been using for the past 60 years,
it is a fairly established process that involves five main steps.
It would be beneficial to learn more about each of these steps, as this would allow us to understand
how large firms perform it in practice.
Let's describe these steps.
Step number one, defining the problem that must be examined and the research objectives.
Step number two involves developing a research plan.
The next step is to collect information that would allow us to solve the problem.
Step number four comprises analyzing the information we've obtained in step number three.
And finally, the fifth step is about making conclusions and presenting the findings of our analysis.
A practical example would help us understand what's being done in each of these steps.
Imagine you're one of the decision makers in Tesla.
So far, the company has introduced three very successful vehicles Roadster, Model S and Model X.
Each of these cars costs more than $70,000, which is a fairly high price.
Tesla plans to create a mass market car model three will be sold for approximately $30,000.
This is a different ballgame, isn't it?
The people buying 30,000 cars differ from the ones buying 70,000 vehicles.
Therefore, you know, the company must carry out careful marketing research to understand the needs
of people in the market for $30,000 car.
What type of car do they have in mind?
Do they have different preferences about design?
What characteristics are important to these clients?
It is obvious the $30,000 car won't be as good as the $70,000 car.
If you're wondering how big the difference will be in this case, so far, Tesla has announced the Model
three will have less power, less range, and only one computer screen in the center of the dashboard
as opposed to two in previous models.
So you have to make compromises and careful marketing research would usually indicate the areas that
are more important to a group of clients.
However, the first order of business for you would be to define the questions you need answered.
You can approach consultants who will execute the marketing research for your company and tell them
to learn as much as possible about middle class individuals interested in purchasing an electric vehicle.
But this will cause a lot of unnecessary information.
On the other hand, if you provide them with a list of characteristics you want investigated for example,
price, battery, endurance, safety, maximum speed, average consumption design, and ask people to
rate these characteristics and provide some input about their importance.
Then it is much more likely the marketing research would reveal the answers you're looking for.
And as any management consultant would advise you, you can have all the data you wish, but it is necessary
to find the balance between the cheapest, fastest and most effective way to answer the research question
at hand.
We can conclude the most important thing at this initial stage is to define the right questions you
need answered, right?
And that's step number one.
In the next phase, you need to think of how the data will be collected.
Do you think you will have to conduct research from scratch and collect new data, which is also called
primary data?
Are you going to use data that's been obtained from Tesla's existing customers and previous marketing
research, or what we call secondary data gathering?
Primary data is much more expensive.
However, secondary data is not always suitable for answering our questions.
That's valid in this case, Tesla does not have data for customers willing to spend up to $30,000 for
a new car, so researchers must find a way to collect primary data.
You as a marketing manager must define how the data will be collected.
Usually, different methods represent a tradeoff between cost efficiency and effectiveness.
We'll learn more about the different ways to collect data in our next lesson as this is a broader topic.
Anyway, once you've decided which questions will be asked and how you will collect the data, it's
time to move forward and collect the data as planned.
Experienced marketing managers can contribute significantly in this phase.
They know how to deal with respondents who are unwilling to answer people not at home and the ones providing
false survey answers.
This is step number three in our process.
In the next phase, we can use the information collected and perform analyses.
This is usually done with the help of statisticians experienced in regression analysis, discriminant
analysis, factor analysis, cluster analysis and other data analysis techniques.
The idea is to use the data to find patterns and isolate clear messages.
Once we've done that, we can use these findings to prepare a well-written document that conveys these
messages and defines clear points of action that can be approved by top level decision makers.
And that's the final fifth step as anticipated.
In our next video, we'll talk about the different ways we can collect marketing information from consumers.
Thanks for watching.

--- Collecting primary data for marketing research ---

In the previous video we mentioned there are several ways to collect information from customers.
It would be great to spend a few minutes and learn more about these techniques here.
Typically, we can obtain information that is qualitative or quantitative.
If we interact with customers and ask about their opinion, these are qualitative insights.
But if they fill an online survey and rank product features by importance, assigning a score from 1
to 10, that's clearly quantitative data.
There are a few ways to collect qualitative data.
We can do it with personal in-depth interviews, focus groups, or the so called projective techniques.
In-Depth interviews can be held over the phone, in person, or through an online conversation in Skype,
for example.
This is a great way to gain insight about a person's opinions, beliefs and values.
Some of these concepts cannot be directly measured with numbers, but are very important for companies
trying to create products that connect with clients.
The interview lasts between 30 and 60 Minutes and is conducted as a one on one conversation.
In-Depth interviews have the obvious advantage of the amount of information that can be obtained by
customers.
Researchers can really understand what drives their decisions.
However, these are costly, and sometimes the results are difficult to quantify and present to high
level decision makers.
A focus group functions in a different way.
These are typically organized discussions with a moderator and 5 to 12 participants.
Focus groups are a qualitative method, which helps marketers understand how people perceive a product
when they hear about it.
Questions are open ended and allow customers to explain their point of view and discuss with other participants.
However, we should know that participants could be influenced by the setting and by other participants.
Hence, their answers could be biased.
The length of these sessions is between one and 2 hours, and participants are usually compensated for
their time.
Projective techniques are interviews in which the person interviewed is unaware of the purpose of the
conversation.
They don't know if they're interviewed by a firm selling shoes, jeans or hats.
The purpose of the research is disguised and responded to frequently asked to interpret other people's
behavior.
When they do, they will indirectly share their true beliefs, values and passions.
Examples of projective techniques are words, association, consumer drawing, sentence completion,
picture responses and so on.
The main advantage of projective techniques is that the answers provided by respondents are unbiased.
However, this is a rather costly method, as only highly trained marketers can apply it and obtain
value from it.
Quantitative data is easier to collect, especially today, when there are thousands of consumers online.
The main techniques to obtain quantitative data through observation and surveys.
It's easy to imagine how the observation technique functions, right?
Following up on our example from earlier, Tesla representatives could meander around houses, gas stations,
parking lots and observe how people who own $30,000 cars behave.
They should look for specific factors, such as how many people are riding in the car, how important
is its size?
How many miles per day are these cars usually used for?
What is the typical driving behavior of the people who own these cars?
This is an expensive but powerful method of obtaining insights and information.
Nowadays, observational techniques are even easier to implement.
Some shopping malls connect with customers devices and analyze their shopping patterns.
In addition, they use this data to send them offers with promotions for stores they have previously
visited.
I'm sure you know Google does it too, right?
Internet giants can collect huge amounts of data that describe our online behavior.
And then they use these data to offer us ads for airplane tickets, hotels, shopping tours and so on.
Today, customer observation has become one of the predominant marketing research techniques.
But as we mentioned earlier, the other way to obtain quantitative data from customers is by creating
a survey with several questions.
These are typically close ended questions that are easy to quantify, easy to aggregate and interpret.
Surveys are an inexpensive way of contacting people and trying to quantify their needs.
The drawback is they will only answer the questions in the survey.
Their qualitative opinions will remain hidden.
Okay.
Excellent.
This is how we can collect primary data for marketing research.
Thanks for watching.

--- Performing client segmentation ---

When we mentioned the example about Coke life we sent, the company was concerned with changing customer
tastes of younger clients.
The 21 to 30 year olds of today want a healthier diet and are more concerned about their sugar intake.
So the company recognized this trend and addressed it with a new product, Coke Life.
At the same time, its older clients were unwilling to switch and continue to prefer Coca Cola Classic,
a drink they've known their entire life.
Recognising the soft drinks market is made of, customers with different tastes and desires is crucial
for all companies operating in the market.
Otherwise, they would all offer the same drink with the same slogan and communicate the same message.
Right?
Well, this is the case in all industries.
In 99% of the cases, the market for a product is made of people who need a different version of it.
The car market is a great example.
It shows us that different market segments prefer different types of cars, and that's why car producers
come up with so many models.
People living in cities prefer smaller cars, as one of their main struggles is finding a parking space.
Folks living in the country like pick up vehicles as they're useful for transportation of farm animals.
Well, everything that must be transported in a farm.
Younger people are interested in design.
They will sacrifice functionality for good looks.
But that's not the case with young family couples with children.
They're in the market for a van or akanbi version, as they prefer having extra space and the list goes
on.
Every group is made of people with similar tastes and desires and to expect a certain version of the
product.
So we can say each group forms a separate market segment.
It's much easier to understand a market if we dissect it into groups and define the segment.
By doing this, we can offer a product corresponding to the one people want.
But how do we perform market segmentation?
We can dissect the market by demographic, by location, by the behavior of certain groups of people,
or by the psychology of people in the market.
These are the four main ways to segment a market.
The demographic criteria are fairly straightforward.
We can use variables such as income, age, gender, height, weight, ethnic group and so on.
Marketers are often trying to imagine their ideal customer.
So, for example, the owner of a Tesla model S would be a person between 31 and 45 years old with a
high income and is male.
See, once the ideal customer has been identified and we know their demographic profile, it's much
easier to be efficient with marketing resources and choose how to reach that customer.
It will be easier to design product features they want to have.
Okay, the next criterion is location.
And this is fairly straightforward, right?
It's reasonable to provide a custom offering for different locations for several reasons, but mainly
because people living in different parts of the world have different tastes and they would expect a
different version of the product.
The next way to segment the market is by separating groups of people according to their behavior.
People who go to the seaside once per year.
People who go to the seaside twice per year.
People with a lot of spare time.
People who are really busy.
You get it right.
It's much easier to create a great product if we understand the lifestyle of the people we're offering
it to.
And last but not least, psychology driven segmentation consisting of dissecting the market in terms
of people's aspirations.
People who want to show they are successful would spend extra on watches, sports cars, luxury furniture
and so on.
They're eager to use these items as a status symbol.
This is how we can segment a market and create products desired by a particular market segment.
In our next lesson, we'll discuss how companies choose a market segment and pursue it as their target
market.
Thanks for watching.

--- Choosing a target customer group ---

In this part of the course, we're talking about marketing specifically.
We're trying to understand the concept of market segmentation and choosing a target market.
In the previous lesson we learned people prefer customized products that correspond to their lifestyle
and aspirations, and customers with similar lifestyles and aspirations form market segments.
But how does this impact companies?
Do they have to choose one market segment and focus on it?
Or perhaps they can address the whole market with the same product and hope to attract customers from
different market segments.
This is the focus of this lesson and it will be a great one.
There are three main ways to address market segmentation.
The first one is to offer the same product to the entire market.
A company that does that refuses to focus on a specific client group and believes everybody wants to
consume its product in the same way.
Typically, this strategy is used in industries where companies can't really differentiate their product.
We all consume sugar in the same way, don't we?
So how does a sugar producer differentiate his offering?
They can't really do that because the market cannot be segmented.
This strategy is undifferentiated offering.
In markets that are easier to segment, companies may choose one of two paths.
They can either focus on a single market segment and offer products customized for that market segment,
or serve multiple segments with customized versions of their products for each segment.
The riskier of the two strategies is the first one.
Focusing on a single market segment could be dangerous because a company puts all its eggs in one basket.
If sales depend highly on a single segment, and suddenly this segment changes its tastes or needs,
the company's entire business would be at risk.
However, in certain industries, it is difficult to combine product offerings from multiple market
segments.
Brioni is an Italian menswear couture house that offers super luxurious menswear.
It's known for its made to measure men's suits and usually charges more than $4,000 for a suit.
The firm has chosen to serve the market segment of men with high income, typically older than 35 years
of age.
Many suits are seen as a status symbol by clients, and they will be unhappy to see a brand extension
that offers Brioni products at a cheaper price and directed towards a different market segment.
Its popularity and prestige impede the firm from serving multiple market segments.
This is a valid consideration for some industries.
For other industries it isn't.
People who drink Coke Classic are okay with the fact the company sells energy drinks as well.
Serving multiple market segments is much easier in the soft drinks industry as expected.
So when a firm chooses its targeting strategy and should remember the three alternatives and have a
clear idea which one of them will be pursued, these are choices that should be coherent with the firm's
mission and vision and will be made at a strategic level.
As we discussed earlier in the course, this is step one of the process.
Step two would be to find the factors that can help us dissect the market.
As we discussed in our previous lesson, these variables can be demographic, geographic, behavioral
or psychographic.
The selection of variables to be used depends on the context we're in.
However, we should select variables that can help us break down the market in segments that are accessible.
The people in them have some unique needs, are sufficiently large to be targeted separately and are
there to stay.
It's reasonable to expect this segmentation will continue to exist and is not just a temporary phenomenon.
And that's how we break down the market in multiple segments.
In Phase three, marketing managers will create profiles of the segments that have been defined earlier.
They'll describe similarities between people who belong in the same segment and trying to come up with
several traits that make them different from people in other segments.
This exercise could be invaluable as it helps guide the firm which market segment corresponds best to
the product it can produce and wants to produce.
Step number four comprises evaluating short listed segments.
What are we going to evaluate?
Well, it's time to focus on money.
At this stage, the marketing manager must build an estimate of the sales potential of shortlisted segments.
They will have to use a bottom up or a top down approach.
Bottom up comprises progressing upwards from the lowest levels.
So let's say we want to forecast the revenues of a hotel.
We can start from the number of rooms that will be available for renting in that hotel.
Then we can ask ourselves how many of these rooms will actually be rented?
It is reasonable to expect that different seasons will bring in a different number of tourists and therefore
a different number of rooms will be rented.
We will have to consider the seasonality of the business.
Then we can multiply the number of rooms and the occupancy percentage and this will give us the number
of rooms that will be rented each season.
Once we have made this calculation, we can multiply these numbers by the average price charged in each
season and to obtain the expected revenues for next year.
We could apply the top down approach considering macro indicators and only top level variables.
So for example, we use the following calculation.
There will be 10,000 tourists coming to the town where the hotel is situated.
Let's assume it has a 20% market share and the average price of one room is $100.
Then 2000 tourists will visit the hotel and its revenues will be $200,000.
This is how we can estimate revenues.
As you saw, one of the variables taken into consideration is market share.
Marketing managers will pay attention to the competitors who operate in the short listed market segments.
They want to know who are they up against as it is much more difficult to compete with well-established
players who are well known to consumers.
The other important variable is costs.
How much must be invested to create a product that addresses a particular market segment?
What about marketing expenditure?
What type of campaign must be organized when entering the market?
These are very important questions that help top management assess the entire business initiative.
It is much likelier a CEO will decide to compete in a segment if financials look promising and the move
is in line with the firm's overall strategy and positioning.

--- The four Ps of Marketing and their importance ---

Okay.
Excellent.
At this point, we know how to conduct marketing research, how to segment the market, and how to assess
the different segments of the market to choose the one that's in line with our business model.
We are making excellent progress.
Let's say we've chosen one of these segments and want to launch a product in it.
How do we do that?
The professional way to do it is to prepare a marketing mix strategy in line with the product or service
we want to offer to customers.
By default, the decisions we make regarding marketing mix will be as good as our understanding of the
target market.
If we've done quality research, the likelihood of coming up with a good marketing mix combination increases.
Four major variables shape a company's marketing mix.
These variables are known as the four P's of marketing, focusing on product, price, place and promotion.
This has proven to be an effective way to understand the decisions a company makes when it enters a
market and competes in it.
Next, we'll talk about each variable in more detail.
These lessons will be very important as the four P's model plays an important role in the world of marketing.
But before doing that, we should understand one thing.
If a company understands the needs of its customers, it can create products that satisfy the client's
expectations and contain the features they desire.
It will offer these products at a price that corresponds to the target market's willingness to spend,
and it will offer them where the target market loves to shop.
It will also build its brand communication in a language that speaks to the target market.
When a company does one of these aspects especially well, it has a competitive advantage.
For example, Google has an exceptional search engine, and that fuels the entire company.
Its pricing is comparable to competitors.
Its product is offered in the same place as the ones of competitors on the Internet.
Its advertising and communication efforts are not that exceptional.
However, its search engine is simply the best.
This is a source of competitive advantage, and given the company's search engine has been number one
for over 15 years, we can say this is a sustainable competitive advantage.
Marketing mix and the four PS is intuitive.
Marketing managers should concentrate on these variables and tailor them regarding the target market
they've chosen to pursue.
Easier said than done.
Right.

--- The product concept ---

The first of the four variables that shape a company's marketing mix we will consider is product the
products that a firm produces and sells to its customers.
There is a reason we want to start with the product variable.
So far we talked about marketing, research, market segmentation, choosing a target market and the
psychology of customers.
All these lessons led us to the conclusion that firms must listen actively to the market.
They have to understand customers in their target market, and if they do that, then they will know
what type of products will win these customers.
There are two main types of products that can be offered to clients.
Goods and services.
The difference between the two is that goods are of tangible nature products such as Apple iPhones and
Tesla cars, while services are of intangible nature.
A haircut or a lawyer consultation.
Whether we talk about goods or services, we can say usually people are not just buying a product that
solves one of their basic needs.
They acquire the benefits and satisfaction they believe they'll obtain from the product.
They're interested in symbols and ideas that help them relate the product to a desired lifestyle and
positive expectations about the future.
Marketers understand that and try to incorporate these ideas when developing a product concept.
When a person buys a Rolex, they want to tell the time, but they're also interested in wearing the
watch as a testament to their status and wealthy lifestyle.
McDonald's is a chain of fast food restaurants, but the company's goal is to create a customer perception
that associates its products with happy family times, children, happiness, pleasant surprises and
good times.
Starbucks doesn't just offer coffee as many other shops and chains do.
It transforms the whole coffee drinking experience, offering high quality coffee drinks, plenty of
choice and excellent service to its customers.
People love drinking their coffee in Starbucks, and often it becomes one of their favorite times of
the day.
Customers do not simply buy products because they satisfy their needs.
Instead, they're interested in becoming clients of firms that offer them a complete product concept,
an idea of a promise, if you will.
The best products make people happier and create a positive expectation before being consumed.

--- Classifying a firm's products ---

Most companies offer more than one product to their clients.
This is a great way to grow a business and an advantageous move from a strategic point of view.
The organization shouldn't rely on a single product.
It's much better if its product portfolio is diversified, right?
The question is how diversified?
Sometimes firms offer products that satisfy a variety of needs, while in others they create multiple
versions of a product aimed at satisfying the same need.
Professional marketers use the term product line when referring to a group of products with a strong
relationship in terms of function, customer, group, market and price range.
A product mix represents the full range of product lines the firm offers.
We need to learn these concepts as they are frequently used by marketing professionals and help them
describe the relationships between various products offered by their company or its competitors.
So let's look at a practical example.
We can take Coca-Cola's product offering.
The company offers three types of products soft drinks, minute made range of products and mineral water.
These are its core product lines.
Each product line contains several products.
