In soft drinks, we would have Coca Cola, Diet Coke, Coke Zero, Fanta Sprite and so on.
The more products we have in a product line, the deeper we say it is.
And the product mix encompasses all three product lines and all products part of these product lines.
This is how we can categorize the products of a company.
In our next video, we'll talk about the typical lifecycle of products and how it impacts a firm's mid
and long term business decision making.

--- The typical product life cycle ---

All right.
As anticipated in this lesson, we'll talk about the typical life cycle of a product.
Most considerations may sound familiar, and they should.
Earlier, we talked about an industry's life cycle.
Many of the insights we learned there are valid here as well.
Product and industry life cycles tend to be aligned in some cases.
In other situations, they are not.
Remember in the nineties we were watching movies on VHS.
Well, we don't do that anymore, but we still watch movies, right?
This is a good example of an industry that outlasted one of the products that were part of that industry.
Similar to the industry life cycle we saw earlier, the typical product lifecycle is made of four main
stages in introduction, growth, maturity and decline.
Let's describe each from a products perspective and try to emphasize the aspects that require a marketing
manager's attention.
Bearing in mind the different phases require different strategies in terms of competition, pricing,
distribution and advertising.
The introduction phase is characterized by low revenues and mostly negative profits.
It's difficult to explain to customers there is a new product they should buy, even if it is a really
good one.
People are reluctant to try new things, and only a small fraction of the customers, the so called
early adopters are open to change.
This is why many products fail at this stage.
A new product requires significant marketing expenses for advertising and communication and rarely converts
well in terms of sales.
However, a huge advantage that shouldn't be forgotten is that early adopters remember which company
introduced a product and stayed loyal to the brand and continue to buy their products.
Such was the case with Apple, who produced the first personal computer in 1977.
Later in the nineties, the company was struggling to develop products that could compete with the ones
offered by other producers, but its loyal customer base stuck with the company.
This was one reason Apple survived for a long time before its successful turnaround.
A product that reaches breakeven revenues equal or greater than production and distribution costs can
be said to have entered its growth phase.
Revenues grow much faster as the number of people who use the product increases substantially.
And companies can use these proceeds to invest in advertising and establish a brand that is recognizable
by customers.
This is the right moment when marketing managers can focus on market share growth and creating barriers
to entry for other companies interested in selling the product sales reached their peak in the maturity
phase and then slow down until the product reaches its decline.
The maturity stage is characterized by an increased level of competition, and marketing managers are
doing their best to keep the firm's market share intact.
This is when companies differentiate their products even more and reach out to specific market niches.
Sometimes price differentiation becomes a very interesting topic as certain players will differentiate
their pricing and focus on clients that will spend more.
Often, marketing managers use advertising to reach out to their target customers who already know the
product exists and have tried it.
They should be convinced it is precisely what they need.
At this stage, it's fruitful to think of alternative ways to sell the product or of alternative uses
of the product.
The automobile industry is a good example of a mature market.
Car producers like Daimler are looking for new ways to sell their products and for innovative uses of
the products.
And that's how Daimler introduced car to go.
A car sharing that allows subscribers to grab cars available on the street and move them from point
A to point B.
The best part is they need not return a car where they grabbed it from.
So that's definitely an alternative way to sell and use the product and it results in higher revenues
given the company can market to a new segment of customers.
Cool, right?
Things become less peachy in the decline period.
The product becomes obsolete as it's probably been substituted by a newer product.
Remember video rental giant Blockbuster?
Well, its core product is obsolete now as everybody watches movies online.
Unfortunately, the firm didn't reshape its competitive strategy on time, which led to its bankruptcy
in 2010.
The decline stage is characterized by decreasing sales.
As customers are shifting away from the product, marketing managers will focus on eliminating low profitability
product lines and points of sales.
They need to take a good, hard look and consider eliminating the product or repositioning it.
These are the four stages in a product lifecycle.
Good marketing managers will consider them when planning.
In our next lesson, we'll talk about the importance of branding.
Thanks for watching.

--- Product branding ---

One of the most powerful ways to get in people's hearts and to stay there is to build a brand they can
trust and relate to.
That's why branding is a crucial element in a firm's marketing efforts.
A brand is a name, picture, symbol, or any other characteristic that distinguishes a firm from its
competitors, and it helps both sellers and customers.
Sellers benefit from branding because brands help customers identify their products.
And then if clients are happy with their purchase, it would be much easier for them to recognize and
buy that product again.
At the same time, it can be difficult for clients to recognize quality products, a brand name they
know can be a symbol of quality and trust.
Therefore, clients will relate their trust for the brand with the quality of the product.
It usually works this way, doesn't it?
When we know of a brand, we feel more comfortable with it.
And this facilitates our decision making process.
When I go to the supermarket and I need to buy detergent, I can't compare all the detergents they have.
I'm no expert.
That's why I typically choose aerial.
I'm familiar with the brand and know that P&amp;G is an excellent firm that is a global leader in its industry.
This makes me feel comfortable with the product, and that was just an example from my own experience.
I'm not a P&amp;G testimonial or affiliate.
That's how I act as a consumer buying detergent.
I'm sure you probably do something similar, don't you?
Brand names are important in several industries.
Most teenagers prefer Nike to ASICs.
How come?
Well, they associate Nike with Michael Jordan and LeBron James, two of the brand's testimonials.
They relate the brand with their favorite athletes and love buying Nike's products as they feel a connection
with the brand.
One of the best examples of how powerful brand names can be is the fashion industry.
We've all heard of famous designer names like Gucci, Armani and Prada, right?
Over the years, these fashion houses have established strong brand names by creating amazing clothing
collections and investing in advertising of these collections.
What about sunglasses, though?
Are they experts in the production of eyewear?
Many people believe they are, as we can see their sunglasses showcased in boutiques around the world.
But they aren't.
They simply leverage their brand name, using it to extend their product line.
There is an Italian firm called Luxottica, which produces most of the eyewear sold and branded with
the name of different world famous fashion houses.
Brand owners receive royalties because customers will buy a product with their name attached to it.
This is how powerful a brand can be.
There are three degrees of brand loyalty, brand recognition, brand preference and brand insistence.
The initial stage is brand recognition.
Before we earn customers trust, they have to learn our name.
In this phase, customers recognize our brand but have no preference for it.
They will consider it as a possible replacement if the brand they prefer is not available.
Ideally, marketing managers will run campaigns and will convince these customers this brand is superior
to other brands on the marketplace, and this will cause a preference for their brand.
They will look for its products first and only if they can't find them.
They will buy alternative brands.
Brand insistence is the best case scenario.
Clients have a strong preference for a brand.
In this case, they are unwilling to buy different products and will spend extra time to find the desired
brand.
Some supermarket chains have found that many customers insist on Coca-Cola.
They are unwilling to switch away and buy an alternative cola product.
That's why they must place Coca-Cola in their stores.
Otherwise, customers could go to a different supermarket and shop there.
Getting into customers hearts is a difficult process, but that can be very rewarding for firms that
do it.

--- Product packaging ---

Packaging plays an important role in the world of marketing.
It's the first thing consumers see when they have a product in their hands.
It shapes their first impressions of the product and the entire brand.
Besides appearances, packaging designers must consider several other variables the protection of a
product, its ease of use, and the way it can be stored in a warehouse and transported by resellers
and distributors.
Therefore, coming up with an optimal packaging solution is not a trivial task.
Designers must ensure the product is well protected, which reduces costs for resellers and distributors
and is still easy to use.
They are constantly challenged by logistics managers who are eager to optimize the number of units that
can be transported in a shipment.
Apple reduced the size of its iPhone packages to be able to ship more items with the same quantity of
transportation.
Giving that the company spends less on packaging, saves from transportation and makes things easier
for distributors.
This is a win win situation.
When we talk about packaging, we should also remember it is not just designers decision the end product.
We see results from a long and complicated discussion between production, marketing, logistics and
sales teams.
Some of the critical variables to be considered are size, shape, materials, colors and graphic symbols.
The last two are important from a marketing perspective.
The choice of colours and symbols influences the emotions of customers.
People associate colors with feelings and experiences.
For example, Blue is considered relaxing and is thought of as a symbol of wealth, trust and security.
Grey is often seen as strong and successful.
Orange is a symbol of low cost and a red provokes excitement.
Marketing practitioners know these subconscious associations and are often willing to test with multiple
focus groups until they determine the optimal packaging solution for their product.

--- The variables influencing product pricing ---

Pricing is the one variable a company can change overnight and see an immediate effect on revenues and
profits.
However, pricing decisions inconsistent with a firm's competitive strategy can be dangerous, which
is why companies should address the topic with much caution and attention.
There are three important components we need to distinguish in this process the amount of product costs
to be produced, the price customers pay to buy the product and the value they acquire from the product.
So to sum up cost, price and value the three main variables to consider when making pricing decisions.
If a company aims at cost leadership, then the focus will be costs, trying to keep them as low as
possible.
The company must offer a price slightly lower than the one offered by competitors.
Conversely, if a firm's strategy is differentiation, then the critical factor will be the value delivered
to customers.
Costs are not that important as companies offering a differentiated product can charge more.
When UCS price discount retailer Poundland thinks about prices, it focuses on its own costs and competitors
prices.
Its strategy is to offer undifferentiated products at a lower price than the competition.
You can buy a four pack bounty in Tesco too, but its price will be 150.
Poundland offers a better deal for the same product.
So as long as the company can offer lower prices than competitors, its competitive strategy works well.
Apple is a company that produces and sells highly differentiated products.
Customers believe its devices and software are best in class and will pay a premium price regarding
Lenovo laptops and mobile phones with the same technical characteristics.
Apple's strategy is to produce a product that delivers a lot of value to customers and to charge extra
for it.
Its business model remains valid for as long as the customers continue to perceive the value they get
from Apple's products is worth the extra dollars they pay.
So pricing must be coherent with competitive strategy.
Although sometimes it is tempting to adjust prices to stimulate short term gains of market share and
increases of sales.
Top level managers have to stay focused on the big picture and make coherent decisions in line with
the firm's long term strategy.

--- The demand curve ---

Okay.
Excellent.
One of the most important aspects marketers study is the demand curve for a product.
For almost all products, we can say customer demand increases when prices go down.
So, for example, if cinemas cut ticket prices and start selling tickets for $8 instead of $10, there
will be more people watching movies.
Some cinema chains are actually doing that on Tuesdays and Thursdays, and saloons are almost always
full of people.
This is a good example of a situation when we have an inverse relationship between demand and price.
The lower the price, the higher the demand.
Right.
How much more sales can we expect if we push down prices only a bit lower?
One of cinema's charged clients $9 per ticket.
The effect will be weaker, but in the same direction.
It's a marketing manager's job to study the demand curve and use the help of market analysts to answer
this question.
We can have three types of demand elasticity elastic demand, inelastic demand and unit elasticity.
Let's examine each of them.
An elastic demand means the percentage change in quantity sold is higher than the percentage reduction
of prices.
So the sales of a product with an elastic demand will increase by 2% if its price decreases by 1%.
This usually happens for undifferentiated products, and when we have multiple alternatives in the market,
inelastic demand is the opposite situation.
The percentage change in quantity is smaller than the percentage change in price, which is the case
with products with few substitute goods and for commodities.
This usually happens when there are few substitute goods on the market and the product is an essential
commodity.
We have to consume it.
There's no way around it.
Even if its price goes up, we will still have to buy it.
Goods like milk, bread and sugar are a good example.
The third scenario is when the percentage change in quantity corresponds to the percentage change in
price.
This is unit elasticity.
For every percentage change of price we can expect an equivalent change in volume.
Sold price goes up by 1%.
Volume sold decreased by 1%.
Understanding the demand curve is an important exercise as it helps firms make important decisions and
evaluate the effect of short term price changes.

--- Performing break-even calculations ---

High demand for a firm's products is great, but high profits are even better.
In this lesson, we'll talk about the relationship between demand, costs and profits.
Let's look at the following example.
A firm rents a movie theater.
It pays 40,000 monthly rent.
The cost of personnel it pays to operate the place is another $20,000, and 12,000 people visit the
cinema every month.
Each of these customers spends an average of $12.
A large portion of the box office receipts is paid to movie creators and distributors.
The cinema and movie owners split the ticket 5050.
And for simplicity, we'll hypothesize there are no other sources of revenues and expenses.
Let's do a few calculations.
12,000 people visit the cinema and pay an average of $12 per ticket.
This gives us revenues of $144,000.
However, half of these revenues are then paid to movie creators, so the company is left with $72,000.
Its rent and staff expenses amount to 60,000, which leaves us with a bottom line of 12,000.
Okay, excellent.
Now we'll need a few definitions.
Let's start by looking at the cost structure of the firm.
There are two types of costs variable and fixed costs.
In our case, the money paid to movie producers is a variable cost, right?
It grows proportionately to the number of tickets sold by the cinema.
If it sells one ticket, $6 will be owed.
And when it sells 12,000 tickets, $72,000 is owed for each ticket sold the firm pays $6.
Simple as that.
What about the cost of personnel and rent expenses?
Are these variable costs too?
I don't think so.
The firm will have to pay rent even if no one shows up to the cinema.
Personnel who work there will expect the same salaries if they have one or 10,000 visitors.
Therefore, we can conclude these are fixed costs that are not dependent on the number of tickets sold.
As long as the firm operates the cinema, these definitions are useful as they help us understand one
thing, all else being equal, a firm must sell enough units to cover its fixed costs.
There are economies of scale.
If we sell more units, the fixed cost per unit decreases and the business has a chance to break even
and profit.
We can look at our example in the following way.
Number of tickets sold multiplied by tickets.
Price minus variable costs equal to 50% of tickets.
Price times the number of tickets sold minus fixed costs of 60,000.
This gives us $12,000.
Let's calculate the break even point.
Now, what is the number of tickets that would allow the company renting the cinema to break even?
In this equation, the number of tickets sold becomes our unknown x.
We would have $12 multiplied by x, -$6 multiplied by x -60,000, and this is equal to zero.
So basic algebra allows us to solve for x and obtain that x is equal to 10,000.
If the cinema theater sells 10,000 tickets or more, it won't lose any money.
If it sells less than 10,000, it will lose money.
The notions of variable and fixed costs and break even analysis we showed here are very helpful when
we have to determine the optimal price point for a product.
These are the tools that would allow us to improve our decisions in terms of target volume sold and
pricing.
You can reinforce what you learned in this lesson by solving the exercise provided in the download section.
This will do for now.
Thanks for watching.

--- Setting up product distribution ---

So far we've covered two of the four P's product and pricing.
In the next few lessons, we'll turn our attention to the third one place or the location where a company's
products are being sold.
Product distribution is probably the most difficult step to set up among the four P's.
How come?
Well, it takes a lot of time and significant energy to build and optimize a distribution channel.
And we have to consider several factors that will influence our decision.
The best distribution channel is the one that delivers a product the customers want, delivers a product
where customers want it, and allows customers to have the product at the right time.
So let's dig deeper and try to understand the implications of the three principles.
On multiple occasions, we emphasize the importance of creating a product that customers want to have.
What is probably equally important, however, is the way it is delivered to them, and this is one
of the final variables determining whether they will acquire it.
A great looking store with prepared sales personnel and optimal product placing can have a huge impact
on sales.
That's why companies like Apple and Tesla have taken matters into their own hands and built their own
stores, which allows them to have direct contact with customers and explain to them the benefits of
their products.
A product must be positioned where its target customers are likely to find it.
Prada owns shops in luxurious shopping streets such as the Fifth Avenue in New York and Monte Napoleone
in Milan.
Companies working with distributors must examine their point of sales carefully and try to understand
if their target customer group matches the one visiting these places.
The other trait of great distribution channels is they deliver products at the right time.
Nobody likes it when an item is out of stock or when there's a long waiting period for its delivery.
Companies want to be efficient with the inventory they keep as it represents a cost.
An optimized logistics flow can be beneficial for both customers and producers.
Great distributors can find the best solution and typically apply just in time.
Policies introduced by car manufacturer Toyota just in time is a method aimed at reducing flow times
within a production system and response times from suppliers and customers.
Nowadays, almost all companies try to implement such procedures.
They use enterprise resource planning systems, also known as ERP systems that automatically optimize
orders, inventory levels, shipments and other aspects which improves the entire distribution flow
and reduces the need for human intervention.
Okay, let's pause for a second.
In the next video, we'll learn about the different types of distribution channels.

--- Types of distribution channels ---

Choosing the right distribution channel is a very delicate decision.
And now we know why.
Let's spend a few minutes and learn about the different types of channels and how each is formed.
When we talk about consumer products, we can pick from one of the following four options direct marketing
channels.
These comprise producers selling products directly to consumers.
The advantage of this type of channel is the producer can establish a direct relationship with clients,
own their data and manage their experience.
However, this comes at the expense of higher costs and organizational difficulties.
Tesla is a great example of a company that sells directly to end users.
To buy a Model S, you can simply order it on their website.
This means cutting the middle man's margin.
The price for the end customer is not increased due to dealers commissions.
Producers can involve retailers who will sell to final customers.
So, for example, Wal-Mart buys many products directly from producers.
The company can do that because its trade volumes are enormous and it can reach out directly to producers.
The third channel we have involves a producer, a wholesaler, a retailer and final customers.
This is a valid option for producers who sell their products to thousands of retailers and cannot manage
the relationship with each of them.
The retailers in this channel will be smaller shops or retail chains that do not have significant trade
volumes and need the services of a wholesaler who buys from producers.
And finally, the fourth channel is the one involving agents.
It is useful for new products that need a stronger push.
Agents can leverage their connections and strong sales skills, communicating with wholesalers and retailers,
and convincing them to try selling the new product.
These are the four types of channels we can have when selling consumer products.
What about industrial products?
The picture we see here is very similar, isn't it?
There is a slight difference in some channels used here.
The direct channel is much heavier as it accounts for over 50% of sales.
This occurs because industrial goods are very expensive and have a high degree of customization and
it makes sense to have direct contact with the customer.
Business clients prefer having direct contact with their supplier, even if it means a more expensive
service.
Their main concern is risks.
Therefore, they are unwilling to lose valuable time when implementing a solution.
The other three channels are similar to the ones we saw for consumer products.
We can have a distributor or an agent who serves as a link between producers and business clients,
or have both a distributor and an agent.
The relationship between producers and distributors will have three main dimensions logistics, financials
and information.
Things are easier to agree about on the technical side of logistic issues.
The producer company will ship its products to distributors who will deliver them to final customers.
And if something goes wrong, clients will return the product to the distributors who will send it back
to the producers.
These procedures are easy to standardize and should be the point of lowest resistance in the relationship
between producers and distributors.
Things become different when money comes in play.
Negotiations about discounts, pricing for the end customer and payment terms are an ongoing topic that
becomes very difficult in most producer distributor relationships.
The easier it is for distributors to replace the firm's products with the ones offered by competitors,
the greater the leverage they will have at negotiations.
The last aspect we mentioned is information.
One of the bigger drawbacks of working with a distributor is they have all your client information and
what is worse in some situations, they might refuse to share it with you as they consider it their
own asset.
Every firm wants to know more about its customers.
And what if someone else owns that information?
Well, obviously that's a problem.
In an ideal world, the relationship between producers and distributors would be flawless.
Both parties will value each other, will be determined to share profits in a fair way, and will work
together in the long run.
The truth is, however, we live in the real world, and producer distributor relationships are rather
complex and require willingness for concessions on both sides.

--- The advent of e-commerce ---

Undoubtedly the fastest growing channel for the last 15 years has been e-commerce.
Every year, customers spend trillions of dollars online, and according to research carried out in
2016, approximately 50% of Internet users have bought products or services online.
And many companies are excited by these trends as this is a great opportunity to win significant market
share in an environment that is still evolving.
E-commerce allows firms to offer goods directly to their clients through a website or mobile application.
By doing this, they can surpass some of the heaviest of traditional distribution.
They need not pay rent for stores and pay salaries to shop personnel.
Eliminating these costs remove some significant barriers to entry.
Nowadays, there are websites selling consumer electronics that don't even keep any inventory.
This pushes down expenses even lower and allows these firms to compete with multinational giants, something
that was unthinkable 20 years ago.
What's even more exciting is that companies can offer their products to consumers in all parts of the
world.
That's an amazing opportunity for firms to secure traffic from organic search or paid advertising.
People who work in e-commerce must be familiar with these terms.
Most companies with a strong online presence have dedicated employees who work tirelessly to optimize
Google rankings, as this leads to inexpensive traffic of customers visiting their online store.
Companies that want faster results must invest in paid advertising.
Be it on Google, Facebook, or simply by acquiring banner impressions on other websites.
The main idea is to earn a positive return after advertising costs have been considered.
To do that, these firms must monitor the number of clicks they receive from an ad and the per each
click.
If 100 people click on an ad and visit a site and each of these clicks costs $0.40, then the ad spend
would be $40.
Let's say four of 100 people purchase a product sold for $25.
This gives you a margin of $60.
If the product being sold costs $30, you would have earned $30.
The good news is this ad can be shown to hundreds of thousands of people with similar interests replicating
the process and earning significant profits.

--- The essence of marketing promotion ---

Trivago is a website that allows visitors to search and compare hotel prices, read customer reviews
and browse photos.
The company claims to be the world's largest hotel search engine and helps customers compare prices
available in 250 booking sites.
Chances are they can save money comparing multiple listings.
The idea behind this product is original and complicated.
That's why the firm's marketing team has run integrated marketing campaigns, delivering the same message
to customers.
Our site allows you to save money when you book a hotel room.
TV ads show various steps of the process, which helps customers gain an idea of how to use the product.
Billboards and banners display an image of people who have booked rooms in the same hotel, but at different
prices.
This Berlin based firm also used radio ads that read Trivago does the Work for You and compares prices
of hundreds of websites.
So we can say Trivago used an integrated marketing approach.
Its promotional messages were consistent.
Its promotional messages were consistent across all advertising channels and share the same objective.
These efforts helped the brand establish an online presence and introduce itself to consumers across
the world.
If the company hadn't done that, millions of potential customers wouldn't have learned about its services,
as he probably guessed.
The fourth PPI we will examine now is promotion, which is essentially the art of communicating a product's
benefits to customers and convincing them to acquire the product once or ideally a repeated number of
times.
We already know the three degrees of brand loyalty, right awareness, preference and insistence.
Marketing communication revolves around the three.
Customers must know a product exists.
Once they do, we need to convince them it is better than competitor products so they can develop a
preference.
In the best case scenario.
Customers who prefer the product will understand its features well, which will develop informational
awareness and that will make them able to explain its benefits to other customers.
Continued purchasing of a product brand insistence is the goal of promotional communication.
It occurs when customers link a product with their own lifestyle.
The feeling of personal connection turns repeated customers into raving fans.
In the next lesson, we will talk about the five steps that precede the launch of a marketing campaign.
Thanks for watching.

--- Creating a marketing campaign ---

Effective communication is about conveying information.
This can happen only if the communicator and the receiver of information start from a common basis.
They need to use the same language, symbols or images.
Otherwise, recipients won't grasp the message.
Marketing campaigns are costly, and the last thing companies want to do is spend money for advertising
and communication.
That leaves consumers indifferent or puzzled.
So our goal here would be to study the five steps typically involved in building an effective communication
campaign.
First, we have to determine what the goal of the campaign is.
Do we want to attract new customers or do we want to build the awareness and preference of existing
customers?
The right message for the two groups would be different.
To gain new customers, we have to deliver a message that presents the brand and the product.
So at the very least, they know they exist.
Existing customers are familiar with the product.
They know how to use it, but we want to keep them around and if possible, increase their brand loyalty.
We need to communicate to them the amazing features of the product and remind them this is exactly what
they're looking for and don't need anything else.
The product can be shown as a lifestyle choice.
This is a message that can resonate particularly strongly within a specific target audience.
The second step consists of gaining an idea about the type of people we want to reach.
And these are the exact same people we had in mind when creating the product, the target audience.
If the target audience is made of multiple sub segments, then marketing managers must decide which
one they would like to address.
Once we know the goal of the campaign and its target audience, then it will be time to think of a message
that would resonate with that target audience.
The message must be customized in a way that is understandable, relatable and easy to remember.
Most companies hire experienced copywriters or agencies to come up with catchy marketing slogans.
So phase number three consists in coming up with the right message.
Once we're ready, it's time to select the Media Channel that would allow us to reach the target audience.
There are a few important parameters we need to consider.
First, the information we can transmit through a TV advertisement and a billboard is different.
The Media Channel must follow the objective of the campaign.
Second, we have to think about costs.
Some channels are very effective and allow you to deliver a message to millions of customers.
However, it is expensive to run a TV ad, for example.
Only large companies can afford to do that.
So we have to keep an eye on costs.
Metrics such as cost per viewer or cost per impression are helpful and facilitate the comparison of
advertising channels.
The third and probably most important variable when you have to select a type of media is if your target
audience consumes this type of content.
So, for example, Nike advertises on TV and chooses broadcasts of sports shows and sport games.
This is when their target audience is in front of TVs.
We have prepared a great downloadable PDF document which compares different communication channels that
can be used.
Please look at it once you complete this lesson.
Step number five consists of producing the marketing materials.
If you've chosen TV, you must shoot a TV commercial.
And if you've chosen billboards or newspapers, you'll need a designer who can create a beautiful poster.
Now, although most companies use the services of specialized agencies, this doesn't mean the marketing
team is not involved.
They have to give guidance and ensure the materials are in line with the message that must be delivered
to customers.

--- The importance of social media ---

Social media conversations are a great opportunity for companies that really want to connect with their
target audience.
Facebook, Twitter, LinkedIn, Google Plus, Pinterest, Ankara have become well established names
with hundreds of millions of active users.
People there gather in groups like popular pages and openly share their experiences about all aspects
of life.
And one of these aspects is the products they use.
Companies can ignore these conversations, listen actively, or even engage in them.
This allows them to a understand customers opinions and receive unbiased feedback, which is invaluable
from a marketing perspective, and b shaped the discussions of clients and clear their doubts or misunderstandings.
Every modern company should leverage its social media presence and invest to understand and influence
the conversation that occurs there.

--- The four Ps of Marketing - A dynamic concept ---

The only way a marketing plan can succeed is if the four P's are aligned and work in unison.
Their role is to implement the strategic direction the top management has chosen.
However, if one of the four P's is not in sync with the others, the entire plan becomes compromised.
Let's provide an example.
Hugo Boss sells high end men's and women's clothing.
Their products are superb and customers love the way they look when wearing them.
These are high end products.
The pricing range is high, but that's not surprising given the high quality products and the high propensity
to spend extra by the firm's customers.
Hugo Boss runs promo campaigns with world famous testimonials like Gerard Butler.
These advertisements are shown on TV and in magazines like Vogue and Men's Health.
That's great because the brand can reach target customers through these media channels and boost its
premium brand image.
Everything sounds great, right?
Product price and promo are synced in an excellent way.
Let's consider the fourth P place.
Let's imagine the following hypothetical situation.
The firm's marketing team sells boss clothes in cheaper locations, slightly outside of city centres.
What do you think will happen?
Well, it will lose its clients.
That's what's going to happen.
Premium customers are unwilling to look for top brands anywhere outside of top shopping streets and
luxurious shopping malls.
A shop in a cheaper area would send a mixed signal, which will confuse the clients.
Try to imagine it.
A superb product.
High prices and solid media presence.
Three strong signals matched with the shop outside of city centres in inexpensive areas right next to
discount stores.
This example is extreme, but it illustrates that all four PS must be integrated and directed in the
same direction.
Otherwise, a company's competitive model wouldn't succeed.
The four PS shouldn't be applied in a static way either.
There can be times when one changes.
Usually it will be the firm's product or the pricing of its products.
Maybe this is better.
Imagine you are a marketing manager in a company that produces air conditioners.
Your R&amp;D team has just let you know they've come up with an amazing breakthrough.
They have created an air conditioner that uses 50% less energy than any other air conditioner in the
marketplace.
Great.
Until this moment, your company strategy has been cost leadership.
But this new finding allows you to create a differentiated product.
So what are you going to do?
Tell the R&amp;D team their amazing breakthrough is not in line with the firm's strategy, even though it
might win the firm market share and allow it to charge more for its products.
And marketing studies indicate potential improvements of profitability.
Or perhaps you could readjust the four PS plan to correspond to a differentiated product that's going
to cost more, will be sold by premium resellers and will need an extensive advertising campaign.
The second scenario sounds better, right?
The four PS can be readjusted and need to be readjusted when one of the PS changes.
Think of the PS as an ecosystem that must adapt to a changing environment.
If one component changes, all four must evolve and adapt accordingly.

--- Allocating the funds available for Marketing - The budgeting exercise ---

The more you spend on marketing, the more customers will know and trust your brand.
However, besides marketing, companies must invest in production, administration, finance, customer
services, sales personnel, top management and so on.
So they have to allocate a certain amount of their resources on marketing.
There are two main ways to come up with a marketing budget, and you've heard the terms bottom up and
top down.
We briefly talked about them in earlier lectures.
In this case, bottom up consists of taking input from marketing teams at a micro level.
Each of these teams handles a certain revenue generating unit, can be a single product or product line.
They will have to indicate the amount of marketing spend needed to achieve planned revenues.
When the estimates of micro teams are aggregated, this gives us the bottom up marketing spend forecast.
As you would expect, top down is an estimate made by the firm's top management.
They'll typically model marketing expenses as a percentage of budgeted revenues, and then division
heads must allocate the budget to micro teams within the structure.
Once budgets have been assigned, the crucial element is how they'll be used by marketing managers.
A common mistake is to allocate resources to a large number of campaigns.
Instead, it is much better to run a few campaigns, but to do it in a powerful and concentrated way.

--- Using KPIs to improve decision-making ---

When can we say a marketing strategy is successful?
That it was worth spending an extra dime?
Easy when there are the numbers to prove it.
Marketing managers should aim to monitor all their decisions through key performance indicators abbreviated
as KPIs.
A well designed KPI is easy to measure and is meaningful.
Focusing on the right KPIs allows a firm to continue successful practices and take corrective measures
in areas that need it.
So, for example, a marketing manager will track sales, but they should also be interested in sales
by distribution channel, by key account, by geography and even by sales employee.
That's a great way to gain insight about where the top line growth comes from and where we should be
worried about problematic performance.
In the same way, a marketing manager will track cost per sales of these breakdowns and gross profit
percentages, which would explain the profitability of each of these segments.
A marketing manager is not only interested in sales as that's just the tip of the iceberg.
They need a holistic approach monitoring every step of the way.
Other KPIs traditionally measured our market share, brand awareness, customer satisfaction, customer
retention and churn out average spend per customer, customer lifetime value and so on.
Without KPIs, all discussions in a firm will be purely qualitative.
There wouldn't be a method to quantify if a certain course of action succeeds.
Everyone would have an opinion and decisions would be taken in a subjective manner.
Numbers help us prevent that, as it is very difficult to argue against quantitative trends.
KPIs may vary as they are specific from firm to firm, but the main message is that successful marketing
managers track their results through a KPI because it will be what shows them if they've made the right
decisions.
Nobody makes perfect decisions the first time.
Please remember that the difference between average and outstanding managers is in the speed with which
they correct their mistakes.

--- Short-term vs. Long-term marketing goals ---

One of the most challenging aspects of marketing is holding the right balance between short term goals
and long term plans.
That's difficult because marketing is one of the few levers the top managers have when they want to
push sales numbers higher in a short time frame.
And this happens all the time.
Trust me, it could happen at the end of a month, at the end of a quarter, and especially at the end
of a year.
Higher sales are a benchmark of how well a company is doing, and top managers can be tempted to push
for heavy discounts, higher advertising spend, special selling efforts and sales practices oriented
towards short term sales instead of long term gains in loyal customers and brand equity.
Warren Buffett is famous for saying it takes 30 years to build a reputation and 5 minutes to destroy
one.
This is valid for people, but it's also true about brands.
Imagine what would happen if Prada offered its bags for $300 instead of $1,000 and more.
Short term sales will increase enormously and the firm will probably have higher overall profit margins.
However, this will come at a cost.
Prada would suffer in the long run as premium clients wouldn't consider the brand as elite anymore.
A pricing signal that makes the brand accessible for more people takes from its appeal to this target
market.
And it is likely Prada will lose its high paying customers, the ones that are most valuable in the
long run.
So we have to be aware of this issue and try to balance the two goals.
Marketing managers must protect the value of their brands in the long run while working with the rest
of the organization when trying to improve current results.
They are the ones who should ring the bell when having concerns that a promotion, an advertising campaign,
a pricing decision or any other marketing decision could hurt the brand over the long haul.
Growing a brand in the long run requires constant efforts and a lot of work.
Some companies have succeeded in doing that.
Red Bull is a great example of a company that has built its brand with consistent efforts.
The company has hosted extreme sport events all over the world from the Red Bull, Indianapolis, to
Felix Baumgartner's jump from the edge of space.
Red Bull is excited about action from the world of adventure sports, freestyle, motocross, base jumping,
wingsuit flying, free skiing, downhill mountain biking, all of that.
And they go to great lengths to show it.
Red Bull not only sponsors events, but creates live streaming of these events, shows them on Red Bull
TV and helps with media coverage.
The brand connects with sport event fans and can build a relationship with them.
They've been doing this for more than a decade.
Advertising spots have promoted Red Bull as the drink that makes you fly, and advertising clips have
reiterated the same message.
Red Bull gives you wings.
The impact of consistent campaigns is even greater as more people notice and get accustomed to the message.
Creating a recognizable brand is a long term exercise, which is hard but very rewarding and makes the
difference between champion companies with a loyal customer base and ones that are in second choice.
It takes time before a message gets to people, but once it does, it stays with them.
And that's very valuable.
How valuable exactly, though?
Well, that's what we will discuss in our next lesson.
Thanks for watching.

--- Interpreting and calculating the Customer-Lifetime-Value formula ---

One of the most important metrics that helps entrepreneurs, managers and marketers when they have to
make important business decisions is customer lifetime value.
It is a projection estimating a customer's monetary worth.
Considering the expected number of years, the customers will buy products from the firm.
Many formulas are used by practitioners to calculate customer lifetime value, but the easiest to understand
is the following.
First, we have to subtract the variable cost from a product selling price, which gives us the margin
we can expect from a single sale.
And then this number must be multiplied by the number of repeat purchases per year and by the average
number of years customers are retained.
Let's provide an example.
If a company sells water with a unitary sales price of $0.60 per bottle and $0.25 of variable costs,
we will have a margin of $0.35 per bottle.
A loyal customer who buys 200 bottles per year and remains a client for five years is worth $350 to
the firm.
Customer Lifetime value.
Simply known as CLV, allows us to make better decisions about sales, marketing, product development
and customer support.
It tells us the most profitable client relationships and what to do to serve these clients better.
It allows managers to quantify these relationships and focus on preserving long term value.
CLV illustrates a customer that buys the firm's products over and over can be more valuable than somebody
who spends more, but does it only once.
In our example, imagine a second customer pays $0.80 per bottle and buys 220 bottles per year.
The main difference is that because he is not loyal to a particular brand of water and switch away pretty
quickly, they're expected to remain a client only for the next two years.
Let's calculate his CLV.
The margin per bottle from this client is higher $0.55, and they buy more bottles every year, 220
instead of 200.
They're expected to be a customer for the next two years, which gives us a CLV of $242.
The moral of the story is that continued business over the long term can be more valuable than a few
sales with higher unitary margins.
CLV is a function that depends largely on three variables the unitary margin per each sale, the frequency
of purchases, and the length of the client firm relationship.
This is an invaluable tool as it helps us understand how much can be invested in marketing to acquire
a new client.
If a new client is worth $400, you don't want to spend more than that to win them over as a customer.
That would mean losing money, right?
And at the same time, CLV gives an idea of how much is reasonable to spend.
Another benefit of calculating CLV is it helps companies define profitable client segments and stimulates
them to retain these clients.
And it helps firms discontinue business with client groups that are not as profitable or even loss making.
One way to do this is to differentiate the offer for unprofitable client groups and even charge them
higher.
In this process, sales reps will know where to concentrate their efforts, and the entire organization
will be aligned on the type of clients that should be taken on board.

--- Case study: Coca-Cola and Christmas ---

COLA is one of the most iconic global brands, and people all over the world are familiar with them.
This is no small part due to their hugely influential marketing campaigns, the most grandiose of these
easily being their Christmas advertisements.
It all started in the 1920s and continues up until this day for almost 100 years.
It isn't surprising that many of us remember at least a few of Coca-Cola's TV spots that aired throughout
the years.
The famous Coca-Cola Truck The Holidays Are Coming song.
My days are coming holidays and let's not forget the image of Santa Claus that is associated with this
festive season is the one depicted in Coke's advertising campaigns.
The first time Santa was featured in an ad run by Coca-Cola was in the 1920s.
At the time, television was as popular as virtual reality is today.
It existed, but was still in its infancy.
This was the beginning of what we've come to know as Christmas and Santa Claus.
Before that, old Saint Nick had a totally different image.
Santa Claus, Father Christmas, or how he was originally known.
Saint Nicholas was a monk believed to be born around 280 C.E., rumored to have given his wealth to
the poor.
Nicholas became known as the protector of children and sailors.
The anniversary of his death on December 6th became a lucky day to get married or make large purchases
like 4K TVs.
Because of this, Nicholas kept his popularity and a reputation, especially in the Netherlands.
The stories of Santa Claus, a shortened form of Saint Nicholas Dutch for Saint Nicholas, continued.
And when they reach New York, the more familiar Santa Claus started to develop, thanks to the newspaper
stories and Christmas shopping and marketing from the malls.
But it's not until the 1920s when Coca-Cola decided to use the recently reimagined Santa Claus in their
Christmas marketing.
Coke had come a long way since its first introduction by John Pemberton, who initially invented a wine,
coca, cocaine, wine.
But after Prohibition was passed in Atlanta in 1886, he developed a non-alcoholic version called Coca
Cola.
Pemberton sold the drink as a patent medicine, which claimed to cure many diseases, including morphine
addiction, indigestion, headaches and impotence.
Obviously, all this was backed up by rigorous scientific studies like the ones performed on the benefits
of snake oil.
For the next couple of decades after Pemberton's death in 1888, the Coca-Cola Company had started its
path to global domination.
Of course, there were plenty of company struggles right off the bat.
The company was split between different manufacturers.
There were buyouts, naming rights and a bunch of other things you'd expect with a company with this
potential.
But obviously it wasn't squabbling between business people that convince the masses to drink a fizzy
brown goo.
It was marketing, and Coca-Cola got this right from the very beginning.
Being the first company to offer free coupons, the company used mostly smartly dressed young women,
often models and celebrities to sell its drink.
Add this to a growing marketplace and plenty of billboards and posters.
And the Coca-Cola Company had found its way into the hands of the masses.
But by 1920, it was time for the Coca-Cola Company to take on the big guns.
The Christmas market, despite popular belief Santa Claus and his more modern look was not first used
by Coca-Cola, but in fact, by white rock beverages for their mineral water and ginger ale.
But let's face it, it's not about doing it first.
It's about doing it best.
And once Coca-Cola had got their hands on the big guy in red, nobody else dared touch him.
The first Coke Christmas ad featured a sterner looking Santa, more akin to how he had been portrayed
in the artwork of the time by artists like Thomas Nast, who originally envisioned Saint Nick in his
bright red suit.
So although it wasn't Coca-Cola who created this more modern looking Santa, they certainly have taken
their new mascot to a whole new level and introduced us to some sidekicks along the way, starting with
the polar bears who were introduced in 1922 in France.
This is Coca-Cola doing what it does best, evoking emotion in the thirties.
Don Sunbeam took the strict Santa from the previous decade and turned him into the warm, friendly,
plump, jolly Santa we associate with Christmas today.
Based on sun blooms neighbour and the imagery of the 1822 poem Twas the Night Before Christmas, this
new and improved Santa captured the spirit of Christmas and was used as the standard Santa for all interpretations,
from book to film to TV.
With this Santa, Coca-Cola had practically glued their brand to Christmas by sending the messages of
family, togetherness, joy and diversity.
Coca-Cola was establishing themselves as a lifestyle accompaniment rather than a product, and its marketing
carried on this way for years, targeting specific audiences, telling stories, creating nostalgia
and giving.
Variances.
Then they introduce Sprite Boy, a terrifying manchild with a painful looking bottle cap for a hat to
represent Coca Cola and Coke being two separate trademarks.
Whatever his purpose was, we should just be glad he's not used anymore.
Coca-Cola's tradition of bringing the communal feeling and the festive spirit continued for decades
with sun blooms final version of Santa created in the sixties being the standard.
Coca Cola faced a relative rough patch in terms of brand strength in the eighties because of some unusual
marketing decisions like New Coke and added pressure from competition like Pepsi.
To counter this, the nineties saw an incredibly strong comeback, especially with their Christmas marketing.
We saw the return of the polar bears promoting a strong sense of togetherness.
And showing that no matter how cold it is, Coke is still a good choice of drink.
After the polar bears came, the Coca Cola trucks, otherwise known as Christmas Caravan, came over
the hill.
No.
These portrayed a new message that Christmas doesn't arrive until Coke says it does.
Up to this day, many say that Christmas doesn't start until they see the Coca-Cola truck advert.
For the first time, the holidays are coming.
It took a century of being ahead of the game in terms of marketing, but it all came together to create
a world where Coca-Cola and Christmas are ingrained in people's minds.
They figured out what it means to give people an experience instead of a product.
Coca-Cola really is battling on two fronts the family and community side of Christmas with the polar
bears and the excitement and magic of Christmas with the caravan, added some gimmicks like the bow
bottle and a physical truck tour of the UK.
And you can see Coca-Cola's Christmas dominance shows no signs of disappearing any time soon.
Even if Pepsi does give it a good go.

--- Intro to Tesla marketing ---

Over the next several lessons, we'll analyze Tesla's marketing mix decisions and the four P's framework
will help us do that.
I'm sure you're familiar with it.
The four P's are product, price, place and promotion, and it is crucial that choice is made in each
of these areas, are in sync and correspond to the overall competitive strategy the firm has chosen
to pursue going forward.
In Tesla's case, we have defined a differentiated strategy.
Therefore, we'll expect to see a high quality, differentiated product with premium pricing and sold
in a way that elevates its status.
Also, a brand image and social status that help clients justify the premium price they have paid for
their Tesla products.
In the next, several lessons will analyze each of these aspects.

--- Tesla's segmentation problem ---

Tesla has been very successful in the EV market over the last few years.
A lot of this has to do with the innovative and well-performing Model S and Model X vehicles.
But also one could easily point out there isn't much competition in the electric car scene.
This is about to change.
BMW has already declared that they will be releasing electric versions of all of their core models over
the next few years.
Other auto producers will do that, too.
Over the next several years, we will see more and more traditional brands will start releasing electric
versions of their existing cars.
When that happens, Tesla will likely find itself with a problem on its hands.
Segmentation.
Right now, Tesla sells three cars Model S, Model X and Model three.
The next automobile to be released in late 2019 or more likely at the beginning of 2020 is Model Y a
crossover?
So a person who wants to buy a Tesla doesn't really have that much choice.
They'll have to select one of the three existing models.
However, traditional auto producers operate under the tested assumption that different people want
to drive different cars in terms of size, price, functionalities and design.
This is the reason why successful brands like BMW have several models in their crossover and SUV line
x1x2x3x4x5
and x six.
The arrival of such vehicles on the market could represent a problem for Tesla because clients are used
to plenty of choice when buying an automobile and Tesla wouldn't be able to differentiate its offering
and offer such made to measure solutions for a long time.
To increase its offering, the company would need to invest in a new factory.
Given that the Fremont plant would be at full capacity producing Model S, Model X and Model three.
However, at the moment, Tesla can't really afford that as it continues to burn cash from a cash flow
perspective.
Raising capital would be problematic because Tesla's stock price has been very volatile and it is likely
that the market wouldn't react well.
It is yet to be seen how fast traditional auto producers will introduce their electric vehicles to the
market.
And once this starts happening, we'll see whether the electric automobile industry will mimic what
we have already seen in the traditional car market.
Nevertheless, when considering Tesla and its future competitive strategy, we have to be aware that
product segmentation is a potential problem for the company in the short and mid-term.
They will likely have to come up with some solution that allows for a more differentiated offering in
terms of size, performance and design.

--- A proof of Tesla's brand strength ---

A great testament to the strength of Tesla's brand is the fact that the company has a successful track
record of selling lifestyle products.
In the summer of 2018, Tesla sold 200 surfboards, priced at $4,500.
All of them were gone in a day.
Later on in September 2018, Tesla presented its wireless phone charger and it also sold out in a day.
Other examples of merchandise sold by Tesla are desktop Superchargers.
Portable Chargers.
Tesla Model S for kids.
1 to 18 scale models of Tesla Model S Tesla mugs steal water bottles, jackets, caps, you name it.
As usual, Tesla doesn't simply sell merchandise to its fans.
It gives them a reason to become even more passionate about the company.
Selling caps, jackets and regular merchandise items is something many firms do.
There aren't that many firms selling surfboards painted with car paint.
Such witty, cool and different offerings is one of the main reasons why Tesla has such a strong brand
following.
In addition, selling merchandise helps the company generate some extra revenue and finance its mission
to make the world a more sustainable place.
There are many customers who are passionate about Tesla's brand and are touched by its products, mission
and identity when managed right.
Such customer affiliation is a great source of value in the long run.

--- Tesla's pricing ---

Pricing is one of the most important variables in the four PS mix.
If it isn't consistent with the overall competitive strategy chosen by the firm, then the entire business
is at risk because inconsistent signals sent to customers are detrimental.
Tesla doesn't have such a problem though.
We already discussed that in our lesson about its target market.
If Tesla wants to establish itself as a company with a differentiated product, then it should continue
charging premium prices as it currently does.
It is yet to be seen how Tesla's prices will be perceived by competitors once premium brands like BMW,
Mercedes and Porsche enter the market.
But currently, it appears that customers are willing to pay a premium price for the firm's products.

--- Before or after savings? ---

Tesla's pricing positions, the company as a premium brand.
We are clear on that.
Our task in this lesson would be to explore the company's website and see how pricing is formulated.
Here I am.
I've opened Tesla dot com in my browser.
I would like to order model three.
And we'll opt for the dual motor all wheel drive, which comes at $41,200.
As you can see below, a standard model three battery will be available in 4 to 6 months.
However, this is what I wanted to show you.
The price is listed as $41,200 after incentives and gas savings.
And then it is also shown as 53,000 before any of these savings.
There is room for confusion, but let's continue with the process.
Hoping Tesla has done a nice job of explaining an $11,800 difference.
We are asked to select an exterior color.
I'll go with a three, six, five careers like blue.
I'll also opt for a white interior, which adds another $1,000 to the price.
That's fine, though.
This is the definition of imaginary money well spent.
Autopilot costs 5000.
This is Tesla's state of the art self-driving software.
In addition, a scarcity technique has been applied.
The current $5,000 price is available at this very moment.
Only if you decide you would like the feature later on, then its price would be 7000.
I'll include autopilot and we'll continue forward.
Okay.
So I'm required to provide some personal information before moving ahead with the purchase.
However, there are two prices on the screen which continues to confuse me a price that includes savings
and a price that doesn't include such savings.
Lets select show details and try to understand what is the difference between the two prices.
The price of the model three version we ordered is $60,500.
This is the purchase price.
However, the people at Tesla wanted to show us the following.
The federal tax credit, which is still available, is a potential saving of $7,500.
