--- Who ensures the numbers in financial statements have been prepared correctly? ---

Okay.
Very well.
Now that we've seen what a complete annual report looks like, it would be great to learn some more
about how it is prepared.
Every company has employees who are responsible for gathering its financial information and using it
in order to produce useful financial statements.
In our second lesson, we said that financial statements are prepared according to a specific set of
rules, local accounting principles, or international accounting standards.
But who oversees whether companies do in fact, respect these guidelines and do not manipulate their
financial statements?
This is the task of auditing firms.
Since its introduction, the need for certain companies financial statements to be audited by an independent
external auditor has been a cornerstone of confidence in the world's financial systems.
The benefit of an audit is that it provides assurance that management has presented a true and fair
view of the company's financial performance and position.
Companies produce financial statements that provide information about their financial position and performance.
This information is used by a wide range of stakeholders in making economic decisions.
Typically, those that own a company, the shareholders are not the ones managing the company.
Therefore, the owners of these companies, as well as other stakeholders such as banks, suppliers
and customers take comfort from independent assurance that the financial statements fairly represent
the company's financial position and performance.
To enhance the degree of confidence in the financial statements, a qualified external party or auditor
is engaged to examine the financial statements, including related disclosures produced by management.
In many cases, this is required by law.
All financial statements include a note from their auditor which certifies or doesn't the credibility
of the figures within the report.
This is an important topic because it helps us realize what is behind the final figures that we see
in the financial statements of large companies.

--- The four dimensions of financial statement analysis ---

Welcome back to the next chapter of this course.
You've now learned about the three core financial statements about the accounting equation.
You know what general ledgers and accounts are.
You are capable of using the double entry principle.
And on top of that you can construct a balance sheet and an income statement.
Additionally, you are well aware that revenue and expense recognition may have a different timing than
cash payments.
We are making excellent progress.
So congratulations.
These skills alone are what any firm would expect you to know if you start as, say, a junior accountant.
However, in this chapter, we're going to delve a little further into financial statement analysis.
Essentially, financial statement analysis is a continuation of accounting that uses the numbers that
were registered in order to produce meaningful information and provide some less obvious insights into
the company's business.
After we've created a balance sheet and an income statement, we can start thinking about financial
statement analysis.
Its main purpose is the assessment of the performance and soundness of four critical dimensions growth,
liquidity, profitability, solvency, or said.
With other words, our goal would be to answer the questions Is the company growing?
Does the company have sufficient liquidity in order to repay its short term liabilities?
Is the profit that is being earned satisfactory?
Is the company able to face its liabilities?
Sounds interesting, right?
The people who say accounting is boring are usually the ones who don't understand it.

--- Calculating subtotals, totals and year-on-year growth ---

A subtotal is the sum of two or more items.
Usually the items are summed because they belong to the same category.
The subtotal is then summed with other subtitles and we subsequently arrive to the total.
For example, the subtotal that you see here aggregates the group of fixed assets.
This subtotal is later summed with other subtitles in order to arrive to total assets.
Subtitles are useful because they help us organize more clearly the financial statements that we are
using.
Calculating growth percentage.
We want to understand how certain drivers changed historically because history can be a good indicator
of the future.
In order to calculate the year on year growth that was experienced by a given parameter, we can use
the following formula current value divided by previous value minus one.
In the example that you see, we would have the value for 2012 divided by the value for 2011 minus one.
This is the growth that we realized going from 2011 to 2012.
Quite intuitively, when we want to increase the value by a percentage, we can multiply the previous
value by one plus the growth percentage.

--- The mechanics of financial statement analysis ---

So the first thing that we learned about financial statement analysis was that it would allow us to
improve our understanding of a given business.
Now we'll go a step further and describe how this type of analysis is actually performed.
We should be aware that no effective analysis can be carried out without thoroughly understanding the
underlying business of the company under consideration.
It is mirrored in the financial statements.
So a good understanding of financial statements is based on a deep understanding of the typical transactions
performed by the company.
This has a direct impact on the way we analyse financial statements.
For example, some transactions are classified as operating in some companies and as financial in other
companies because the two companies have different underlying businesses.
Therefore, the understanding of a company's business is a crucial factor that will help us perform
a meaningful analysis.
But how do we analyze the company that we are interested in?
Let's say that we have its income statement and balance sheet for the last three years.
That's very good.
But what do we do with them?
That's a valid question.
Financial statement analysis is based mainly on comparison.
Obviously, we can compare the same entity over time, given that we have three years of historical
data.
We will learn about the behavior of revenues, expenses and other items over the course of time.
This will help us understand where the business now is with respect to where it was two years ago.
A lot of investors are primarily focused on growth potential rather than current earnings.
We can also choose to compare the firm with different entities from its industry and industry.
Analysis consists of comparing the firm's performance against its main competitors or comparing it with
an industry average.
This would allow us to understand a great deal about the business under consideration.
Is it growing faster than the rest of the market?
Is it able to charge higher prices?
Does it have superior profitability?
Is the business operating efficiently in terms of working capital investments?
Is the business using excessive leverage?
Indeed.
This is certainly an interesting comparison to be made.
The truth of the matter is that practitioners rarely use only one of these two types of analysis.
Their contemporaneous application is highly recommended.
In our next lessons, we'll be much more practical.
The real world examples that we'll examine will provide you with some valuable hands on experience.

--- Using Different P&L and Balance Sheet Formats + Horizontal and Vertical Analysis ---

Great.
So now we know what sub totals and totals in a financial statement are and we also know how to calculate
year on year growth.
This is already a good start that will ease our work.
The next thing will mention are financial statement formats.
When we talk about different formats, we mean different ways to arrange the items inside the balance
sheet and the income statement.
Please remember that there is no rule regulating how to organize the formats of financial statements.
What is important is to remain consistent with names and definitions and to use a format that describes
the business under consideration.
Well, for example, for some businesses, it may be more appropriate to use an income statement format
that shows a gross profit subtotal that is given by revenues minus cost of goods sold.
Other businesses, on the other hand, may not use this format and will directly show operating profit,
also known as EBITDA and so on.
The decision is up to the company's management and should be taken in a way that best represents the
underlying business.
A popular balance sheet format is the one grouping assets and liabilities according to their maturity,
current and non-current.
However, there are also some other formats.
Analysts often organise the balance sheets that they are using by separating operating from non-operating
assets and liabilities.
Now let's suppose that we've chosen the correct format and we are ready to start carrying out our analysis.
There are two main types of analysis that we can perform horizontal and vertical.
Horizontal analysis represents the comparison of historical financial information over a series of reporting
periods.
The main idea is to see if any numbers are unusually high or low with respect to the information about
previous periods.
For example, we can wonder how much the total revenues increased in percentage compared to the ones
observed a year ago.
Horizontal analysis is also known as trend analysis because it shows the development of a given parameter
over time.
On the other hand, vertical analysis involves the numbers from the same accounting period.
Each item is divided into a specified base, which is the 100% figure in the balance sheet.
This base is usually the value of total assets while in the income statement.
This is the total revenues figure.
As you can see in this example, the gross profit, operating income and net income are divided by total
revenues, which allows us to understand what percentage of revenues they represent.
Then once we've done that, we can compare these figures with the company's peers or with its historical
data and make observations that are based on the development of its financials.
So now that we know about different income statement and balance sheet formats and about horizontal
and vertical analysis, we are ready to delve into another interesting topic ratio analysis.

--- Understanding how to calculate "Days": DSO, DPO, and DIO ---

Hi and welcome back.
In this video, we will learn more about the day's methodology, which is used to represent some balance
sheet items.
The days calculation indicates how many days are necessary in order to cash receivables, pay trade
payables, or that an item leaves the warehouse of the firm.
Days sales outstanding DSO.
Days payable outstanding DPO and days inventory outstanding di0.
R Working Capital Components.
DSO is a measure of the average number of days that a company takes to collect revenue after a sale
has been made.
A low DSO number means that a company needs fewer days in order to collect its accounts receivables.
A high DSO number shows that a firm is selling its products to customers on credit and taking longer
to collect money.
DPO is a company's average payable period.
Days payable outstanding tells how long does it take for a company to pay its invoices from trade creditors
such as suppliers?
D IO is a financial measure of a company's performance that gives investors an idea of how long it takes
a company to turn its inventory into sales.

--- Financial ratios that are helpful to investors ---

Congratulations on making it this far.
You've covered a huge amount.
You've learned about the three main financial statements, the key income statement and balance sheet
accounts, the core principles of bookkeeping, the double entry principle, the accounting equation.
And you know how to work with T accounts and what a debit and credit is.
And you've also learned about financial statement analysis, horizontal and vertical analysis, and
much more.
The next two videos are really the last piece of the puzzle.
We'll be looking at ratio analysis.
Its key goal is to analyze four main dimensions liquidity.
Solvency.
Profitability.
And growth.
These are, in fact, the four key topics that need to be addressed when analyzing a given business.
Liquidity is the firm's capability to pay its short term obligations.
If we say that a company is liquid, this usually means that a significant portion of its assets are
easily convertible in cash.
Meanwhile, solvency indicates a company's ability to meet its long term obligations.
If a company finances a high portion of its assets with financial debt, then it runs the risk of having
financial difficulties due to increasing interest expenses.
Profitability ratios are important for every company as it has to produce sufficient profits that will
satisfy its employees and owners.
Given that there can be several drivers that determine whether a company is profitable, it is common
practice to break down some of the profitability ratios into smaller ingredients.
Growth ratios provide insights about the speed at which a company's revenues, margins, profits and
assets are growing.
So these are the four main dimensions.
Let's show a few ratios that are used in order to analyze each of these important dimensions.
Some of the most common liquidity indicators are the current ratio and the net trade cycle calculation.
The current ratio is given by the fraction between short term assets and short term liabilities.
It allows us to have an idea about the company's ability to pay its short term liabilities.
The recommended value for this ratio varies according to the industry in which the company operates.
However, a ratio above two is usually considered good.
The net trade cycle shows how many days it takes for a company to convert resources that it uses in
the production of its goods into cash, also known as the cash conversion cycle.
This calculation measures the amount of time for which each dollar is tied up in the production and
sales process before it is converted into cash through sales to customers.
In order to calculate the net trade cycle, we need to calculate DSO.
Dio and DPO.
Calculating the net trade cycle is easy once we have DSO, DIO and DPO.
It is given by DSO plus DIO, minus DPO.
This calculation would allow us to understand whether the firm is efficient in its use of capital.
A peer comparison is always helpful in such situations.
The main ratios used to evaluate solvency are the debt ratio, also known as leverage and the interest
coverage ratio.
The debt ratio shows what portion of a company's assets is financed with debt in order to calculate
it.
We need to divide total liabilities into total assets.
Of course, the lower this is, the more sustainable the business under consideration.
For many industries, a value of up to 0.67 is acceptable.
However, you should always compare the ratio with an industry average and avoid applying it mechanically.
When a company's debt increases, it has to pay higher interest expenses.
Right.
Financial institutions always want to leave a cushion, which is why they often measure the proportion
between a company's operating profits, EBIT and its interest expenses.
If it is higher than two, then this means that the company will be able to pay its interest expenses
and will likely be able to repay some of the principal.
Two.
Alternatively, a ratio below one shows that a company is unable to repay even its interest expenses
and will produce losses for its shareholders.
There are many profitability ratios, but we will concentrate on just a few.
ROA means return on assets.
It is given by EBIT divided by total assets.
This is an indicator of the profitability of the company without considering the way in which its activities
were financed.
Roae, on the other hand, stands for Return on Equity and is given by net income divided by shareholder's
equity.
It shows how much profit was generated by a company with the money that shareholders invested in its
activities.
The ROE ratio can be decomposed.
Take a look at the following formula.
It shows us that ROE is equal to the following three components.
ROA the ratio that we saw before multiplied by total assets over owner's equity multiplied by net income
over EBIT.
Each of the three components points to a different aspect of the company's structure.
ROA is a measure of profitability that does not consider financial structure.
Then we have the debt to equity ratio, which is an indicator of the company's financial structure,
showing the ratio between external and own financing.
And finally, the third ratio incorporates the impact of non-operating items, financial items, taxes,
extraordinary items on the company's profitability.
Finally, we have growth ratios.
Examples of these ratios are change of sales ratio, growth in total assets, etc. We already saw how
to calculate these changes.
The calculation of revenues growth can be useful because it helps us understand how fast a company is
growing and to compare its growth with its peers.
This is one of the crucial aspects that determines how interesting the business is going to be to investors.
Okay.
Wonderful.
We learned more about the various types of ratios, and I think that now is the best time for a practical
exercise.
In our next lesson, we'll calculate each of these ratios for one of the largest companies in the world,
P&amp;G.

--- Intro to Negotiation ---

Hi and well done on making it to the negotiation part of this course.
Negotiation is a core skill which has an impact on almost every area of your life from personal relationships,
like talking to your spouse or peers, to your professional development and career advancement.
Here, we will teach you the fundamentals of negotiation, and we'll cover tactics and tools.
We will show you negotiation is not a skill few are born with, but an expertise you can acquire and
perfect through practice and research.
In the next several lessons, we will talk about why negotiation is a core management skill and how
to avoid the most common pitfalls that make people ineffective negotiators.
Then we will move on to the must have preparation.
Before you sit at the negotiation table, you will learn about concepts such as Batten and reservation
point.
Mastering the preparation will then enable us to discuss the different types of negotiation win, lose,
win, win and their mix and delve deeper into the most effective strategies for each one.
Of course, we won't miss out on core techniques like anchoring and framing and more context specific
angling methods like future value focus or the nibble.

at the negotiation table and how to deal with broken trust.

--- Why is negotiation a core managerial skill? ---

Let's begin simply negotiation is at its core, a means to an end.
We engage in negotiation when we must make a decision, but our decision making authority is limited,
and we cannot do it without consulting other parties.
Negotiation does not only happen in the world of business, it is necessary and always present in personal
relationships and the humdrum of day to day life.
It can be one on one or involve multiple people, companies, countries, and so on.
So who needs to know how to negotiate effectively?
Simple.
Everyone.
But having the tools and techniques to reach an agreement on favorable terms is especially important
in a business setting where results are translated into their monetary value.
Consider the dynamic of the business world today.
There is constant turbulence in fierce competition in almost every market, and situations and circumstances
change on a daily basis.
We can, in fact identify four prime reasons why a successful manager needs to be an effective negotiator.
Naturally, dealing with competition heads our list to ensure a business continues to grow and accumulates
revenue.
A manager needs to navigate a landscape of competitors.
They must be advocates for their products and make sure they stay informed about the alternatives on
the market.
Just for perspective, over 10,000 FMCG businesses operate in the United States.
Do you think there is enough space on the supermarket shelves for all of them?
Learning how to stay on top of your negotiation game is essential when dealing with clients and suppliers.
Now think about constancy and security in the world of business.
It's not very high, right?
The current reality is that people are mobile and flexible and a venture manager must understand this.
Studies show the average millennial will change jobs four times in the first ten years after graduation.
This means managers must constantly create new possibilities, negotiate higher up and lower down for
positions, and find ways to fill them.
Managers are in round the clock negotiation mode and must be good at it.
But as you will learn in the next lessons, this is often not the case.
In addition, if you consider the structure within a company, both hierarchically and in terms of the
relationship between employees, you will notice people are becoming more and more dependent on each
other.
As teams become more diversified and expertise is more narrowly defined.
Employees need constant feedback and the terms of projects and agreements must be negotiated.
It is the manager's duty to facilitate this.
They must be able to promote the interests and well-being of the members of the company, while at the
same time making sure this won't hurt the financial results.
To that end, a manager needs to be well versed in various negotiation strategies and tactics and able
to communicate effectively and exert influence.
Okay.
So we can conclude the business culture today makes it imperative for a manager or any motivated individual
to understand the art of negotiation, to be able to productively interact within between and across
different organizational environments.
Awesome.
Now join us in the next lesson to learn why this is often not the case and why some people struggle
with negotiating.
Thanks for watching.

--- Why are people bad negotiators? ---

In our previous video we mentioned people are often not effective when negotiating.
Executives at various management levels make no exception.
Often they are unable to perform well at the negotiating table.
What is even more interesting is that when asked to evaluate their negotiation ability, the large majority
of them claim to be good negotiators when only a fraction managed to reach Win-Win resolutions when
tested.
So what happens when people underperform during negotiations?
They typically end up either leaving money on the table or settling for too little.
They might choose to walk away from the table without realizing that whatever is being offered is miles
better than any alternative.
Or they do the opposite.
Settle for terms a much worse than the alternative.
In any case, the negotiator walks away without realizing the potential of the negotiation and certainly
without reaching a win win outcome.
Why does this happen?
There are three problems everyone is susceptible to.
You may call them the human curse.
These are being egocentric.
Letting bias cloud your judgments and satisficing.
Let's discuss these one by one.
Egocentrism refers to the fact that people like to consider their own experiences as better than they
in fact are, and in a themselves flattering way.
For example, if a class of MBA students were asked to rank their problem solving skills, most of them
will say that they are better than the average MBA student.
This is by default, not possible.
Susceptibility to bias is another reason why people fall short on the negotiation table.
Often, when considering their performance during negotiations, people choose to pay more attention
to instances where they did well and completely ignore areas they might need to improve.
They're only interested in the information that would confirm any beliefs they already hold and not
dispute them.
Although this may seem like a problem that does not have an impact on negotiation outcomes, it does.
The consequences of bias show themselves in the longer run because having a favourable view of yourself
makes you evaluate situations incorrectly and feeds the belief you know a lot and therefore do not need
to improve.
Another thing about positive bias is it nudges the negotiator to underestimate the importance of background
research on their counterpart and lack of information about the players in a deal leads to relying on
intuition during the negotiation and improvising, which are dangerously ineffective methods for reaching
a win win.
Negotiations are a systematic process that demands an adequate level of preparation.
To be successful as a negotiator, you must be informed and able to anticipate your opponent's actions
rather than be uninformed and waiting to react to what they do and allow them to be the ones setting
the pace.
So pay attention to how you perceive yourself in the environment and try to correct any counterproductive
ideas.
Okay, so far so good.
The third instance of the human curse is satisficing.
This is not as complicated as it sounds.
Satisficing is essentially the opposite of optimizing.
If optimising is adapting to the situation and trying to gain as much as possible.
Then Satisficing is settling for less than what you could otherwise have.
When a person satisfies, they fail to take advantage of the situation, and usually both bargaining
parties suffer some loss as a result.
Don't worry if the mechanics are a bit unclear at this point.
We will outline the whys and the house of the situation in the next few lessons.
And since now you know what makes for a lousy negotiator, we can begin discussing how to become a great
one.
Awesome.
In the next video, we will teach you about the banana, one of the most important tools you can have
in your negotiation toolbox.
See you then.
And thanks for watching.

--- Understanding BATNA ---

Okay.
So there are a few critical components to a successful negotiation which are often not considered by
the bargainer, the BATNA, the reservation point and the bargaining range.
Here we will focus on the Betina.
The best alternative to a negotiated agreement or a partner is the most advantageous course of action
a negotiator can take if they're at an impasse with their bargaining partner and cannot reach an agreement.
This is an alternative course of action you can take, and it is necessarily outside of the negotiations.
It is not a bottom line or the least favorable terms you're willing to agree to.
The banana is a way out from a bad deal.
Think of it as having a side deal.
It's something that can act as a leverage point and as an insurance policy.
The core utility of a banana is it protects you from accepting an unfavourable deal.
The rule of thumb is accept any set of terms that are better than the banana and reject any offer that
is worse.
It is a precious value compass.
There are three steps to determining what your best alternative is.
First, you must make a list of the courses of action you can take if you fail to reach an agreement.
Then you must look through the list and select several of the more promising ideas and make them concrete,
improve them so they become tangible and coherent options, which you can realize if it comes to it.
Finally, choose the option which makes most sense financially and considering future relationships
and business combinations.
Awesome.
Now, and this is important.
You must remember to translate your BATNA into the language of the negotiations you were about to enter.
This is necessary.
If you want to be certain you will not be walking away from a decent deal for a worse patina.
For example, if you are considering switching insurance companies and say Allstate offer a plan with
a 7% and 10% annual rate increase or a state farm have a policy with 30% less than the all state rate.
You start off leaning towards signing with State Farm, but you decide to be cautious and make some
more calculations.
You dig deeper and call both companies to discuss their options and notice there is some fine print
to the State Farm terms.
You translate both policies into what they mean financially and make the prices comparable by period.
You conclude you will be better off with all states renewal rates than with State Farm?
Awesome.
But if you hadn't spent the extra time, you would have opted out for the worse option.
Do you see what we mean by translating a banana into the terms of the current negotiation?
Excellent.
Let's keep going.
Baton up must correspond to a real and possible set of events in the world.
It should not be a made up scenario you hold on to as a safety net that would defeat the purpose of
having a banana.
And unlike in a real life scenario, a banana can change due to shifts in the market.
It is a dynamic concept that adapts to different circumstances.
In fact, a great negotiator would make it their priority to constantly try and improve their own banana
and spoil that of their opponent.
So once you have identified your banana, should you reveal it to your opponent or keep it a secret?
If it is a strong banana and you are certain it is better than the other side thinks it is, you should
lay it on the table.
It is very likely your bargaining partner will stop negotiating as if you have no viable alternatives
and the conversation will become more beneficial to you.
If you think your banana is weak, however, it will be best if you do not openly discuss it.
And what happens if you do not have a banana at all?
Very often the negotiator without a banana will find themselves in an insecure position.
They will either feel pressured to reach an agreement at all costs because they do not think they have
other alternatives or end up being overoptimistic about the offer on the table because they lack perspective.
In any case, saving resources by not exploring your banana usually results in much poorer negotiation
outcomes in the longer run.
We will end this lesson here and next time we will pick it up with a discussion of reservation points
and bargaining ranges.
Sweet.
Thanks for watching.

--- Reservation point and the Bargaining range ---

Hi, and welcome back.
As this lesson is related to the previous one, let's recap what we talked about.
We learned about Bataan and how and why to have won a banana is what you will do if the negotiations
fail and you do not reach an agreement.
It is an alternative outside of what's being negotiated.
You remember right now let's talk about the reservation point.
It's also called a bottom line or a walk away.
This is the point at which your Bertino becomes the better option.
It is the trigger point where you will take your best outside alternative instead of continuing with
the negotiations.
Essentially, this is the point beyond which a negotiator will not go.
So if you think of your bana as something a bit more abstract than the specific numbers, issues and
terms discussed during a negotiation, then the bottom line is your banana but quantified and monetized.
Developing a reservation point is very similar to determining your banana.
You again go through a process of researching and listing possible outcomes of the negotiation and end
up selecting the one representing the minimum you would agree on.
The reservation point is more important to the negotiation outcome than the banana.
It is immediately available and relevant in an ongoing negotiation, whereas the banana is just outside
of the negotiation.
Reach.
Makes sense, right?
Awesome.
All bargaining parties have their own reservation point.
If you're in a situation in which you work for, let's say, L'Oreal and the company wants to acquire
a competitive firm's hairline products, you would have a worst case scenario of how much you would
be willing to pay to acquire these products.
Let's say you will go as high as $250 million.
That is your reservation point.
The hair care company will have a number in mind as well, under which it will not be willing to sell
$200 million.
This is their bottom line, and the distance between those two points is what we call a bargaining range.
The bargaining range is where the negotiation happens.
It can be either positive or negative.
A bargaining range is positive when a buyer's reservation point is above the sellers, which is the
case we're looking at right now.
And the range is negative when the places are switched and the seller's resistance point is above that
of the buyer.
Like this.
In the second scenario, a settlement will be generally impossible to achieve.
The situation can be resolved only if one or all negotiators change their bottom lines like this.
And now an agreement can be reached.
Straightforward, right?
Cool.

--- Assessing yourself ---

In one of the early lessons, we talked about how negotiation is a skill which you can practice and
become an expert in.
One of the keys to becoming an effective negotiator is not forgetting.
Preparation is crucial.
It is the make or break of a successful outcome.
In fact, when you think about the importance of preparation, recall the Perito Rule or the 8020 Division
to reach your desired outcome.
80% of the effort should be put in the preparation beforehand, and only 20% should be the actual work
you put in during the negotiation.
We can divide the preparation phase into three stages self assessment, counterpart assessment and assessment
of the situation.
Let's begin with a step by step overview of what we can do during self assessment.
First things first define your best case scenario.
This is your most desired outcome and your target.
It should be the opposite of your reservation point.
The bottom line should not influence your target.
If this happens, you're making yourself susceptible to what is known as the winner's curse.
The winner's curse starts happening when you pay too much attention to what you want to walk out with
from a negotiation as a bare minimum, and you set your goals to low.
In those situations, people tend to under Aspire and they open the negotiations with a low request,
which is easily granted.
But once your bargaining partner grants a request, they are entitled to make requests of their own
and become reluctant to grant you anything else you might want.
Overall, the winner's curse results in a very regrettable outcome far off your real target.
Okay.
So you don't play weak, but you shouldn't be too tough either.
Do not open the negotiations with a target that is too high while also refusing to make any compromises.
This almost always results in retaliation from your bargaining partner because they will feel threatened
and will turn defensive.
And we do not want that.
Negotiations are a process based on communication and concessions.
You must tread the line between under and over, aspiring carefully when you're determining your target.
Another thing you should pay attention to is sunk costs.
When you invest money to achieve something, this money is already gone.
This is known as a sunk cost.
For example, the capital expenditure you have made to build a house is a sunk cost and the money you
still need to pay is a future cost.
It is important to know sunk costs and future costs are two different things and they should not be
allowed to affect your decisions during the negotiation.
The focus must remain at what stands to be gained now, not what has been lost in the past.
So far, so good.
Knowing your value is super useful when assessing yourself.
If you are negotiating as a private individual, look at websites telling you what your services are
worth in your market.
These are places like Glassdoor dot com, salary.com get rates dot com and some government websites.
This will give you an idea of what your pay grade is based on criteria like title, experience, education
and region.
And if you are negotiating on behalf of a firm, look up the product value, company and market value
and where you rank once your information itch has been scratched, you can move on to determining your
Betina and reservation point.
If you need a quick refresher on how to best do that, feel free to go back to lessons and have another
look at our advice.
Cool.
Once you've covered the fundamentals, you should spend some time thinking about what your must haves
want.
To haves and nice to haves are.
Having a systematic list of preferences will enable you to be coherent in your negotiations, and it
will make you more aware of what you want to achieve and what you're willing to concede to get there.
In fact, when you're thinking about these issues, trying to come up with alternatives to each one,
the must want to and nice to have.
This will add a lot of value to the negotiation and will give you more ways to achieve your target.
There's also the added benefit of appearing flexible to the other party, which invites them to reciprocate.
That's bound to be useful, right?
Great.
This covers the extent to which you can prepare for a negotiation by looking at the situation on your
end.
Join us in the next lesson when we'll talk about how to assess your negotiation partner and the position

--- Assessing your opponent ---

Now that we've covered how to assess yourself and your position before entering negotiations, we can

There are five core things you must know about your opponents to be an effective negotiator.
First, you must know who they are.
Understand their positions, importance, and how they are involved.
They might be a representative, or they could have a direct stake at the negotiations.
If you can try to learn who will be making the decisions and who the stakeholders are, often these

your bargaining partner is after and what they value.
Second, understand what the other party finds most and least important.
This is like defining your own must want to and nice to have but in less depth.
Since you do not have direct and immediate access to their game plan.
If you can figure this out, you will be one step ahead and will know what is open to negotiation and
what they're willing to concede.
Once you can identify the issues on the table, you will be able to adjust your negotiation tactics
accordingly and pay for your way to a beneficial outcome.
Does that make sense?

And so on.

That said, not knowing your opponent's patina is a severely disadvantageous position because it takes
the power to set the terms away from you.
Unfortunately, finding out somebody else's patina is a lot easier said than done.

--- Assessing the situation ---

The last step in your preparation is assessing the situation you're in.
Now that you know who the players at the table are and what's important for them, you must think about
what the environment of the negotiations is going to be like.
There are several elements to determining the negotiations context.
First, you must understand the relationship you have with the other party.
A negotiation can be a one shot interaction or be a long term relationship, which is bound to open
more avenues for conversation that repeat on a regular basis.
If you can define the extent to which the relationship with others at the negotiation table is important
to you in your future, you can inform your grand strategy and approach with certain confidence.
Think of it this way If you do not think about the importance of a relationship, you risk entering
the negotiations too aggressively.
And when a battle which will later cost you the war, you stand to benefit a great deal from considering
how your relationship with the counterparty may grow and if trust needs maintained over time.
Sometimes it is better to concede at an early stage of a longer partnership to ensure future contracts
success.
Moreover, people in business build a reputation with time.
As Warren Buffett said, it takes 20 years to build a reputation and 5 minutes to destroy one.
What do you do when negotiating with others remains on your track record?
If you have a history of abusing counterparts in a weaker negotiating position, this may also reflect
on people's willingness to do business with you.
You also must think about possible conflicts that might arise.
Is it possible you and your bargaining partner won't reach a consensus because of clashing beliefs or
opinions and values, or because you are negotiating over scarce resources and there is a clash of interests?
This is a very important consideration to have because often people who have differing ideologies are
unable to walk past them and rarely reach a win win.
There is something you can do.
However, if you find yourself in this situation, attempt to steer the conversation towards interests,
even if this is still rocky territory.
Negotiators who are in dispute over interests are a lot more likely to make value added trade offs and
end up in agreement than are those in conflict over values.
Okay, excellent job so far.
Please don't forget that as anything, negotiation processes have time constraints.
Of course, all negotiations will have some sort of a time frame, but you must pay attention to what
the cost of the time spent is.
If time is very expensive for you or your bargaining partner, you must expect pressure to close a deal
as soon as possible.
It is commonly assumed that being pressured for time is a weakness and a disadvantage.
Hence, any time related costs should be kept secret.
This is not necessarily the case.
First, a deadline for one party is also a deadline for all negotiators.
If the clock runs out, everyone at the negotiating table must go with their badness.
And second, when a deadline draws nearer, the amount of concessions and compromises negotiators make
grows rapidly.
And usually this happens on both sides of the table, which makes perfect sense, right?
Time pressure for one is time pressure for all.
Something else to mention while on the topic of time time horizon, this is the time distance between
the negotiation event and the consequences of the agreement that was reached.
There is an unwritten law regarding the time horizon which states the longer the distance between those
two events, the more likely it is to reach a good win win.
So if I run a telecommunications firm and start negotiations for a contract renewal as soon as possible,
the chances of obtaining a better outcome are higher.
The more time top level managers have, the more likely it is they will find out how they can do business
together and design a win win situation in the long run.
Okay, we've discussed time now.
Let's move on to place.
Where are the negotiations going to happen?
This is not an irrelevant detail.
Negotiating at your own turf is advantageous, whereas wearing the guest jersey is not.
Therefore, it will be in everybody's interest if some effort is spent to ensure neutral grounds for
negotiating.

--- Distributive negotiations (Slicing the pie) ---

Welcome back.
In this lesson, we're going to talk about the first major approach to negotiating distributive negotiations.
The core idea of distributive negotiations is to distribute a fixed amount of resources like money or
the slices of a pie.
Do you remember the bargaining range we discussed in lesson five?
It is important here if there is an overlap between your reservation point and your opponents, the
bargaining zone is positive and there's room for negotiations, remember?
The point of going into distributive negotiations is to secure as much of the bargaining range for yourself
as you can.
This type of competitive negotiating is often called win lose or zero sum y.
If you manage to claim a lot of the negotiation surplus, your opponent will be left with less.
The more one side gets, the less the other side receives.
Don't forget the issues on the table are fixed.
Or at least this is what you and your opponent have chosen to believe.
But more on that in our lesson on integrative negotiating.
Distributive bargaining is effectively a dance between your reservation point and your partner's walk
away.
You're trying to learn or make an educated guess about the value of their walkaway and use that information
to guide you through the negotiation.
In distributive bargaining, the goal is to reach a resolution which is closer to your target and ultimately
your partner's reservation point, not the other way around.
One thing you can do if you want to make sure you have the strategic advantage is to gain intel.
You should learn as much as you can about your opponent and what they value.
Your bargaining power is strongly grounded in how clearly you have defined your target must have an
patina or other alternatives and how well you know your opponent's goals.
The better you are prepared, the more capable you will be to make informed decisions about when to
concede and when to firmly hold on to your position to achieve the best resolution for yourself.
At the same time, the rule of thumb is that you keep your own cards close to your chest and share as
little information about your wants and options with your partner.
There are situations in which this can be overruled, but more on that next time.
In the academic circles, distributive negotiations are associated with aggressive and destructive bargaining
tactics and suboptimal negotiation tactics.
This is because one of the consequences of perceiving a negotiation as a zero sum game is that both
parties tend to put a lot of focus on their differences instead of considering what they could both
get out of the deal.
The popular opinion in conflict resolution theory is that winless bargaining should not be used because
it does not deliver the best results.
However, there are specific situations in which aggressive negotiations make more sense than negotiations,
which focus around creating value for all parties.
For example, when buying a used car or haggling at a flea market, we will learn why a bit later.
So even though some theorists dismiss the need for distributive negotiating altogether, we hope by
the end of the module you will be able to decide for yourself whether or not this approach holds merit.
Next, we'll learn about the different strategies to claim issues and distribute the pie during distributive
bargaining.
Thanks for watching.

--- Distributive strategies (Pie-slicing strategies) ---

Now that we know what distributive negotiation is, let's talk about the different strategies you can
use to achieve a beneficial agreement.
We've already discussed patterns, reservation points and targets, and now it's time to revisit them
in light of the new knowledge we've accumulated.
When you're entering negotiations thinking it's a win lose situation, you must do everything in your
power to maximize your chances to be the winning side.
The pie is on the table and you want the bigger slice.
One of the easiest ways to tip the scales in your favor is to revisit your BATNA, reassess and improve
it.
Remember, the battle is the objective reality and your bargaining partner may have managed to estimate
what it looks like for you.
Having a strong alternative to the negotiated agreement will reduce the power they have over setting
the anchor or determining the bargaining range.
While you're thinking about your BATNA.
Revisit your reservation point.
Do not uncover it during the negotiations.
Regardless of how friendly the conversation may be, letting your opponent know what your bottom line
is invites them to offer you that price, which effectively takes away any surplus you may have otherwise
secured.
There are only two circumstances under which you can openly declare your reservation point.
One is when the clock is running out and you're nearing the deadline for an outcome.
In this situation, walking out with a deal, even at your reservation point, is still better than
resorting to your BATNA.
And the other is if you have an amazing partner and can afford to be aggressive and you would be happy
either if the other side walked or if they offered you something exceeding your reservation point.
Okay, now a word of caution when you choose to reveal either your bottom line or your Betina, keep
in mind this needs to be truthful information and not a lie.
Lies are a unethical and be very good at catching up with you, especially in the digital age where
your presence is constantly updated and reviewed.
Strategy.
Tip number four has to do with your opponent's patina.
And we cannot stress this enough.
Try and learn it.
This will minimize their leverage and increase yours.
How about that?
When you're in a win lose situation, one of the most advantageous things you can do to secure a good
chunk of the pie is to make the first offer, but only if you're prepared.
Otherwise you are risking setting the bar either too low or too high.
In the first scenario, you may fall victim to the winner's curse.
In the second, you may alienate and make the other party hostile.
If you manage to make the first offer, it will serve as an anchor point, ensuring you come out of
the negotiations with the better deal.
Of course, getting this much in return doesn't come cheap.
Making the first offer is a difficult task.
The offer should be well balanced.
It should not insult your bargaining partner or reveal the bargaining space too much.
That said, don't ever make an offer structured as a range.
One of the main things you're trying to achieve with the first offer is to land it in the bargaining
zone.
If you succeed, it will act as a guiding star for your opponent and they will issue a counteroffer
with your first offer in mind.
You would be orchestrating the negotiations.
However, it is important to remember to be patient.
Sometimes the counteroffer will not come as quickly as you expect.
Do not change your initial offer with a concession right away.
This will signal weakness and will effectively downplay your opening demand.
If you're not ready to make the first offer and instead your bargaining partner opens first, you must
counter offer immediately.
This accomplishes two things.
It reduces the importance of the initial anchor and makes it known you are open to bargain.
Now, when you're making your counteroffer, it's important to use an opening you have planned beforehand,
which is not driven by the offer you've just received.
The reason why is simple.
The goal is to move the focal point of the negotiation away from what was just set as an anchor.
Following the anchor defeats the purpose of the counteroffer play.
The way to sidestep this danger is to devise your offer in advance by considering your opponent's patina
and your initial target.
Does that make sense?
Excellent.
Let's say you have already opened or counter offered and you're somewhere in the bargaining range because
these are distributive negotiations.
Each party will want to maximise their own share of the pie, which means both of you will end up making
concessions.
These must also be prepared in advance.
Any reductions you make must be followed by concessions on the other end as well.
The negotiation game is best played in a back and forth manner.
Cool.
Now there are several things to know about concessions making increasingly larger concessions than your
bargaining partner is ill advised.
You'll end up losing more than you will gain.
The best approach to conceding is called the grid model or gradual reduction in tension.
According to its framework, the optimal strategy is to begin negotiating with a hard stance, then
make several small concessions and finish up with some larger ones.
Finally, be careful not to be tricked by the even split ploy.
If the other party suggests an even split, they will likely get more out of the deal than you.
How?
Well, as no two parties are perfectly matched for resources.
Concessions have different reference points depending on who's making them.
If donating $200,000 to a nonprofit is okay for a tech corporation, it may not be as okay for a medium
sized company.
In addition, if your opponent offers at the end of the negotiations, all right, let's wrap this up
and just split the difference and you accept outright.
It will be as if you're agreeing you have been treating them unfairly up until now or asking too much
of them.
So now you just want to get what you can and leave the table.
When offered the even split.
The best line of action would be to agree as a sign of good faith, but also try to change the terms.
Ask for something extra, for example.
In fact, to be most effective in this situation, try to imagine what you could ask for even before
entering the negotiations.
Yes.
In case we haven't made this clear until now, the key to becoming a successful negotiator is preparation,

--- Interest-based bargaining ---

Hi and welcome back.
In this lesson, we will talk about interest based negotiations and why they are considered the more
optimal approach to successful outcomes.
We already talked about distributive negotiations.
To recap, the end goal with distributive negotiations is to get as much as you can from a set amount
of resources effectively, whatever you manage to secure for your party, the other party loses.
This is the epitome of a win lose situation in interest based negotiation.
All negotiators at the table have a shared goal create more value for everyone.
Reach a state where everyone wins.
This type of negotiating is also called integrated or expansive.
These are just some different names for the same approach.
Interest based negotiations are about learning what your counterparties needs are leveling with them,
figuring out what they're interested in, but haven't openly claimed yet.
The idea is to expand the pie of options and find outcomes that are mutually beneficial.
The driving force behind interest based negotiating is the constant search for gains that will be advantageous
for all parties.
This isn't always easy.
In fact, everyone involved in the negotiation should work hard to identify them.
But once defined, these shared interests become real opportunities and goals all negotiators collectively
strive to achieve.
This makes the negotiations much friendlier and smoother.
The goal of this type of negotiation is to satisfy the desires of all parties and create a true win
win opportunity.
Awesome.
Right.
Here are some telltale signs that the other party is interested in expansive negotiations.
More than one issue is on the table, and it's okay to bring in other issues that were not previously
discussed.
The more options, the better the chances for a beneficial deal.
Side deals are not frowned upon.
Having a side deal works here because it is kind of like bringing new issues to negotiation.
Again, it's a numbers game.
Your bargaining partner doesn't want the exact same things as you from the negotiation issues.
This is an excellent environment for a win win settlement.
Imagine the following.
You have an apple and you and your best friend want it.
However, you're both planning on using it for different purposes.
You want to eat it and your friend wants to use the seeds to try and plant an apple tree.
So you're one apple can serve both of you equally well.
Makes sense, doesn't it?
Splendid.
To give you an idea of how interest based negotiations tend to go, Lee Thomson, author of The Mind
and Heart of the Negotiator, outlines the stages of the conversation like this.
If we split the negotiations into four parts, the first is typically taken up by some sort of powerplay
because both parties are trying to establish themselves as the one holding more influence.
Then begins the information disclosure.
Parties discuss priorities and preference strength in the third quarter.
They start issuing offers and counters.
This is where any arguments take place.
It is important.
The conversation remains rational and respectful here.
Claims should be supported by facts and logic, and the final stage is where the agreement ought to
be reached.
Parties review and try to build on what has been put on the table during the conversation.
And that's the negotiations done.

--- Interest-based negotiation strategies ---

Now that we know there is a more effective and friendlier alternative to the old fashioned win lose
negotiation technique, we can learn more about how to be great at interest based negotiations before
we jump into strategies to expand the pie.
We should talk briefly about the common misconceptions and errors related to interest based negotiations.
Okay.
First, people tend to enter negotiations believing the interests of their own party are entirely incompatible
with the desires of the other party.
This is overwhelmingly often not the case and only hinders the negotiation.
When both parties miss out on the shared value they can gain.
They end up reaching what is known as a lose lose agreement.
As you can tell, this is the exact opposite of what we want.
Second, the notion that negotiations are inherently a redistribution of fixed resources or for me to
win, you must make a sacrifice.
Type of relationship is dangerously engraved in people's minds and reduces flexibility a lot.
You must try and remember the pie is not fixed and a tartlet and a cupcake can also be added to the
table.
If you manage to sidestep this cognitive obstacle.
You will be more effective in developing an accurate understanding of what is actually on offer and
what the other parties interests are.
And finally, reaching a win win is not a must.
If you enter the negotiations determined to reach a win win, you will put unnecessary pressure on the
conversation.
In addition, you should leave any urge to compromise at the door.
Concessions are an element of win lose negotiations.
Here, we're trying to create a new pie, not divide a few pieces and be careful not to reveal your
banana.
Revealing it might work during distributive negotiations, but has no value here.
Cooperation isn't equal to laying all your cards on the table.
Right.
Great.
We know the no no's for interest based effectiveness.

If you are successful, you will be much more informed and better at solving problems, mid negotiation

So how do you go about facilitating perspective taking?
Simple ask diagnostic questions.
You can try with the W questions that invite an open response like what, why, when, where and their
non w cousin how.
These provide a non-threatening way to get information.
Unlike closed ended questions which often inspire defensive behavior and encourage the other party to
misrepresent facts or even lie.
But remember, negotiations are a back and forth process, and this means you must provide your partner
with knowledge about what you're after and what your priorities are.
It's only fair, right?
And not just that.
If you disclose information about your party, it will signal that you are approaching these negotiations
openly, which will invite the principle of reciprocation to enter play.
If your counterpart still hasn't shared information.
Chances are they will now.
In addition to probing with diagnostic questions, it is also very important to engage in active listening.
This allows you to make deductions about your counterparties interests, even if they're not being completely
open with you.
And more importantly, you will get a sense of what their preferences are and how they rank.
Another valuable thing you can do is try to make package deals.
Package deals are useful for two reasons.
They allow the negotiating parties to come up with trade offs between individual issues, and they make
reaching an impasse much less likely.
Apart from package deals, you can also aim to think about different offers.
You can make that hold the same value for you and make them simultaneously.
This both creates the impression of flexibility and makes it easier and more likely to uncover mutually
beneficial issues, useful and not super difficult.
The only ingredient you need is and we know you've never heard this one before.
Preparation.
A major difference between interest based and distributive negotiating is that in interest based situations,
the fact that both sides don't want the same things can be exploited.
In fact, it is encouraged.
This drives finding issues to trade to find mutual value.
For example, there is a package deal on the table consisting of X, Y and z issues.

You can choose to trade off X and Y to gain Z.

--- Choosing the correct negotiation strategy ---

There are two dimensions you must consider the extent to which you care about your future relationship
with your negotiating partner, and how important the end result of the negotiations is.
Right.
So with two guiding dimensions we can define for negotiation situations.
It is easier to see it on a graph like this.
So you have the two dimensions relationship importance on the Y axis and outcome importance on the x
axis.
And you get the four situations low on relationship importance low an outcome importance high on relationship,

High on outcome and low on relationship.
High on outcome.
So what style do you use to negotiate in each case?
If you're low in both dimensions, you will be here.
This means you are likely not going to have other interactions with this person in the future, and
there's not a lot of value on the table.
So minimal risk and no future consequences.
Think of haggling at a flea market.
