In this case, the optimal style to choose is distributive negotiating or splitting the pie.
It doesn't make sense economically to spend the resources to expand the pie and create a mutually beneficial
outcome.
So you just split the resources that are already on the table and each party continues with their day
without wasting precious time.
The situation changes if we move up the relationship axis.
Now the negotiations have high relationship stakes.
You would like to continue working with your counterpart, right?
The outcome value isn't very important.
This situation is something like setting up payments for your product, where the client prefers to
split the price into three deposits instead of two, which is what's best for you.
The best thing to do in this setting is to agree to their terms or serve the pie.
You will still receive the money.
You would just need to wait longer.
This is a non-confrontational style of negotiation, and its main purpose is to strengthen and maintain
a relationship.
If we switch on the dimensions and go to low relationship, high outcome value instead of serving the
pie, you would be smart if you're aggressive and take the pie.
This is a variation of distributive negotiating, but here you are trying to get as much as possible
from the resources available.
Say you're buying a real estate property.
Your company is about to spend a lot of money and you're probably not going to buy another real estate
property from this agent again.
So the impression you make is not crucial.
You should try to extract maximum value from the negotiations and move on to making this property your
dream office premises.
And finally, let's visit the last quadrant.
High relationship value and high outcome importance.
This is where interest based negotiating finds a place to shine.
Imagine you're negotiating the terms of getting a hosting provider to host your business.
These are the people you will be working with continuously because they will provide tech support,
update your security and upkeep your platform.
You want to be on good terms with them.
On the other hand, you will also pay a lot of money for the service.
So what's the best thing to do?
And that's right.
Try to identify common interests and create more value for both parties.
You can offer to create more revenue for them in exchange for a smaller total on the price tag.
It makes sense, right?
Awesome.
Remember, think about the nature of the negotiations before sitting at the table and determine what
your style will be.
The dynamics you set may be the difference between getting a lot of value from the outcome and not.
Thanks for watching and see you in the next lesson.

--- Aversive tactics and protecting yourself from them ---

Okay.
Now we know how to prepare for successful negotiation, how to approach different situations, and tailor
your strategy accordingly.
We learned about distributive and interest based negotiations and mentioned counteracting unfair maneuvers.
Here we want to talk about the five most common tactics negotiators use when they try to gain advantage.
It is important you know these so you can identify and power them quickly.
Does that make sense?
Great.
Let's get started then.
The first tactic is called highball or lowball, depending on what the negotiations are about.
This is when your opponent opens with an unreasonably high or low offer.
This has one purpose only remember anchoring.
This move is made so they can set an anchor that makes you rethink your reservation point and adjust
your target to something that agrees with them more.
It's a tactic that relies on you not doing the research before entering the negotiations.
But if you have done your research, you can easily par this attempt.
You will most likely recognize the move for what it is, and you will be able to call it out.
Don't worry, this will not antagonize your partner.
It will simply show them you know how the game is played and you want to get back to negotiating.
And if you're not sure if this is the high ball, low ball maneuver.
Be curious about why they're making it.
If it is a legit claim, your partner will have a rock solid justification.
The second most common tactic is the bogey.
This is when your counterpart pretends to hold on to an issue as if it is of crucial importance to them
when in fact it isn't, and then concedes it effectively pressuring you to make an equally big concession.
Unless you've done a really thorough background check on your opponent, you will probably have a hard
time detecting a bogey.
One thing that gives it away is if there is a very sudden change in your opponent's attitude, it is
unlikely they defend an issue with a fiery passion.
1/2, but then give it up as soon as something they want hits the table.
Just to be sure, you should ask questions, have them justify why they switched positions so rapidly.
This is another case where information is paramount.
Remember to prepare for your negotiations and you will be set for success.
Tactic number three is the avalanche.
This is the one where they try to bury you under information numbers, statistics, reports, figures
and facts.
It has one goal and one goal only to distract you.
Thus, the issue you're faced with becomes deciphering what's important and what's just word salad.
The best way to counter the avalanche is to be consistent and specific in your stance.
Make them clarify what is truly substantial to these negotiations.
Be careful not to agree to anything you do not fully grasp.
If needed, consult experts on your end, but do not give up.
If you keep a clear head, you will get through this tactic.
The Nipple is another famous move negotiators like to use at the end of negotiations with the nipple.
Your opponent will agree on some terms.
And to wrap up the negotiations just before you shake on it, they will ask for a small concession on
your part to be thrown into the deal as a complementary side to the main dish.
You can protect yourself from the nibble without much effort.
You need to be candid in the beginning and create a roadmap of the issues you want to discuss.
Ask your opponent to follow your lead.
Once the negotiations begin repeatedly, offer them to let you know if they are interested in something
else as well.
If they don't take advantage, then you should not feel any guilt turning down their nibble attempt
at the end of the talks.
And if that doesn't work, have a small issue or two.
You would like to get out of the deal and offer them a trade.
They will either agree or back down from theirs.
Either way, a good outcome.
Lastly, be careful not to deal with people who claim to have authority but actually lack any decision
making power.
You will recognize them by the fact that they need to consult their superiors.
If you have any doubt you're not discussing your issues with somebody in charge, ask them if they are
authorized to carry out the outcomes of this conversation, if they're hesitant or admit they do not
have the authority.
Refuse to continue the negotiations until you meet with somebody of the appropriate rank.
Anything else will prove a waste of precious resources, time and energy.
Nice.
You're now even more prepared to deal with negotiating parties who do not always mean well and see negotiations
as a win lose only.
In the next lesson, we will talk about how to resolve conflicts at the bargaining table.
Thanks for watching.

--- Conflict resolution ---

Negotiations are never only smooth sailing and this is part of the game.
Actually, it is common for negotiators to act in a way that provokes negative reactions in their counterpart,
especially in the beginning.
These are usually things used to assert dominance, like challenging the abilities of the other party
or how legitimate they are.
However, once you know these things are fair play, you can identify them and use what we'll discuss
here to par.
For instance, if your bargaining partner decided to question your ability to carry out a task you were
an expert in, or they dismissed your ideas with a You've Got to be joking, which doesn't leave much
room for a response.
You can try to remember this is a game and they're trying to trick you into an emotional response.
If they succeed, that would undermine your position and the force will be with them.
One way to sidestep a similar attack is to do one of the following.
You can either take a strategic break and rein in your emotions.
We call this going to the balcony.
This is a nice, harmless move that has the added benefit of also interrupting any negative momentum
coming your way.
Alternatively, you can vocally recognize what your counterpart is doing as a power play and then move
on.
Phrases like I actually know what I'm doing and instead of interrupting me, can you let me walk you
through my idea?
Work well to restore the power balance and advance the negotiations in the desired direction.
Or you can attempt to steer the focus in a more productive direction by reminding them of the purpose
of the meeting.
Sounds fair, doesn't it?
In addition, you can try to employ some of the interest based strategies you now know.
For example, even if you end up in the middle of a business dispute, trying to remember that while
emotions might be running high, the resolution does not need to be a win lose battle, just like the
entire negotiation itself.
You can also take a step backwards and reevaluate trying to identify the shared values and interests
you and your bargaining partner have and capitalize on them, even if the issues you agree on are superficial
and seemingly unrelated.
This will lay the groundwork for pinpointing the main reason for the dispute.
Finally, you can use the passage of time as your secret weapon.
What do we mean by this?
Well, as time passes, many things can change about the circumstances around which the conflict arose
in the first place.
For one, both parties grow more accustomed to working with each other and to dealing with the dispute.
One thing you should keep in mind is that time only works if you do.
It is a winning strategy to invest effort into keeping the communication going with the other party.
Meeting regularly encourages willingness to approach and openly discuss the differences that feed into
the dispute.
This can happen either gradually in the course of these frequent meetings or at a single sit down.
Whatever the case, patience and perseverance often do the trick.
And if you're unlucky in this respect and your negotiation partner is really unwilling to meet you halfway,
then you might get lucky otherwise.
The person who's being divisive may leave the position or in the negotiations, which in turn can restore
the hopes and efforts towards reaching a resolution.
The bottom line is do not underestimate the importance of what time passage can do for a conflict.
Awesome.
Next, we will talk about the best techniques for building trust and how to fix it in case it gets broken
and the feelings of fairness are hurt.
Thanks for watching.

--- Establishing trust ---

Think about the last time you entered negotiations over something either in your personal life or in
a work setting.
You went in looking forward to being treated fairly and given a reasonable amount of information.
Right.
Yet if you look closely at the memory, you probably approached your partner with a grain of mistrust
and suspicion and were not willing to give them all the information you had yourself.
That happens in most negotiations and unfortunately fosters a toxic bargaining environment as both parties
feel the need to reciprocate the reservation and hostility.
And this lowers the productivity of the meeting, not an optimal turn of events.
Therefore, you can be confident that knowing how best to build trust is an integral skill.
We will talk about five ways in which you can encourage trust in your interactions.
First, make use of the resources you already have.
If you have control over who's the person you'll be negotiating with.
Use your network to guide your selection process.
Review your options.
Look at references and ask your network for referrals.
We already mentioned the importance of knowing your bargaining partner well.
Learning what connections you share and name dropping.
This is just as important in establishing a trusting relationship as is anything else.
When you counterpart understands they have been recommended by a friend, they will already be less
suspicious and more accommodating, both because you share a link and because there is somebody else's
recommendation at stake.
If, on the other hand, you feel like you're going to miss out on an opportunity by sticking to your
network and you're thinking about negotiating with a stranger, make sure you vet them first, check
their credentials and consult independent sources to try and verify them.
Hint Think about LinkedIn's skill endorsement section.
It rests on similar logic.
A second tactic would be to build a bond before the negotiations start.
Really, we cannot stress this enough.
If reciprocity is the driving force behind turning negotiations into a sour contest, then it makes
sense.
It will also be the key to nurturing a candid relationship.
Right?
In fact, studies show that both parties and the negotiation outcome benefit from spending time and
effort on setting up an informal meeting with no shoptalk involved.
And if a longer event like lunch is ill advised because of time pressure, then a five minute chat over
the phone or Skype has also been proved to be an effective strategy to facilitate rapport.
Not only do these gestures of goodwill drive trust levels up universally, but they consistently pay
off in terms of economic gains.
We mean more mutually beneficial outcomes and higher gross numbers.
Not so bad for a lunch or a phone call, right?
Okay.
One reason why trust breaks at the negotiating table is that when conflict arises, the parties begin
to overstate their own importance and place greater value on the contributions they have made.
Such a difference in perspective makes us feel as if we're being cheated, even betrayed.
The sense of unfairness is especially prominent when one bargaining side approaches the negotiations
with unconditional trust.
That said, trust tactic number three is about making sure your expectations about the trust level of
the negotiations are adequate.
What does that mean?
It means you essentially need to set some ground rules about how this relationship will progress.
Be candid and let them know you're slow to trust and you believe in the methodical and gradual proof
of reliability.
Now, don't think this is rude, aggressive, or will convey that you're being cautious and mistrusting.
If you're open about your reasons, your bargaining partner will be happy to consider the terms.
After all, this is the fair and pragmatic thing to do.
Tactic number four is a deceptively easy one.
Gain the other party's favor.
There are several things you can do to achieve this.
For example, you can research them, learn about their interests, trajectory, customs.
This is an absolute must if you are negotiating with somebody from a different country or industry.
If your field has a field specific lexis or jargon, familiarizing yourself with it will save you many
embarrassing situations and will let them know you respect them and are serious about your partnership.
But be careful not to focus too much on making a great impression during the negotiations.
And don't forget to make claims.
After all, negotiations take place so both parties can get something out of working together.
Be honest about what you expect and what you're willing to concede.
And and this is a big one.
Remember to explain how valuable to you is each thing you decide to give up.
If you forget this step, it is very easy for your counterparty to assign very little importance to
your sacrifice and therefore to forget to reciprocate.
Number five, give your partner some of your time.
Don't rush the negotiations.
This is especially relevant if you are the person holding the higher position.
Give them time to talk and let them explain their standpoint or frustration.
Being the person who is actively engaged results in the biggest mutual value because a, you learn what
makes the other party tick and avoid it in the future.
And b their trust in you grows, making them more likely to understand and respect your decisions.
As a result, both parties exit the negotiation with a sense of overall fairness, which is what we
want, right?
Establishing trust is often the result of whether we feel like we're being treated fairly.
One of the most effective ways to convey interest in fairness is to engage with the conversation, listen
actively, and acknowledge what they bring to the table when appropriate.
Show them you appreciate a clever idea they have, or how rapidly they are adjusting to the dynamic
circumstances.
Turns out the price you need to pay to build trust is not so high after all, is it?
Instead of feeling pressured to make concessions so your counterparty doesn't think you mistrust them,
employ these tactics, and establish a relationship that is much more secure.
Great.
This concludes the lesson on how to build trust.
And next, we'll talk about how to fix it when it's broken.
It's an easy lesson, so let's go.

--- Broken trust and how to repair it ---

No matter how hard we try, sometimes we either fail to establish trust during negotiations, or worse,
we somehow manage to violate it.
If the latter and the relationship is important to you, like when you're planning on being in business
with this party in the future, trust needs to be repaired.
This is not as easy as it seems.
Sometimes the person whose trust has been betrayed doesn't want to spend the effort and resources to
rebuild what is broken and goes on to setting new relationships.
But if you're lucky and your bargaining partner is willing to commit to rebuilding, you can follow
these steps during your attempt to reestablish the trust that was lost.
Number one, let them know you understand something is wrong and their trust has been violated.
This will show you are not blindsided by the negotiations and respect them enough to take a step back
and reevaluate your approach.
Then try to put yourself in their shoes, gain perspective, and try to discover what was the precise
point when their trust was broken.
If you manage to isolate the specific grievance, you can proceed to.
Number three, time to admit you recognize this event as the destroyer of trusts.
Let them know you understand what you did was not fair.
Number four Admit fault and accept the responsibility for breaking the trust.
It would be best if you tried to keep it simple and not engage in a debate about where exactly the fault
lies.
As you've probably guessed, this will only make repairing the trust this much harder.
And lastly, number five offer to make amends and compensate for the violation of the relationship.
Of course, after you have gone through all five stages, it is still possible for your counterparty
to refuse to accept the apology, which means the relationship will remain broken.
The only thing to do in this situation is try to determine why they are unwilling to reestablish trust.
Once, you know, try steps one through four again, you may have gotten it wrong somewhere along the
line.
Keep in mind that sometimes your bargaining partner may forgive the violation but decide to ask for
reparations that are truly unreasonable.
In this case, the relationship will remain broken until you find terms of amends that work for both
of you.
It may take some additional work.
If the reparations they ask for are adequate, the trust will be repaired as soon as you carry out your
end of the deal.
That's fair, right?
Naturally, the best scenario is if the other party accepts your apology and doesn't ask for anything
in terms of reparations.
This shows they are open to re-establishing the trust and continue the working relationship.
Of course, there might be some aftermath after the incident.
For example, lingering guilt and elevated self-consciousness on your end, or increased vigilance and
suspicion on theirs.
But as we mentioned, a few lessons back time and consistency often fix these emotions.
Just remember, patience is paramount in negotiations.
Awesome.
Now your negotiation toolbox is almost complete, and you're one lesson away from our case study.
It will be a fun one.
Get excited.

--- Mediums of negotiation ---

In this lesson, we want to discuss mediums of communication.
Choosing the right communication media for the negotiations is important because it is one of the subtle
ways to influence the outcome.
Essentially, the technology we choose or don't choose to negotiate through affects how we see the negotiations
and what the most fitting behavior is.
The general split is between face to face bargaining and not face to face, and each has its own reign
to shine.
The case for face to face interactions is easy to make if you think about all mediums of communication
as lying on a spectrum, starting with low on social presence and ending with a high on social presence,
then face to face is on the far right end and written typed communication is on the far left.
The rest is in between.
The higher the social presence, the easier it is to build rapport, especially if there has been no
previous interaction between the sides.
Building rapport, as you will see in our module on persuasion, is one of the six key ways to have
others comply with your requests.
An added benefit of face to face bargaining is that people are less likely to twist the truth.
This makes it a lot easier to create joint value than when one or both parties are lying or are worried
the other might be lying.
Does that make sense?
Great.
Compared to other mediums of communication, face to face has the lowest frequency of deceit and suspicion
and hence the lowest likelihood of reaching an impasse.
Another important aspect of face to face is that it conveys nuanced meaning.
You know how you can read a person by the expression on her face or her body language in mimicry?
These cues are present during negotiations and people heavily rely on them to adjust their offers.
Face to face bargaining capitalizes on this advantage.
Removing these cues decreases the messages quality.
Without intelligible information, reaching a win win becomes a lot harder.
So being face to face communication excellent for building rapport, ensuring truthfulness and conveying
meaning clearly.
What about the alternative means of interaction?
Turns out there is a good case for them as well.
For example, if we set aside the benefits of face to face communication, you will notice that if you
were in a situation where you were highly accountable for the outcome, holding a face to face can enhance
the tension.
If there is a lot of uncertainty at the table, another person's gaze may be interpreted as an attempt
to overpower and dominate, which can then lead to retaliation.
When tensions are high, stick to communication over audio channels only.
We do not advise opting out for email or written interactions in these situations because the extreme
scarcity of social cues tends to make people more direct in their communication so much they sometimes
tend to lose sight of what is the desirable behavior and become aggressive or rude.
This is likely one of the reasons why email negotiations have a higher rate of walkouts.
On the other hand, email is great when it comes to sidestepping social hierarchies.
When people of different status negotiate their hierarchy rankings determine how much each party talks
online.
However, the cues for status differences are much less salient and talk time evens, which results
in a higher chance for reaching a mutually beneficial outcome.
Sounds like the better option, right?
Excellent.
So in summary, face to face is the go to option when you must establish rapport, if it already exists
and or the negotiations will be high on tension.
We opt out for audio communication to reduce hostility and when the negotiations have a lower stake
and face to face is either too costly or unavailable as an option, we can use email to ensure more
equal participation.
Cool.
Right.
Okay.

we've learned so far because they are certainly to be found there.
Hint it is often referred to as the deal of the century.

--- Reciprocation ---

If you were paying attention to the intro video, you already know that in this one we will be discussing
the principle of reciprocation.
This is the first of the series of six Shortcuts to Mastering the Art of successful interactions on
both a professional and personal level.
Number one is the principle of reciprocation.
Reciprocation is an extremely effective technique to stimulate a relationship of favor, giving and
compliance.
It works in a very straightforward way.
You should try to repay in kind what another person has provided to you.
At a glance, not much to it, right?
In reality, however, this rule has stood the test of time like no other and has been a driving force
in society from as early as the first written historical documents date.
What all of this essentially means is that if a colleague invites you to their house warming, it becomes
your obligation to invite them to a gathering of your own.
If a friend does you a favor, you should do one in return, and so on.
The reciprocity rule is so strongly entrenched in our society that we become obliged to repay kindnesses,
invitations and gifts.
And the best aspect of it is that when under a feeling of obligation, individuals are much more likely
to say yes to a request.
At the core of the rule is that it enforces uninvited debts.
For example, studies and experience have shown time and again that a marketing strategy that has a
free sample component creates a relationship of indebtedness between the merchant and the buyer.
The strategy is effective because the person who receives the unexpected gift has a few limited options
to reciprocate, depending on what the context is.
The individual can either agree to listen to the product's description, donate to the cause being presented
to them, or say tip their waiter or waitress more money.
In fact, a series of studies demonstrated that when attempting to persuade people to respond to a marketing
survey sending out sticky notes along with the survey's change, the answer rate dramatically.
It turned out that sending out an empty, sticky note increased response rate 34% to 43% and sending
out handwritten notes made it go through the roof, reaching 69% activity.
The principle of reciprocation here was borne out because the person had put in time and effort to create
a personal note, which was not what the Social Convention suggested, and therefore was accepted as
something of a gift.
This is also why responders not only had a higher response rate, they provided significantly better
answers.
There is a second way in which the reciprocity rule can be used to persuade somebody to comply with
the request.
And this is through the so called reciprocal concessions or the rejection than retreat technique, as
it is also known as.
This technique is based on a very simple principle.
You maximize your chances of receiving a yes by first making a larger request that will most likely
be turned down after the refusal.
You make a similar request, which is actually what you were initially interested in.
At this point, your conversation partner will likely be feeling like you made a concession and somehow
did them a favor.
Therefore, creating a need in them to respond with a concession of their own and the only option they
have immediately available to them would be your second request.
Look at retail stores, selling practices, for example.
Here the client is consistently shown the most expensive model.
First, if they agree to it, the store loses nothing.
If they decline, however, the store assistant comes up with a counter offer for a more sanely priced
model.
On one occasion, this ensured that the store average sale went from $550 to $1000.
Okay, so reciprocation is a very potent persuasion technique.
That said, it's not difficult to see.
It can sometimes be used unethically against us.
So is there a way for us to say no to it?
Keep in mind that reciprocation works because it is rooted in the principles of society and we've been
brought up condition to be on the lookout for fairness and obligation.
If you do not reciprocate, you would personally feel like you were a less than good human being, and
it's likely that your peers would feel some indignation as well.
One thing you could do is remember that you only need to reciprocate the favour for what it actually
is.
In that sense, if the initial gift turns out to simply be a device or a trick device to motivate us
to return the favor with interest, then it is not a gift and doesn't need to be returned.
The reciprocation rule.
States made a favour with a favour.
It does not say, however, respond to a trick with a favour.
So the takeaway from the lesson is reciprocity is a powerful persuasion tool.
But to get the most out of it, you need to be the first one to offer a favour.
And that favour needs to be personalised and unexpected.
Great.
Thanks for watching and tune in next time when we will talk about consistency or how people like to
continue with the commitment once they have made the first step.

--- Consistency ---

Hi and well done on making it to another great lesson.
In this video we will talk about commitment and consistency and how to make them part of your persuasion
toolkit.
You may have noticed that backing out of promises or deals always brings about a sense of unpleasantness
and dread.
This is because people are habitual beings and like to be consistent.
Simply put, consistency refers to the fact that people like to act in line with the things they have
already said or done.
If they do not, that creates a sensation in the mind.
Psychologists call cognitive dissonance when actions and attitudes or opinions don't match.
It creates the truly unpleasant feeling of being a hypocrite.
Therefore, people try hard to avoid entering cognitive dissonance by adjusting their behaviors and
matching them to attitudes and actions as they go along.
Additional pressure to be consistent comes also from the fact that society largely views inconsistency
as an undesirable trait and often associates it with indecisiveness and even intellectual weakness.
One way to tap into this persuasion resource is to attempt to identify and then ask for insignificant
commitments the other person can make.
There is tons of research in social psychology that clearly identifies the success of this strategy.
For example, having a group of students write speeches describing the dangers of AIDS and the importance
of condom use, and then recite them in front of their peers, increase the likelihood of these students
to use condoms in the future.
So the students had already made a commitment, both personally when writing the speeches and publicly
when presenting them, and they needed to adjust their behavior accordingly.
This worked even better if they were also asked to list any occasions when they'd failed to use protection
that elevated their need to rectify the inconsistent behavior.
There is another simple force at work here and it is a bit subtle.
The students were asked to write the speech.
Writing has magical powers, as it turns out.
Once a person agrees to certain arrangements and puts it on paper, then the pressures of commitment
become this much more real and palpable.
In fact, there is a super effective technique.
Door to door sales people employ to minimize order cancellations based on this principle.
When there is an order that needs to be filled out, they ask the customers to write down the sales
agreement rather than doing it themselves.
This reduces the number of cancellations drastically.
We can think about this in even simpler terms, and it would still be equally effective.
For example, a medical center that is trying to reduce the number of missed appointments needs only
to ask people to commit to canceling the appointment beforehand and they will achieve their goal, asking
people, Will you please phone in if you need to cancel?
And having them say yes instead of telling them Please let us know.
Creates an arrangement and people by and large, do not break those.
Now, the commitment principle is of course, very well known to the business world as well.
Savvy managers and strategists use it widely in their marketing campaigns.
It is referred to as the foot in the door technique.
For example, the strategy can be to get a client to sign up for a full line distribution by first getting
them to agree only to a small order.
The idea here is that both time and effort have been put into creating the arrangement and establishing
the partnership, and it is now only fitting that this partnership start to grow.
Does that make sense?
Awesome.
So the takeaway from this really is that the individual who is looking to use the commitment principle
to get their ideas across is looking for a small initial commitment.
In an ideal world, this commitment would be made voluntarily.
The person who is agreeing to it would be actively engaged in the process and the deal would be sealed
in writing.
But even in suboptimal situations where not all of the above are present, the principle still holds
strong and is very effective.
Okay, we will end this lesson here.
Join us in the next video where we will be talking about social proof or how and why people are influenced
by peers when making a decision.
Thanks for watching.

--- Social proof ---

Hi there.
Glad to see you're back for another great lesson.
In this video, we will be talking about the concept of social proof or consensus.
You may have noticed or you may have been endlessly annoyed by laugh tracks running in the back of sitcoms.
But have you ever wondered the precise reason why those are there?
Put simply, there is a general tendency that when people find themselves in a situation of uncertainty,
when they're not sure how to act, they look at the environment around them to find cues and guidance
like laughter from a ghost audience.
And they are especially influenced by people of their own age and status.
This type of reasoning is what's driving the success of websites and applications like Yelp, TripAdvisor
and like a local guides.
People like to rely on the experience and opinion of like minded people to guide their own decisions
and actions.
There is an excellent experiment that demonstrates the utility of this principle in action.
The main goal of the study was to try to get people who are staying at a hotel in Arizona to reuse their
towels for reasons were given to four different groups of guests, each appealing to a different thing
the environment perspective, donation of end of year laundry savings, an already completed donation,
or the fact that the majority of the hotel guests reuse their towel at least once per day.
And yes, the last method proved most effective, with a 10% increase in towel reuse from when people
were only considering the environment.
Or, to put it otherwise, as effective as thinking about the environment turned out to be, with 35%
of the people reusing their towels.
Taking a cue from the other people who visited the same hotel was more.
45% of hotel guests decided to reuse their towels.
And if those people were told that the majority of people staying in this hotel and in this exact room
reuse their towels, compliance numbers reached 50%.
Amazing, right?
One driving force behind social proof is the need to belong to a social group.
Humans base about two thirds of their self-image on the social groups they're a part of.
It stands to reason that when a situation occurs that makes us feel uncertain, we reevaluate ourselves
and start thinking on behalf of the group.
It makes sense to be a part of for this decision.
For example, if choosing between several hair products and individual can ask themselves What would
make sense for me as a young college graduate on a budget who cares about quality?
She would go with the choice that fits this description the best, and many others in her position will
too.
So how can the social proof principle be applied in a marketing strategy?
One way to do it is to give your product credibility boost by gathering testimonials, identify your
target audience and have satisfied customers.
Show them that your product or service is something their peers enjoy.
In case your mind jumped directly to Oh, no infomercials, as did ours, let us reassure you that this
is not what we mean by testimonials.
In today's digital age, there are tons of exciting ways to market a product.
Using social proof, you can collaborate with bloggers that share your target audience to popularize
your product or use podcasts.
You can also aim to generate comments, which are often the go to testimonial in online marketing.
One very clever strategy used in TV ads was to change the usual tagline from operators or standing by
to If our lines are busy, please call again.
At this point, you can probably guess what that accomplished.
The simple switch conveyed that many others are also calling in.
Thus, the product or service must be very desirable and you will not be placing your eggs into the
wrong basket if you also trust it.
An easy way to convey a similar message in an increasingly online world is by introducing counters on
your Web platform, for example, of how many people have bought or support the product.
Think about Kickstarter.
The funded in one day banner on a project fulfills this exact purpose and it is super effective.
All of that said, the wisdom of the crowds can be a mighty tool if you just know how to harness it.
We hope it gave you some pretty useful ideas, but feel free to go on and brainstorm with friends.
We're sure you can come up with amazing stuff.

a person increase the chances of a yes?

--- Liking ---

Hello and welcome back.
In this video, we're going to talk about liking or better yet, what role does out preference for specific
people in interactions play in the art of persuasion?
Having a preference for one thing over another is a fundamentally human characteristic, which, as
everything else, fundamentally human we do not have complete control over.
This makes it an exploitable and powerful tool in our persuasion toolkit.
We like to be in partnerships with people we like, and if we like the people, the chances of saying
yes and reaching an agreement are great.
There are three important factors that determine whether we would like another individual.
Are they similar to us?
Do they pay us compliments and do they express a desire to cooperate with us?
Of course, being similar with somebody does not mean acting like fraternal twins and certainly does
not entail the need for you to fabricate an entirely new persona that matches the other person.
In fact, people are pretty good at noticing false similarities.
What they appreciate, however, is genuine interest in an aspect or two of the things they enjoy.
Would the wide availability and reach of social media information is accessible enough for you to get
a general idea of where the commonalities with a given person begin and where they end?
Do you have similar beliefs or backgrounds?
Did you grow up in the same town?
What is their favorite football team or a movie director?
Any of these can be a great base for building rapport and effectively ensuring smooth negotiations.
Studies demonstrate that the search for common grounds can be so subtle that things like a similar sounding
name or a shared birth date can make a person say yes.
For example, during a sales agreement, even being from the same sex or age can have a positive effect
on the interaction.
Okay, but what are you supposed to do if there just isn't anything similar between you and the other
person?
There is no business situation in which it is feasible for you to simply go, Oh well, shake hands
with them and leave.
What you can do instead.
However, it's trying to bring about the chameleon effect.
The Chameleon Effect is a term social psychologists use to describe the act of mirroring in social situations,
or when one person adjusts their stance, sentence, structure and style of communication to match their
partners.
People, by and large, do this unconsciously, and typically, when they recognize their mirroring,
they immediately disengage.
Unfortunately for them, this is actually counterproductive.
The chameleon effect is identified as a leading factor in establishing new friendships that just feel
easy from the get go.
It facilitates and increases empathy as well as liking and bonding.
All of which are factors which contribute to a smoother and more productive interaction.
That said, attempting to mirror your partner's body language is not a behavior you need to rectify.
Quite the opposite.
It's something you can actively strive for in order to increase the perception of similarity between
the two of you and to reach an agreeable arrangement.
There is a study that illustrates in concrete terms how invaluable the initial building of Rapports
before commencing negotiations.
Students doing MBA courses in two different universities were asked to set up online negotiations and
were split into groups.
One group was instructed that time is of the essence and the negotiation should be complete as soon
as possible.
Another group was given a different task.
They were advised to first exchange a couple of personal emails with their assigned partner and try
to get to know them a little bit and only then proceed to negotiating.
Now the results speak for themselves.
The time is of the Essence Group reached an agreement 55% of the time, whereas the second group were
able to close the negotiation successfully in 90% of the cases.
Usually with better terms for both parties.
Finally, this example brings us to one final point regarding online communication, be it with a same
level colleague or a cross continent brand manager.
Written text has an insidious quality to it because it fails to transmit any of the non-verbal cues
on which we base a large part of our assessment of the other person.
And this should not be overlooked, especially when entering a new relationship with the hopes of a
profitable exit.
It is crucial that you attempt to convey as clearly as possible the tone of the conversation, perhaps
with an emoji or a less rigid elaboration on a point.
In the end, it's up to you, but there is no shame in humanising yourself, as the saying goes, even
though you are communicating through a computer.
You do not need to sound like one.
And don't forget, like we mentioned a minute ago, schmoozing during negotiations is not a bad thing.
It is almost a necessity.
And if carried out with a cooperative other side, it can lead to excellent outcomes.
Does all that make sense?
Awesome.

--- Scarcity ---

Okay, great.
At this point, we've covered more than half of the persuasion principals, which is awesome.
But that doesn't mean that the interesting part is over.
In fact, today's lesson is going to be pretty cool.
We're going to talk about the principle of scarcity.
In the simplest terms, scarcity is the feeling of wanting more of what we cannot have.
This principle is at the core of supply and demand in economics.
Think about people who collect baseball cards or camp outside a bookshop only to get their copy of whatever
the latest frenzy is about.
Autographed.
Both groups of people feel that having a very rare item is somehow important and a reason enough on
its own to want to have that item.
Scarcity is a fundamental part of human psychology and can be viewed both as a weakness and as a powerful
feature of mankind.
If you're having trouble imagining just how powerful scarcity can be as a stimulating device, consider
what happens when a government bans a substance or a movie like Ray Bradbury's Fahrenheit 451, or when
a company announces that it will be pulling a product off the market like the iPod Classic in 2014.
All those things experienced a sudden, huge surge in demand with people being willing to pay four or
five times the original price.
Now, some have suggested that the reason why human psychology works this way when faced with scarcity
is because humans have an inherent need to feel in control.
What that means is that when the environment presents us with circumstances, we're not necessarily
thrilled about.
We want to remain in charge of how and to what extent they affect us.
So if a product we enjoy is taken off the market, it feels like the company is trying to prevent us
specifically from having this product.
The only feasible option then becomes us trying to get our hands on that product.
It gives us a sense of accomplishment and satisfaction, which ultimately results in valuing the product
more.
Does that make sense?
Awesome.
So the concept of scarcity is closely linked to the feeling of losing something.
The prospect of losing things typically stimulates people to make decisions that try to avoid this perspective
loss.
Right.
Cool.
Now, how do we use this human tendency in our marketing efforts?
Easy.
We aim to create a sense of urgency and a feeling that if the person doesn't act fast, they stand to
lose something which is available right now.
One tactic that is commonly used to achieve this is called the deadline tactic.
Basically, you introduce some sort of timeframe within which an offer will be valid and pair this with
a call to action that carries a sense of urgency.
For example, do not miss out on this opportunity.
Contrast this with the mere statement.
Take advantage of this opportunity.
The previous one sounds more exciting, right?
Great.
One thing we need to point your attention to, however, is that the scarcity principle does not work
linearly.
What this means is that it is not the case that when scarcity increases, demand increases invariably
with it.
If everything is scarce or let's say, discounted, then people become used to the scarcity and the
scarcity becomes the baseline.
Or to put it differently, if instead of there being only a handful of superheroes, everyone has superpowers,
then these superpowers stop being super and become mere features like fair complexion and dark eyes.
That said, it is a general rule of thumb that when you are putting a product or a line on offer, you
need to keep their quantity below 30% of all the company or brands products.
It makes for better business.
So far, so good.
Now a final word about personal use of the scarcity strategy.
When negotiating with or presenting an idea to a manager or a colleague and you would like to get their
compliance, do not forget to present potential loss figures alongside the unique benefits of your new
idea.
The psychology of loss is much more powerful force in decision making than the psychology of gayness.
Go figure.
Okay.
That's it for this lesson.
See you in the next one where we will be talking about authority and why it is important to convey that
you are qualified on a topic to achieve the compliance you need.
Thanks for watching.

--- Authority ---

Amazing.
You have now reached the final sprint of our section on persuasion.
It already feels like, you know, a ton more about how to convey your ideas in a convincing way, doesn't
it?
Excellent.
In this lesson, we will look at authority as a tool that can be used to elicit compliance in others
like the rest.
The authority principle doesn't work in any confusing way.
It simply uses the propensity we humans have for trusting, knowledgeable, credible people who seem
to be experts in their fields.
And why wouldn't we?
Our drive for looking up to authority figures stems from the fact that we are constantly part of social
structures and there is always a hierarchy.
From very young, we have been conditioned not to talk back to adults and to respect the superiority
of teachers and follow their guidance.
One reason why authority works is because people need to be in control, or at least to know that things
are under somebody's control.
We mentioned that in our previous lesson about scarcity and why that works, remember?
So when an authoritative figure comes along, we're willing to step back and trust that they can reign
over the situation.
This plays well with our fundamental need for order and security.
Try to imagine how you would react if a police officer approached you and asked you to move your vehicle
because a parade will be coming through the street.
Or if they sent you or wanted for questioning in a case, you would most likely comply in both cases.
Why?
Because they convey that they have complete control over the situation.
In fact, in these situations, people are not only compelled to obey, they also feel like they're
not even permitted to challenge whatever request is being made.
Okay.
The police also have something else that contributes to the effectiveness of their request.
Their uniforms are one of the key cues of authority.
People use rules of thumb to decide what constitutes an authoritative figure.
The trick isn't to be a person of authority.
The trick is to seem like you are one.
As Robert Baldini puts it, it is often the connotation and not the content that matters for achieving
compliance.
And uniforms certainly help.
They are a symbol of authority.
Other things can be symbols, too.
For example, role appropriate clothing, like a three piece suit in a board meeting or trappings like
an expensive car or accessories, like a watch or jewelry.
Titles also work well, like using your full job title when negotiating or attempting to convey authority
instead of just your name.
In fact, there is a well documented example that demonstrates how well the authority principle works
in business.
Upon advice from a persuasion specialist, a real estate firm decided to change its approach when connecting
customers with the department they were interested in because the firm has both a sales and a rental
team.
When people phoned in, the receptionist used to tell them, Sales, let me put you through to Barbara
or Rent.
Let me connect you with Steven.
At the advice.
However, the receptionist started introducing who Barbara and Stephen were by referring to their credentials.
Oh, you need the sales department.
Let me put you through to Barbara, who has over 15 years of experience selling properties in this neighborhood.
Not much of a change, but it ended up increasing the number of appointments made by 20% and the number
of closed deals by 15%.
One other takeaway point here is that you don't need to always advertise yourself.
Indeed, sometimes that may not be appropriate.
What you can certainly do, however, is arrange for somebody else to do it for you.
For example, if you are delivering a presentation for an audience that doesn't know you well, have
another person introduce you with a few statements you've prepared.
All of this translates very smoothly into marketing terms as well.
A company that lists the quality of its own product is more likely to have that product dismissed by
the public in favour of another that has somebody else vouching for its quality.
Even better if that person is a recognised expert.
Think about what a marketing campaign that uses the phrase nine out of ten experts agree achieves.
Their product gains credibility because it is supported by third party others.
And these people are specialists.
Our trust can safely go towards this product.
Great.
A word of caution before we complete this lesson.
Remember when we talked about today's younger generation being more digitalised and trusting their peers
when choosing a product?
They look at reviews and comments online and often turn a rebellious, blind eye to expert opinions,
especially when they recognise that this is just the authority trick into play.
Marketing to this segment of society needs to be a bit more creativity and clever use of the principle.
That completes our lesson, and it's a wrap on the persuasion part of the course.
Well done.
And don't forget to head over to our interactive exercises once you complete the entire course.
